Adhyāya 81

Sanskrit

एकाशीतितमो ऽध्यायः- ८१ ।

ekāśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ- 81 |

Sanskrit

मार्कण्डेय उवाच

mārkaṇḍeya uvāca

81.1

Sanskrit

सावर्णिः सूर्यतनयो यो मनुः कथ्यते ऽष्टमः । निशामय तदुत्पत्तिं विस्तराद् गदतो मम ॥

sāvarṇiḥ sūryatanayo yo manuḥ kathyate 'ṣṭamaḥ | niśāmaya tadutpattiṃ vistarād gadato mama ||

English

81.1–2Mārkaṇḍeya said: Listen, as I tell you in detail the origin of Sāvarṇi1, the son of Sūrya, who is called the eighth Manu: how that greatly fortunate Sāvarṇi, the son of Ravi2, became the lord of a manvantara3 through the power of Mahāmāyā4.5

81.2

Sanskrit

महामायानुभावेन यथा मन्वन्तराधिपः । स बभूव महाभागः सावर्णिस्तनयो रवेः ॥

mahāmāyānubhāvena yathā manvantarādhipaḥ | sa babhūva mahābhāgaḥ sāvarṇistanayo raveḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 81.1–2, above.

81.3

Sanskrit

स्वारोचिषे ऽन्तरे पूर्वं चैत्रवंशसमुद्भवः । सुरथो नाम राजाभूत् समस्ते क्षितिमण्डले ॥

svārociṣe 'ntare pūrvaṃ caitravaṃśasamudbhavaḥ | suratho nāma rājābhūt samaste kṣitimaṇḍale ||

English

Formerly, in the Svārociṣa interval6, there was a king named Suratha, born in the line of Caitra7, [who ruled] over the whole circle of the earth.8

81.4

Sanskrit

तस्य पालयतः सम्यक् प्रजाः पुत्रानिवौरसान् । बभूवुः शत्रवो भूपाः कोलाविध्वंसिनस्तथा ॥

tasya pālayataḥ samyak prajāḥ putrānivaurasān | babhūvuḥ śatravo bhūpāḥ kolāvidhvaṃsinastathā ||

English

While he was protecting his subjects well, as though they were his own sons, there arose enemies, kings who were destroyers of the Kolas9, too.10

81.5

Sanskrit

तस्य तैरभवद् युद्धमतिप्रबलदण्डिनः । न्यूनैरपि स तैर्युद्धे कोलाविध्वंसिभिर्जितः ॥

tasya tairabhavad yuddhamatiprabaladaṇḍinaḥ | nyūnairapi sa tairyuddhe kolāvidhvaṃsibhirjitaḥ ||

English

He fought a war with them, though he was a wielder of the rod of exceedingly great power11; and in that battle he was defeated by those destroyers of the Kolas, even though they were fewer.12

81.6

Sanskrit

ततः स्वपुरमायातो निजदेशाधिपो ऽभवत् । आक्रान्तः स महाभागस्तैस्तदा प्रबलारिभिः ॥

tataḥ svapuramāyāto nijadeśādhipo 'bhavat | ākrāntaḥ sa mahābhāgastaistadā prabalāribhiḥ ||

English

Then, having come to his own city, he became lord of his own territory13; and that greatly fortunate one was at that time overpowered by those powerful enemies.14

81.7

Sanskrit

अमात्यैर्बलिभिर्दुष्टैर्दुर्बलस्य दुरात्मभिः । कोषो बलञ्चापहृतं तत्रापि स्वपुरे ततः ॥

amātyairbalibhirduṣṭairdurbalasya durātmabhiḥ | koṣo balañcāpahṛtaṃ tatrāpi svapure tataḥ ||

English

Then, even there in his own city, his treasury and his army were seized from him, weakened as he was, by his ministers, who were powerful, corrupt and evil-minded.15

81.8

Sanskrit

ततो मृगयाव्याजेन हृतस्वाम्यः स भुपतिः । एकाकी हयमारुह्य जगाम गहनं वनम् ॥

tato mṛgayāvyājena hṛtasvāmyaḥ sa bhupatiḥ | ekākī hayamāruhya jagāma gahanaṃ vanam ||

English

Then that king, his sovereignty taken from him16, went alone, mounted on a horse, into a dense forest, under the pretext of hunting.17

81.9

Sanskrit

स तत्राश्रममद्राक्षीद् द्विजवर्यस्य मेधसः । प्रशान्तश्वापदाकीर्णं मुनिश्ष्योपशोभितम् ॥

sa tatrāśramamadrākṣīd dvijavaryasya medhasaḥ | praśāntaśvāpadākīrṇaṃ muniśṣyopaśobhitam ||

English

There he saw the hermitage of Medhas18, the best of the twice-born19, thronged with wild beasts that had become peaceful and adorned by the sage's pupils20.21

81.10

Sanskrit

तस्थौ कञ्चित् स कालञ्च मुनिना तेन सत्कृतः । इतश्चैतश्च विचरंस्तस्मिन् मुनिवराश्रमे ॥

tasthau kañcit sa kālañca muninā tena satkṛtaḥ | itaścaitaśca vicaraṃstasmin munivarāśrame ||

English

He stayed there for some time, honoured by that sage, wandering here and there in that hermitage of the excellent sage.

81.11

Sanskrit

सो ऽचिन्तयत् तदा तत्र ममत्वाकृष्टचेतनः । मत्पूर्वैः पालितं पूर्वं मया हीनं पुरं हि तत् । मद्भृत्यैस्तैरसद्वृत्तैर्धर्मतः पाल्यते न वा ॥

so 'cintayat tadā tatra mamatvākṛṣṭacetanaḥ | matpūrvaiḥ pālitaṃ pūrvaṃ mayā hīnaṃ puraṃ hi tat | madbhṛtyaistairasadvṛttairdharmataḥ pālyate na vā ||

English

At that time, there in the hermitage, his mind drawn by the sense of 'mine'22, he thought: "That city was protected in former times by those who came before me23, and now it is bereft of me. Is it being protected according to dharma by those servants of mine, whose conduct is evil, or not?"24

81.12

Sanskrit

न जाने स प्रधानो मे शूरहस्ती सदामदः । मम वैरिवशं यातः कान् भोगानुपलप्स्यते ॥

na jāne sa pradhāno me śūrahastī sadāmadaḥ | mama vairivaśaṃ yātaḥ kān bhogānupalapsyate ||

English

"I do not know what enjoyments25 that chief of mine, the brave elephant26 ever in rut27, will obtain, having fallen into the power of my enemies."28

81.13

Sanskrit

ये ममानुगता नित्यं प्रसादधनभोजनैः । अनुवृत्तिं ध्रुवं ते ऽद्य कुर्वन्त्यन्यमहीभृताम् ॥

ye mamānugatā nityaṃ prasādadhanabhojanaiḥ | anuvṛttiṃ dhruvaṃ te 'dya kurvantyanyamahībhṛtām ||

English

Those who always followed me, [supported] with favours, wealth and food29, surely30 now serve other kings31.32

81.14

Sanskrit

असम्यग्व्ययशीलैस्तैः कुर्वद्भिः सततं व्ययम् । संचितः सो ऽतिदुः खेन क्षयं कोशो गमिष्यति ॥

asamyagvyayaśīlaistaiḥ kurvadbhiḥ satataṃ vyayam | saṃcitaḥ so 'tiduḥ khena kṣayaṃ kośo gamiṣyati ||

English

"That treasury33, gathered with great hardship, will surely go to ruin, as those men, whose habit is to spend improperly, keep spending continually."34

81.15

Sanskrit

एतच्चान्यच्च सततं चिन्तयामास पार्थिवः । तत्र विप्राश्रमाभ्यासे वैश्यमेकं ददर्श सः ॥

etaccānyacca satataṃ cintayāmāsa pārthivaḥ | tatra viprāśramābhyāse vaiśyamekaṃ dadarśa saḥ ||

English

The king kept thinking continually of this and of other things. There, near the hermitage of the brahmin, he saw a certain Vaiśya35.36

81.16

Sanskrit

स पृष्टस्तेन कस्त्वं भोः हेतुश्चागमने ऽत्र कः । सशोक इव कस्मात्त्वं दुर्मना इव लक्ष्यसे ॥

sa pṛṣṭastena kastvaṃ bhoḥ hetuścāgamane 'tra kaḥ | saśoka iva kasmāttvaṃ durmanā iva lakṣyase ||

English

He was asked by him: "Who are you, sir? What is the reason for your coming here? Why do you look as if you were sorrowful, as if you were dejected?"37

81.17

Sanskrit

इत्याकर्ण्य वचस्तस्य भूपतेः प्रणयोदितम् । प्रत्युवाच स तं वैश्यः प्रश्रयावनतो नृपम् ॥

ityākarṇya vacastasya bhūpateḥ praṇayoditam | pratyuvāca sa taṃ vaiśyaḥ praśrayāvanato nṛpam ||

English

Having heard these words of the king, spoken out of affection, the Vaiśya, bowed low in modesty, replied to the king.

Sanskrit

वैश्य उवाच

vaiśya uvāca

English

81.18The Vaiśya said: "I am a Vaiśya named Samādhi38, born in a family of wealthy men, and I was cast out by my sons and wife39, wicked people, out of greed for wealth."40

81.18

Sanskrit

समाधिर्नाम वैश्यो ऽहमुत्पन्नो धनिनां कुले । पुत्रदारैर्निरस्तश्च धनलोभादसाधुभिः ॥

samādhirnāma vaiśyo 'hamutpanno dhanināṃ kule | putradārairnirastaśca dhanalobhādasādhubhiḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 81.18, above.

81.19

Sanskrit

विहीनश्च धनैर्दारैः पुत्रैरादाय मे धनम् । वनमभ्यागतो दुः खी निरस्तश्चाप्तबन्धुभैः ॥

vihīnaśca dhanairdāraiḥ putrairādāya me dhanam | vanamabhyāgato duḥ khī nirastaścāptabandhubhaiḥ ||

English

Bereft of wealth, wife and sons, since they took my wealth41, I have come to the forest in sorrow, and I have also been cast out by trusted kinsmen.42

81.20

Sanskrit

सो ऽहं न वेद्मि पुत्राणां कुशलाकुशलात्मिकाम् । प्रवृत्तिं स्वजनानाञ्च दाराणाञ्चात्र संस्थितः ॥

so 'haṃ na vedmi putrāṇāṃ kuśalākuśalātmikām | pravṛttiṃ svajanānāñca dārāṇāñcātra saṃsthitaḥ ||

English

Such as I am, staying here, I do not know how things stand with my sons, my own people and my wife, whether it goes well or ill with them.43

81.21

Sanskrit

किं नु तेषां गृहे क्षेममक्षेमं किं नु साम्प्रतम् । कथं ते किं नु सद्वृत्ताः दुर्वृत्ताः किं नु मे सुताः ॥

kiṃ nu teṣāṃ gṛhe kṣemamakṣemaṃ kiṃ nu sāmpratam | kathaṃ te kiṃ nu sadvṛttāḥ durvṛttāḥ kiṃ nu me sutāḥ ||

English

Is it well in their house now, or is it ill? How are they? Are my sons of good conduct, or of bad conduct?44

Sanskrit

राजोवाच

rājovāca

81.22

Sanskrit

यैर्निरस्तो भवांल्लुब्धैः पुत्रदारादिभिर्धनैः । तेषु किं भवतः स्नेहमनुबध्नाति मानसम् ॥

yairnirasto bhavāṃllubdhaiḥ putradārādibhirdhanaiḥ | teṣu kiṃ bhavataḥ snehamanubadhnāti mānasam ||

English

Why does your mind still fasten affection on those greedy ones, your sons, wife and the rest, by whom you were cast out because of wealth?45

Sanskrit

वैश्च उवाच

vaiśca uvāca

English

81.23The Vaiśya said: It is just as you have spoken, in these words that concern me46. What can I do? My mind does not take on hardness47.48

81.23

Sanskrit

एवमेतद्यथा प्राह भवानस्मद्गतं वचः । किं करोमि न बध्नाति मम निष्ठुरतां मनः ॥

evametadyathā prāha bhavānasmadgataṃ vacaḥ | kiṃ karomi na badhnāti mama niṣṭhuratāṃ manaḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 81.23, above.

81.24

Sanskrit

यैः सन्त्यज्य पितृस्नेहं धनलुब्धैर्निराकृतः । पतिस्वजनहार्दं च हार्दि तेष्वेव मे मनः ॥

yaiḥ santyajya pitṛsnehaṃ dhanalubdhairnirākṛtaḥ | patisvajanahārdaṃ ca hārdi teṣveva me manaḥ ||

English

My mind is still tender49 toward those very people, greedy for wealth, by whom I was cast out after they abandoned a father's affection and the affection of a husband and of kinsmen50.51

81.25

Sanskrit

किमेतन्नाभिजानामि जानन्नपि महामते । यत्प्रेमप्रवणं चित्तं विगुणेष्वपि बन्धुषु ॥

kimetannābhijānāmi jānannapi mahāmate | yatpremapravaṇaṃ cittaṃ viguṇeṣvapi bandhuṣu ||

English

I do not understand what this is, though I know [the facts], O man of great mind52: that my heart should lean toward affection for [my] kinsmen, even though they are without good qualities53.54

81.26

Sanskrit

तेषां कृते मे निः श्वासो दौर्मनस्यं च जायते । करोमि किं यन्न मनस्तेष्वप्रीतिषु निष्ठुरम् ॥

teṣāṃ kṛte me niḥ śvāso daurmanasyaṃ ca jāyate | karomi kiṃ yanna manasteṣvaprītiṣu niṣṭhuram ||

English

For their sake sighs and dejection arise in me. What can I do, since my mind does not turn hard toward them, though they have no affection [for me]55?56

Sanskrit

मार्कण्डेय उवाच

mārkaṇḍeya uvāca

English

Mārkaṇḍeya said:575859

81.27

Sanskrit

ततस्तौ सहितौ विप्र तं मुनिं समुपस्थितौ । समाधिर्नाम वैश्यो ऽसौ स च पार्थिवसत्तमः ॥

tatastau sahitau vipra taṃ muniṃ samupasthitau | samādhirnāma vaiśyo 'sau sa ca pārthivasattamaḥ ||

English

Then the two of them, together, approached that sage, brahmin: this Vaiśya named Samādhi, and that best of kings.

81.28

Sanskrit

कृत्वा तु तौ यथान्यायं यथार्हं तेन संविदम् । उपविष्टौ कथाः काश्चिच्चक्रतुर्वैश्यपार्थिवौ ॥

kṛtvā tu tau yathānyāyaṃ yathārhaṃ tena saṃvidam | upaviṣṭau kathāḥ kāściccakraturvaiśyapārthivau ||

English

Having duly exchanged conversation60 with him, in the proper manner and as was fitting, the two, the Vaiśya and the king, sat down and held some conversations.61

Sanskrit

राजौवाच

rājauvāca

81.29

Sanskrit

भगवंस्त्वामहं प्रष्टुमिच्चाम्येकं वदस्व तत् । दुः खाय यन्मे मनसः स्वचित्तायत्ततां विना ॥

bhagavaṃstvāmahaṃ praṣṭumiccāmyekaṃ vadasva tat | duḥ khāya yanme manasaḥ svacittāyattatāṃ vinā ||

English

Revered sir, I wish to ask you one thing; tell me that which brings sorrow to my mind, in the absence of [its] being under the control of my own thought62.63

81.30

Sanskrit

ममत्वं गतराज्यस्य राज्याङ्गेष्वखिलेष्वपि । जानतो ऽपि यथाज्ञस्य किमेतन्मुनिसत्तम ॥

mamatvaṃ gatarājyasya rājyāṅgeṣvakhileṣvapi | jānato 'pi yathājñasya kimetanmunisattama ||

English

I have lost my kingdom, and yet I feel the sense of 'mine'64 even toward all the limbs of the kingdom65, though I know [their faults], like one who does not know. What is this, best of sages66?67†

81.31

Sanskrit

अयं च निकृतः पुत्रैर्दारैर्भृत्यैस्तथोज्झितः । स्वजनेन च सन्त्यक्तस्तेषु हार्दे तथाप्यति ॥

ayaṃ ca nikṛtaḥ putrairdārairbhṛtyaistathojjhitaḥ | svajanena ca santyaktasteṣu hārde tathāpyati ||

English

And this man68, wronged by his sons, his wives and his servants, and likewise cast out, and abandoned by his own kinsfolk, is nevertheless excessively tender toward them.69

81.32

Sanskrit

एवमेष तथाहं च द्वावप्यत्यन्तदुः खितौ । दृष्टदोषे ऽपि विषये ममत्वाकृष्टमानसौ ॥

evameṣa tathāhaṃ ca dvāvapyatyantaduḥ khitau | dṛṣṭadoṣe 'pi viṣaye mamatvākṛṣṭamānasau ||

English

Thus this man and I, both of us, are exceedingly distressed, our minds drawn by the sense of 'mine'70 toward an object whose fault we have seen.71

81.33

Sanskrit

तत्किमेतन्महाभाग यन्मोहो ज्ञानिनोरपि । ममास्य च भवत्येषा विवेकान्धस्य मूढता ॥

tatkimetanmahābhāga yanmoho jñāninorapi | mamāsya ca bhavatyeṣā vivekāndhasya mūḍhatā ||

English

Then what is this, greatly fortunate one72, that delusion comes upon both of us, though we are men of knowledge? This folly befalls me and this man alike, blinded as we are in discernment73.74

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi75 said:76

81.34

Sanskrit

ज्ञानमस्ति समस्तस्य जन्तोर्विषयगोचरे । विषयश्च महाभाग याति चैवं पृथक् पृथक् ॥

jñānamasti samastasya jantorviṣayagocare | viṣayaśca mahābhāga yāti caivaṃ pṛthak pṛthak ||

English

Every living creature has knowledge77 within the range of its objects, O greatly fortunate one; and the objects themselves differ in this way, each going its own separate way78.79

81.35

Sanskrit

दिवान्धाः प्राणिनः केचिद्रात्रावन्धास्तथापरे । केचिद् दिवा तथा रात्रौ प्राणिनस्तुल्यदृष्टयः ॥

divāndhāḥ prāṇinaḥ kecidrātrāvandhāstathāpare | kecid divā tathā rātrau prāṇinastulyadṛṣṭayaḥ ||

English

Some living beings are blind by day, and others are blind by night; some living beings are equal in sight, by day as likewise by night.80

81.36

Sanskrit

ज्ञानिनो मनुजाः सत्यं किन्तु ते न हि केवलम् । यतो हि ज्ञानिनः सर्वे पशुपक्षिमृगादयः ॥

jñānino manujāḥ satyaṃ kintu te na hi kevalam | yato hi jñāninaḥ sarve paśupakṣimṛgādayaḥ ||

English

Human beings are indeed endowed with knowledge, but they are not the only ones; for all beings, beginning with domestic animals, birds and wild beasts81, are endowed with knowledge.82

81.37

Sanskrit

ज्ञानं च तन्मनुष्याणां यत्तेषां मृगृपक्षिणाम् । मनुष्याणां च यत्तेषां तुल्यमन्यत्तथोभयोः ॥

jñānaṃ ca tanmanuṣyāṇāṃ yatteṣāṃ mṛgṛpakṣiṇām | manuṣyāṇāṃ ca yatteṣāṃ tulyamanyattathobhayoḥ ||

English

The knowledge that those human beings have is the same as that which those beasts and birds have; and the knowledge that those beasts and birds have is the same as that of human beings. In the same way, whatever else there is, it is alike in both83.84

81.38

Sanskrit

ज्ञाने ऽपि सति पश्यैतान् पतङ्गाञ्छावचञ्चुषु । कणमोक्षादृतान् मोहात्पीड्यमानानपि क्षुधा ॥

jñāne 'pi sati paśyaitān pataṅgāñchāvacañcuṣu | kaṇamokṣādṛtān mohātpīḍyamānānapi kṣudhā ||

English

81.38–39Even though there is knowledge, look at these birds85, who, out of delusion, are intent on dropping grains into the beaks of their fledglings, though they are themselves tormented by hunger. Human beings, tiger among men86, are full of longing toward their sons, out of greed for a return benefit. Do you not see these [men] too?87

81.39

Sanskrit

मानुषा मनुजव्याघ्र साभिलाषाः सुतान् प्रति । लोभात्प्रत्युपकाराय नन्वेतान् किं न पश्यसि ॥

mānuṣā manujavyāghra sābhilāṣāḥ sutān prati | lobhātpratyupakārāya nanvetān kiṃ na paśyasi ||

English

Its English is given with 81.38–39, above.

81.40

Sanskrit

तथापि ममतावर्ते मोहगर्ते निपातिताः । महामायाप्रभावेण संसारस्थितिकारिणा ॥

tathāpi mamatāvarte mohagarte nipātitāḥ | mahāmāyāprabhāveṇa saṃsārasthitikāriṇā ||

English

Even so, [these beings] have been cast into the whirlpool of mamatā88 and the pit of delusion by the power of Mahāmāyā89, which brings about the continuance of saṃsāra.90

81.41

Sanskrit

तन्नात्र विस्मयः कार्यो योगनिद्रा जगत्पतेः । महामाया हरेश्चैतत्तथा संमोह्यते जगत् ॥

tannātra vismayaḥ kāryo yoganidrā jagatpateḥ | mahāmāyā hareścaitattathā saṃmohyate jagat ||

English

Therefore no wonder should be felt in this matter. This is the yoganidrā91 of the lord of the world, and the Mahāmāyā of Hari92; thus the world is deluded.93

81.42

Sanskrit

ज्ञानिनामपि चेतांसि देवी भगवती हि सा । बलादाकृष्य मोहाय महामाया प्रयच्छति ॥

jñānināmapi cetāṃsi devī bhagavatī hi sā | balādākṛṣya mohāya mahāmāyā prayacchati ||

English

For she, the Goddess, the Blessed One94, Mahāmāyā, having forcibly drawn in the minds even of those who have knowledge, delivers them to delusion.95

81.43

Sanskrit

तया विसृज्यते विश्वं जगदेतच्चराचरम् । सैषा प्रसन्ना वरदा नृणां भवति मुक्तये ॥

tayā visṛjyate viśvaṃ jagadetaccarācaram | saiṣā prasannā varadā nṛṇāṃ bhavati muktaye ||

English

By her this entire world, this moving and unmoving universe, is brought forth96. She, when gracious and a giver of boons, becomes the means of release97 for human beings.98

81.44

Sanskrit

सा विद्या परमा मुक्तेर्हेतुभूता सनातनी । संसारबन्धहेतुश्च सैव सर्वेश्वरेश्वरी ॥

sā vidyā paramā mukterhetubhūtā sanātanī | saṃsārabandhahetuśca saiva sarveśvareśvarī ||

English

She is the supreme knowledge99, the eternal one, who is the cause of release; and she herself, the sovereign lady of all the lords100, is also the cause of the bondage of saṃsāra.101

Sanskrit

रोजोवाच

rojovāca

81.45

Sanskrit

भगवन् ! का हि सा देवी महामायेति यां भवान् । ब्रवीति कथमुत्पन्ना सा कर्मास्याश्च किं द्विज ॥

bhagavan ! kā hi sā devī mahāmāyeti yāṃ bhavān | bravīti kathamutpannā sā karmāsyāśca kiṃ dvija ||

English

Venerable sir102, who is that Goddess whom you call Mahāmāyā? How was she born, and what, brahmin103, is her work?104

81.46

Sanskrit

यत्स्वभावा च सा देवी यत्स्वरूपा यदुद्भवा । तत् सर्वं श्रोतुमिच्छामि त्वत्तो ब्रह्मविदां वर ॥

yatsvabhāvā ca sā devī yatsvarūpā yadudbhavā | tat sarvaṃ śrotumicchāmi tvatto brahmavidāṃ vara ||

English

And I wish to hear from you all of this: what that Goddess's own nature is, what her form is, and what her origin is, you who are the best of those who know brahman105106.107

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

81.47

Sanskrit

नित्यैव सा जगन्मूर्तिस्तया सर्वमिदं ततम् । तथापि तत्समुत्पत्तिर्बहुधा श्रुयतां मम ॥

nityaiva sā jaganmūrtistayā sarvamidaṃ tatam | tathāpi tatsamutpattirbahudhā śruyatāṃ mama ||

English

She is eternal, her form is the world108, and by her all this is pervaded. Even so, let her origin be heard109 from me in its many forms.110

81.48

Sanskrit

देवानां कार्यसिद्ध्यर्थमाविर्भवति सा यदा । उत्पन्नेति तदा लोके सा नित्याप्यभिधीयते ॥

devānāṃ kāryasiddhyarthamāvirbhavati sā yadā | utpanneti tadā loke sā nityāpyabhidhīyate ||

English

When she manifests herself for the accomplishment of the gods' purpose, then in the world she is said to be "born," even though she is eternal.

81.49

Sanskrit

योगनिद्रां यदा विष्णुर्जगत्येकार्णवीकृते । आस्तीर्य शेषमभजत् कल्पान्ते भगवान् प्रभुः ॥

yoganidrāṃ yadā viṣṇurjagatyekārṇavīkṛte | āstīrya śeṣamabhajat kalpānte bhagavān prabhuḥ ||

English

81.49–50When, at the end of the kalpa111, the world had been turned into a single ocean, the venerable lord Viṣṇu spread out Śeṣa112 and rested on him, resorting to yoganidrā; then two fearsome asuras, the renowned Madhu and Kaiṭabha113, who had arisen from the wax of Viṣṇu's ear, rose up to kill Brahmā.114

81.50

Sanskrit

तदा द्वावसुरौ घोरौ विख्यातौ मधुकैटभौ । विष्णुकर्णमलोद्भूतौ हन्तुं ब्रह्माणमुद्यतौ ॥

tadā dvāvasurau ghorau vikhyātau madhukaiṭabhau | viṣṇukarṇamalodbhūtau hantuṃ brahmāṇamudyatau ||

English

Its English is given with 81.49–50, above.

81.51

Sanskrit

स नाभिकमले विष्णोः स्थितो ब्रह्मा प्रजापतिः । दृष्ट्वा तावसुरौ चोग्रौ प्रसुप्तं च जनार्दनम् ॥

sa nābhikamale viṣṇoḥ sthito brahmā prajāpatiḥ | dṛṣṭvā tāvasurau cograu prasuptaṃ ca janārdanam ||

English

81.51–52Brahmā, the Prajāpati115, who was seated in the lotus of Viṣṇu's navel, saw those two fierce asuras and saw Janārdana116 fast asleep. With his mind fixed on a single point, he praised that yoganidrā, who had made her dwelling in the eyes of Hari, in order to awaken Hari.117118

81.52

Sanskrit

तुष्टाव योगनिद्रां तामेकाग्रहृदयस्थितः । विबोधनार्थाय हरेर्हरिनेत्रकृतालयाम् ॥

tuṣṭāva yoganidrāṃ tāmekāgrahṛdayasthitaḥ | vibodhanārthāya harerharinetrakṛtālayām ||

English

Its English is given with 81.51–52, above.

Sanskrit

ब्रह्मोवाच

brahmovāca

English

Brahmā said:

81.53

Sanskrit

विश्वेश्वरीं जगद्धात्रीं स्थितिसंहारकारिणीम् । निद्रां भगवतीं विष्णोरतुलां तेजसः प्रभुः ॥

viśveśvarīṃ jagaddhātrīṃ sthitisaṃhārakāriṇīm | nidrāṃ bhagavatīṃ viṣṇoratulāṃ tejasaḥ prabhuḥ ||

English

The lord of fiery energy [praised]119 her, the sovereign of the universe, the supporter of the world, who brings about its maintenance and dissolution: Viṣṇu's Sleep120, the Blessed One, the incomparable.121

81.54

Sanskrit

त्वं स्वाहा त्वं स्वधा त्वं हि वषट्कारः स्वरात्मिका । सुधा त्वमक्षरे नित्ये त्रिधा मात्रात्मिका स्थिता ॥

tvaṃ svāhā tvaṃ svadhā tvaṃ hi vaṣaṭkāraḥ svarātmikā | sudhā tvamakṣare nitye tridhā mātrātmikā sthitā ||

English

You are svāhā122, you are svadhā, you are the vaṣaṭkāra123, you whose nature is sound. You are nectar. In the imperishable, eternal [syllable] you abide threefold, consisting of the mātrās124.125

81.55

Sanskrit

अर्धमात्रा स्थिता नित्या यानुच्चार्या विशेषतः । त्वमेव सन्ध्या सावित्री त्वं देवि जननी परा ॥

ardhamātrā sthitā nityā yānuccāryā viśeṣataḥ | tvameva sandhyā sāvitrī tvaṃ devi jananī parā ||

English

You abide as the eternal half-mātrā126, you who are especially unutterable. You alone are the evening worship (sandhyā) and Sāvitrī127; you, O Goddess, are the supreme mother.128

81.56

Sanskrit

त्वयैव धार्यते सर्वं त्वयैतत्सृज्यते जगत् । त्वयैतत्पाल्यते देवि त्वमत्स्यन्ते च सर्वदा ॥

tvayaiva dhāryate sarvaṃ tvayaitatsṛjyate jagat | tvayaitatpālyate devi tvamatsyante ca sarvadā ||

English

By you alone everything is upheld; by you this world is created; by you, O Goddess, this [world] is protected; and you always devour [it] at the end129.130

81.57

Sanskrit

विसृष्टौ सृष्टिरूपा त्वं स्थितिरूपा च पालने । तथा संहृतिरूपान्ते जगतो ऽस्य जगन्मये ॥

visṛṣṭau sṛṣṭirūpā tvaṃ sthitirūpā ca pālane | tathā saṃhṛtirūpānte jagato 'sya jaganmaye ||

English

In the creation you are the form of creation; in the protection you are the form of maintenance; and likewise at the end of this world you are the form of dissolution, O you who are the whole world131.132

81.58

Sanskrit

महाविद्या महामाया महामेधा महास्मृतिः । महामोहा च भवती महादेवी महेश्वरी ॥

mahāvidyā mahāmāyā mahāmedhā mahāsmṛtiḥ | mahāmohā ca bhavatī mahādevī maheśvarī ||

English

You are Mahāvidyā133, Mahāmāyā, Mahāmedhā, Mahāsmṛti and Mahāmohā, Mahādevī and Maheśvarī.134

81.59

Sanskrit

प्रकृतिस्त्वञ्च सर्वस्य गुणत्रयविभाविनी । कालरात्रिर्महारात्रिर्मोहरात्रिश्च दारुणा ॥

prakṛtistvañca sarvasya guṇatrayavibhāvinī | kālarātrirmahārātrirmoharātriśca dāruṇā ||

English

You are also the prakṛti135 of everything, the one who manifests the three guṇas136; and you are Kālarātri, Mahārātri and the dreadful Moharātri137.138

81.60

Sanskrit

त्वं श्रीस्त्वमीश्वरी त्वं ह्रीस्त्वं बुद्धिर्बोधलक्षणा । लज्जा पुष्टिस्तथा तुष्टिस्त्वं शान्तिः क्षान्तिरेव च ॥

tvaṃ śrīstvamīśvarī tvaṃ hrīstvaṃ buddhirbodhalakṣaṇā | lajjā puṣṭistathā tuṣṭistvaṃ śāntiḥ kṣāntireva ca ||

English

You are Śrī139, you are Īśvarī140, you are Hrī141, and you are Buddhi, whose mark is awareness142. You are Lajjā, Puṣṭi143, and likewise Tuṣṭi; you are Śānti, and Kṣānti as well.

81.61

Sanskrit

खडिगनी शूलिनी घोरा गदिनी चक्रिणी तथा । शङ्खिनी चापिनी बाणभुशुण्डी परिघायुधा ॥

khaḍiganī śūlinī ghorā gadinī cakriṇī tathā | śaṅkhinī cāpinī bāṇabhuśuṇḍī parighāyudhā ||

English

You are the bearer of the sword144, the bearer of the spear, the terrible one; likewise the bearer of the mace and of the discus; the bearer of the conch and of the bow; you have arrows and the bhuśuṇḍi145 as your weapons, and the iron club (parigha) as your weapon.146

81.62

Sanskrit

सौम्या सौम्यतराशेषसौम्येभ्यस्त्वतिसुन्दरी । परापराणां परमा त्वमेव परमेश्वरी ॥

saumyā saumyatarāśeṣasaumyebhyastvatisundarī | parāparāṇāṃ paramā tvameva parameśvarī ||

English

You are gentle, gentler than all gentle things147, and indeed exceedingly beautiful. You are the supreme one among the higher and the lower148, and you alone are the supreme sovereign lady149.150

81.63

Sanskrit

यच्च किञ्चित् क्वचिद्वस्तु सदसद्वाखिलात्मिके । तस्य सर्वस्य या शिक्तिः सा त्वं किं स्तूयते तदा ॥

yacca kiñcit kvacidvastu sadasadvākhilātmike | tasya sarvasya yā śiktiḥ sā tvaṃ kiṃ stūyate tadā ||

English

O you who are the soul of all, whatever thing there is anywhere, whether real or unreal151, you are the power152 of all of that; then how can you be praised153?154

81.64

Sanskrit

यया त्वया जगत्स्त्रष्टा जगत्पात्यत्ति यो जगत् । सो ऽपि निद्रावशं नीतः कस्त्वां स्तोतुमिहेश्वरः ॥

yayā tvayā jagatstraṣṭā jagatpātyatti yo jagat | so 'pi nidrāvaśaṃ nītaḥ kastvāṃ stotumiheśvaraḥ ||

English

You, by whom even he who is the creator of the world155, who protects the world and devours the world, was brought under the power of Sleep: who here is able to praise you?156

81.65

Sanskrit

विष्णुः शरीरग्रहणमहामीशान एव च । कारितास्ते यतो ऽतस्त्वां कः स्तोतुं शक्तिमान् भवेत् ॥

viṣṇuḥ śarīragrahaṇamahāmīśāna eva ca | kāritāste yato 'tastvāṃ kaḥ stotuṃ śaktimān bhavet ||

English

Since Viṣṇu, I157, and Īśāna158 as well have been made by you to take bodies159, therefore who could be capable of praising you?160

81.66

Sanskrit

सा त्वमित्थं प्रभावैः स्वैरुदारैर्देवि संस्तुता । मोहयैतौ दुराधर्षावसुरो मधुकैटभौ ॥

sā tvamitthaṃ prabhāvaiḥ svairudārairdevi saṃstutā | mohayaitau durādharṣāvasuro madhukaiṭabhau ||

English

Such as you are, O Devī161, you have thus been praised by me through your own noble powers. Bewilder these two asuras162, Madhu and Kaiṭabha, who are hard to overcome.163

81.67

Sanskrit

प्रबोधञ्च जगत्स्वामी नीयतामच्युतो लघु । बोधश्च क्रियतामस्य हन्तुमेतौ महासुरौ ॥

prabodhañca jagatsvāmī nīyatāmacyuto laghu | bodhaśca kriyatāmasya hantumetau mahāsurau ||

English

And let Acyuta164, the lord of the world, be quickly brought to awakening, and let his awakening165 be effected, in order to slay these two great asuras.166

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

81.68

Sanskrit

एवं स्तुता तदा देवी तामसी तत्र वेधसा । विष्णोः प्रबोधनार्थाय निहन्तुं मधुकैटभौ ॥

evaṃ stutā tadā devī tāmasī tatra vedhasā | viṣṇoḥ prabodhanārthāya nihantuṃ madhukaiṭabhau ||

English

81.68–69The ṛṣi said: Thus praised there by Vedhas167, the Devī in her form of Tamas168, for the awakening of Viṣṇu and for the slaying of Madhu and Kaiṭabha, came forth from his eyes, mouths, nostrils, arms, heart and chest169, and stood in the sight of Brahmā, whose birth is unmanifest170.171

81.69

Sanskrit

नेत्रास्यनासिकाबाहुहृदयेभ्यस्तथोरसः । निर्गम्य दर्शने तस्थौ ब्रह्मणो ऽव्यक्तजन्मनः ॥

netrāsyanāsikābāhuhṛdayebhyastathorasaḥ | nirgamya darśane tasthau brahmaṇo 'vyaktajanmanaḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 81.68–69, above.

81.70

Sanskrit

उत्तस्थौ च जगन्नाथस्तया मुक्तो जनार्दनः । एकार्णवे ऽहिशयनात्ततः स ददृशे च तौ ॥

uttasthau ca jagannāthastayā mukto janārdanaḥ | ekārṇave 'hiśayanāttataḥ sa dadṛśe ca tau ||

English

And Janārdana, the lord of the world172, freed by her, rose up from his serpent-couch173 on the single ocean174; and then he saw those two.175

81.71

Sanskrit

मधुकैटभौ दुरात्मानावतिवीर्यपराक्रमौ । क्रोधरक्तेक्षणावत्तुं ब्रह्माणं जनितोद्यमौ ॥

madhukaiṭabhau durātmānāvativīryaparākramau | krodharaktekṣaṇāvattuṃ brahmāṇaṃ janitodyamau ||

English

Madhu and Kaiṭabha, evil-minded and exceedingly strong in vigor and prowess176, their eyes red with anger, had made ready to devour Brahmā.177

81.72

Sanskrit

समुत्थाय ततस्ताभ्यां युयुधे भगवान् हरिः । पञ्चवर्षसहस्राणि बाहुप्रहरणो विभुः ॥

samutthāya tatastābhyāṃ yuyudhe bhagavān hariḥ | pañcavarṣasahasrāṇi bāhupraharaṇo vibhuḥ ||

English

Then the blessed178 Hari rose up and fought with those two for five thousand years, the all-pervading lord179, with his arms as his only weapons.180

81.73

Sanskrit

तावप्यतिबलोन्मत्तौ महामायाविमोहितौ । उक्तवन्तौ वरो ऽस्मत्तो व्रियतामिति केशवम् ॥

tāvapyatibalonmattau mahāmāyāvimohitau | uktavantau varo 'smatto vriyatāmiti keśavam ||

English

And those two, too, intoxicated with excessive strength and utterly bewildered by Mahāmāyā, said to Keśava181: "Let a boon182 be chosen from us."183

Sanskrit

भगवानुवाच

bhagavānuvāca

English

The Blessed One184 said:185

81.74

Sanskrit

भवनेतामद्य मे तुष्टौ मम वध्यावुभावपि । किमन्येन वरेणात्र एतावद्धि वृतं मम ॥

bhavanetāmadya me tuṣṭau mama vadhyāvubhāvapi | kimanyena vareṇātra etāvaddhi vṛtaṃ mama ||

English

"Today may you two be pleased186 with me, and may you both be slain by me187. What need is there of another boon here? For this much has been chosen by me."188†

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

81.75

Sanskrit

वञ्चिताभ्यामिति तदा सर्वमापोमयं जगत् । विलोक्य ताभ्यां गदितो भगवान् कमलेक्षणः ॥

vañcitābhyāmiti tadā sarvamāpomayaṃ jagat | vilokya tābhyāṃ gadito bhagavān kamalekṣaṇaḥ ||

English

Then, when the two, thus deceived189, had looked at the whole world, which consisted of water, the Blessed One, the lotus-eyed, was spoken to by them.190†

81.76

Sanskrit

आवां जहि न यत्रोर्वो सलिलेन परिप्लुता । प्रीतौ स्वस्तव युद्धेन श्लाघ्यस्त्वं मृत्युरावयोः ॥

āvāṃ jahi na yatrorvo salilena pariplutā | prītau svastava yuddhena ślāghyastvaṃ mṛtyurāvayoḥ ||

English

Kill us two where the earth is not submerged in water191. We two are pleased192 with your fighting; you are worthy of praise as our death.193

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

81.77

Sanskrit

तथेत्युक्त्वा भगवता शङ्खचक्रगदाभृता । कृत्वा चक्रेण वै च्छिन्ने जघने शिरसी तयोः ॥

tathetyuktvā bhagavatā śaṅkhacakragadābhṛtā | kṛtvā cakreṇa vai cchinne jaghane śirasī tayoḥ ||

English

Having said, "So be it," the Blessed One, the bearer of the conch, discus and mace194, laid [them] on his thigh195, and the two heads of the two were severed196 with the discus.197†

81.78

Sanskrit

एवमेषा समुत्पन्ना ब्रह्मणा संस्तुता स्वयम् । प्रभावमस्या देव्यास्तु भूयः शृणु वदामि ते ॥

evameṣā samutpannā brahmaṇā saṃstutā svayam | prabhāvamasyā devyāstu bhūyaḥ śṛṇu vadāmi te ||

English

In this way she came into being198, hymned by Brahmā himself. But hear further of the power199 of this Goddess; I will tell it to you.200

Sanskrit

इति श्रीमार्कण्डेयपुराणे सावर्णिके मन्वन्तरे देवीमाहात्म्ये मधुकैटभवधो नाम एकाशीतितमो ऽध्यायः

iti śrīmārkaṇḍeyapurāṇe sāvarṇike manvantare devīmāhātmye madhukaiṭabhavadho nāma ekāśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ

English

Thus ends the eighty-first chapter201, named "The Slaying of Madhu and Kaiṭabha," in the Devīmāhātmya202, in the Sāvarṇika203 manvantara, in the revered Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa.204

  1. Sāvarṇi: Metronymic/patronymic-type name of the eighth Manu, son of Sūrya and Chāyā, whose story follows; the form Sāvarṇa appears in the preceding verses for the same Manu. ↩
  2. Ravi: A name of Sūrya, the sun god, so the same father as in 81.1. ↩
  3. manvantara: The age of a Manu, one of the fourteen periods in a cycle of creation, each ruled by a Manu, with its own Indra and gods. ↩
  4. Mahāmāyā: 'Great Illusion', the Goddess as the supreme power of māyā, whose glory is the subject of the Devīmāhātmya section that begins here. ↩
  5. The reading: mahāmāyā in 81.2 could be read as a name of the Goddess (chosen, since the following chapters tell of her) or as a common compound 'great illusion'. Also, the vocative-less address: no addressee is named, so none is supplied. The instrumental anubhāvena is rendered 'through the power of'. ↩
  6. Svārociṣa interval: The second manvantara, presided over by the Manu Svārociṣa; antara is a synonym of manvantara. ↩
  7. the line of Caitra: Caitra is the ancestor of Suratha's dynasty; the compound caitravaṃśa can also be read as 'the family of Caitra'. ↩
  8. The reading: caitravaṃśa-samudbhavaḥ: Caitra could be a proper name (the founder of the line) or a patronymic-type adjective; I took it as a personal name. Also 'antare' is used for 'manvantare'; I rendered it 'interval'. Bracketed 'who ruled' supplies the verb for the locative 'over the whole circle of the earth', since 'abhūt' is already used for 'was'. ↩
  9. destroyers of the Kolas: The compound kolāvidhvaṃsin is uncertain. It is commonly taken as 'destroyers of the Kolas', a people or tribe, and the next verse names them as Suratha's foes. Other readings take kolā as 'hog' or as a place name. ↩
  10. The reading: kolāvidhvaṃsinaḥ: 'destroyers of the Kolas' (a people or place) is the usual reading; 'Kolāvidhvaṃsin' as a single people's name is also possible, and some read 'kolā-vidhvaṃsin' with kolā as a city. I chose the first as the plainest. Also, tathā ('too', 'likewise') is rendered 'too', meaning that these kings were also enemies of Suratha; the exact link is uncertain. ↩
  11. a wielder of the rod of exceedingly great power: Sanskrit ati-prabala-daṇḍin: daṇḍa, the rod, is the king's power to punish and command, so the phrase means one whose authority and force were very great. It describes Suratha. ↩
  12. The reading: The compound atiprabaladaṇḍinaḥ is genitive singular and agrees with tasya (Suratha). It could be read as 'whose rod (army, force) was exceedingly strong', or as 'a mighty wielder of the rod'. I took daṇḍin as 'wielder of the rod', that is, of royal authority and force. The verse then contrasts his great power with his defeat by fewer enemies. ↩
  13. he became lord of his own territory: nijadeśādhipo 'bhavat says only "became lord of his own country". Probably the sense is that he was left ruling just his own land, his wider dominion having been lost in the war. This is an interpretation; the Sanskrit does not say "only". ↩
  14. The reading: nijadeśādhipo 'bhavat: it may mean that he was reduced to ruling only his own territory, or simply that he became lord in his own country. I keep the literal "became lord of his own territory" (the draft's "remained" was wrong, as the reviewer says) and give the narrower sense in the note only as a likely reading. ākrāntaḥ: I accept the reviewer and now render "overpowered" instead of "attacked". This suits the defeat in 81.5 and the loss of his treasury and army in 81.7. I dropped the added "Even so" and the added "king"; the verse has only sa mahābhāgaḥ, "that greatly fortunate one". I read ākrāntaḥ as a separate clause linked by "and", though it could also be taken with abhavat as part of one statement. ↩
  15. The reading: The verse has one neuter singular predicate, apahṛtam, with two subjects, koṣaḥ and balam; I rendered it as a plural ('were seized'). Balibhiḥ ('powerful') is taken as an adjective of amātyaiḥ, as are duṣṭaiḥ and durātmabhiḥ; all three agree with the ministers, who are the agents. Durbalasya ('of him, weakened') is a genitive with the sense of 'from him', going with the seizure. 'Tataḥ' is rendered 'then'; it could also mean 'thereafter' or 'from that cause'. ↩
  16. his sovereignty taken from him: The compound hṛtasvāmya is a bahuvrīhi qualifying the king: one whose lordship or ownership (svāmya) has been seized, in keeping with the loss of treasury and army in 81.7. ↩
  17. The reading: The printed text reads 'bhupatiḥ' with a short u; I read it as bhūpatiḥ, 'lord of the earth', i.e. the king. The compound hṛtasvāmyaḥ could also be understood as 'whose lordship was taken away', as I have it, rather than 'deprived of his possessions'; svāmya means lordship or sovereignty, so I kept the former. ↩
  18. Medhas: The stem is medhas, printed here in the genitive medhasaḥ. He is the sage in whose hermitage Suratha takes refuge. ↩
  19. the best of the twice-born: dvijavarya, a title of honour for a distinguished brahmin; it qualifies Medhas and is not a second person. ↩
  20. the sage's pupils: The printed compound muniśṣya- is a misprint for muniśiṣya-, 'pupils of the sage'. ↩
  21. The reading: The text prints muniśṣyopaśobhitam. I emend the misprint to muniśiṣyopaśobhitam, 'adorned by the sage's pupils'. The agent is the pupils, and the sage (muni) is the owner of the hermitage, that is, Medhas himself. In medhasaḥ I take Medhas as a proper name in the genitive, qualified by dvijavaryasya, 'of the best of the twice-born'. The two genitives agree and refer to one man. An alternative would be to read medhasaḥ as a common noun, 'of one of good understanding', but a name is more natural here. In accordance with the reviewer, I dropped the claim about editions reading Sumedhas, which I cannot verify, and the reference to later events. I rendered ākīrṇa as 'thronged' instead of 'crowded'. For dvija I kept the glossary's term 'twice-born' in the epithet, since dvijavarya is a title in which the brahmin sense is understood. ↩
  22. the sense of 'mine': mamatva is possessive attachment, the feeling 'this is mine', a form of worldly delusion that the chapter links to māyā. ↩
  23. those who came before me: matpūrvaiḥ is a compound meaning 'those whose predecessor was I' in form, but it is read as 'my forefathers or predecessors'. It here means the earlier rulers of his line. ↩
  24. The reading: matpūrvaiḥ can be read as 'by my predecessors/ancestors' (chosen, as the context of former protection requires) or literally 'by those with me as predecessor', which makes no sense here. Final 'na vā' is read as the second half of an alternative question ('or not?'), not as a plain statement. ↩
  25. enjoyments: bhoga: enjoyments, comforts, what one receives in the way of food and care. The sense is general. ↩
  26. that chief of mine, the brave elephant: pradhānaḥ is an adjective agreeing with śūrahastī (hastin, 'elephant'); sa and me also belong with it. The king is speaking of his foremost elephant. ↩
  27. ever in rut: sadāmadaḥ: mada is the secretion and frenzy of a bull elephant in rut (musth); 'always in rut' marks a vigorous, prized animal. ↩
  28. The reading: I accept the reviewer's points on na jāne and sa. The negation governs the whole question, 'what enjoyments he will obtain', and the translation is now one sentence with sa rendered as 'that'. I have dropped the bracketed 'elephant' and the claim that the text names no elephant, since śūrahastī is the elephant and pradhānaḥ agrees with it. 'Elephant' replaces 'tusker'. The note on bhoga is now general. Two readings remain possible. Pradhāna could be a chief minister, but the agreement with śūrahastī sadāmadaḥ and the 'enjoyments' that follow favour the elephant. Śūrahastī could also be read as 'elephant of the brave' rather than 'brave elephant'; I take the latter, the plainer reading of a karmadhāraya compound. ↩
  29. [supported] with favours, wealth and food: The instrumentals prasādadhanabhojanaiḥ give what the king bestowed on his followers: his favours, wealth and food. They do not give the followers' motive. ↩
  30. surely: dhruvam is an adverb here, 'surely, certainly'. It is not the name Dhruva, the pole star, which is in the glossary. ↩
  31. serve other kings: anuvṛtti is compliant attendance on a master, here the following of a new lord. The compound anyamahībhṛtām is genitive plural, 'of other kings'. ↩
  32. The reading: prasādadhanabhojanaiḥ: the reviewer is right that the instrumental marks what the king gave, not the followers' purpose, so I dropped 'for the sake of'. The Sanskrit has no verb for the instrumental to depend on, so I supplied '[supported]' in brackets. dhruvam: adverb 'surely' or the name Dhruva. I keep the adverb, since no person is referred to and the sense is the king's confident inference. ↩
  33. treasury: kośa: the royal treasury, one of the essential constituents of a kingdom. ↩
  34. The reading: The verse continues the king's thought begun in 81.11, so the quotation marks are kept open to match the preceding verses. The participle kurvadbhiḥ agrees with taiḥ (those men, i.e. the servants), so the spending is theirs. The compound asamyagvyayaśīlaiḥ is read as 'whose habit is improper spending' (śīla = habit). The words ati-duḥkhena are taken as one adverbial phrase, 'with great hardship', although the e-text prints a stray space in duḥ khena. ↩
  35. Vaiśya: A member of the third varṇa, the class of merchants, traders and agriculturists. This man is Samādhi in the later verses of the chapter. ↩
  36. The reading: viprāśramābhyāse: abhyāsa can mean 'nearness, vicinity' (chosen) or 'repetition, practice'. The sense of proximity fits the context. vipra is taken as Medhas, the brahmin of verse 9, so 'the hermitage of the brahmin'; the compound could also be read plurally, 'the brahmins' hermitage'. The Vaiśya's name is not given in this verse. ↩
  37. The reading: The verse does not name the speaker or the one asked. Since 81.15 has the king seeing the Vaiśya and 81.17 has the Vaiśya replying to the king's words, I take 'tena' to be the king and 'sa' the Vaiśya. 'Bhoḥ' is a vocative of address, rendered 'sir'. ↩
  38. Samādhi: The Vaiśya's personal name. It is the same word as the term samādhi (meditative concentration), which the glossary has already explained. ↩
  39. my sons and wife: putradāraiḥ is a plural compound in the instrumental, literally 'sons and wives'. The plural of dāra is conventional, and 'wife' is the natural English sense. The instrumental marks the agents of the passive verb 'cast out'. ↩
  40. The reading: The reference '628.1' is the GRETIL numbering for the speaker's line and is kept as given. The compound dhanināṃ kule is read as 'in a family of wealthy men' and not 'in the family of a wealthy man'; the plural genitive favours the former. ↩
  41. since they took my wealth: The absolutive ādāya, 'having taken', has no subject of its own in the verse. Its agents are the wife and sons of the previous verse, who cast him out out of greed for his wealth. ↩
  42. The reading: 1. The text reads bandhubhaiḥ, which is a misprint. I read bandhubhiḥ, the instrumental plural of bandhu, which the sense and metre require. 2. The grouping of putraiḥ is ambiguous. It may join dhanaiḥ and dāraiḥ under vihīna ('bereft of wealth, wife and sons'), or it may be the agent of ādāya ('the sons having taken my wealth'). I took the first reading, with the sons also among the agents of ādāya, because 81.18 names both sons and wife as those who cast him out. 3. The plural dhanaiḥ and dāraiḥ are rendered as the collective 'wealth' and 'wife', which is the usual sense of dāra in the plural. 4. āpta-bandhu is rendered 'trusted kinsmen'. 'Close' or 'reliable' kinsmen would also be possible. ↩
  43. The reading: kuśalākuśalātmikām ("of the nature of well-being and ill-being") qualifies pravṛttim; I render it as "whether it goes well or ill". The word pravṛtti can mean 'news', 'state of affairs' or 'activity'; I chose 'how things stand' because the following verses ask about their welfare and conduct. In svajanānāṃ ca dārāṇāṃ ca, the genitives depend on pravṛttim together with putrāṇām. Atra saṃsthitaḥ is 'staying/situated here', that is, in the forest. ↩
  44. The reading: kiṃ nu is repeated as an interrogative particle ('is it...?'). The verse is a direct question continuing the Vaiśya's statement in 81.20, so I render it as his own questions without a speaker's line. sāmpratam is taken as 'now, at present' and construed with the first question. sadvṛttāḥ and durvṛttāḥ are literally 'of good conduct' and 'of bad conduct'; I keep the literal sense. ↩
  45. The reading: 1. dhanaiḥ (instrumental plural) can be taken with nirasto ("cast out on account of wealth") or with lubdhaiḥ ("greedy for wealth"). I chose the first, as in 81.18, where the Vaiśya says he was cast out dhanalobhāt, "out of greed for wealth". Either way the sense is nearly the same. 2. The e-text gives no speaker's line before this verse. The sense shows the king Suratha is speaking, answering the Vaiśya Samādhi, and the next line gives the Vaiśya's reply. I have added no speaker's line, because the Sanskrit given has none. ↩
  46. that concern me: asmadgatam, literally 'gone to us'. The plural pronoun stands for the singular 'me', so the words are those about the speaker's own case. ↩
  47. does not take on hardness: na badhnāti niṣṭhuratām, literally 'does not bind (or hold) hardness'. The sense is that his mind does not become harsh toward his family, as the previous verse urged. ↩
  48. The reading: The e-text reads 'vaiśca uvāca' at 632.1, which is a misprint. I read it as 'vaiśya uvāca', 'The Vaiśya said', as in the earlier speaker's line at 628.1 and as the context requires. ↩
  49. tender: hārdi is an adjective agreeing with manaḥ (mind): 'affectionate, heartfelt'. It is not the noun hārda, 'affection', used earlier in the verse. ↩
  50. the affection of a husband and of kinsmen: The compound patisvajanahārdam is awkward. Many texts of this passage read patiḥ svajanahārdaṃ ca, 'and I, the husband, [abandoned] the affection of kinsmen'. The GRETIL text gives the compound. ↩
  51. The reading: (1) patisvajanahārdam: the GRETIL compound could be 'the affection of a husband and of kinsmen', which I chose, or a corruption of patiḥ svajanahārdaṃ ca, 'and I, the husband ... the affection of kinsmen'. The compound reading is the one the text gives. (2) pitṛsneham: 'a father's affection' (that which the sons owed or gave) or 'affection for a father'. I chose the first, as the verse concerns the sons' abandonment of their father. (3) santyajya is taken to have the same agent as nirākṛtaḥ, namely the greedy ones (yaiḥ). ↩
  52. O man of great mind: Vocative mahāmate, addressed by the Vaiśya to the sage Medhas; compare the glossary entry mahāmati. ↩
  53. without good qualities: viguṇa: lacking guṇas, that is, good qualities or virtues; the word can also mean 'faulty' or 'ill-behaved'. ↩
  54. The reading: jānann api has no stated object. I supply 'the facts', meaning that the kinsmen are unworthy, which the preceding verses make clear. The speaker is the Vaiśya (Samādhi), but the verse does not name him, and neither does my translation. The 'sage' addressed in the note is Medhas, taken from the story context, not from the verse itself. ↩
  55. though they have no affection [for me]: teṣv aprītiṣu is a locative of the persons concerned ("toward them, the unloving"), and is here read as qualifying teṣu. The object of the missing affection is implied, namely the speaker. ↩
  56. The reading: kṛte with the genitive teṣām means 'for the sake of, on account of'. The verse has 'yan na' ("because not" / "that not"); I take yat as causal, 'since', giving the sense that the mind does not harden. Aprīti can mean 'lacking affection' or 'displeasing'; I chose 'having no affection', which suits the context of their casting him out. The phrase [for me] is supplied to make the English parse. ↩
  57. brahmin: Vocative vipra: Mārkaṇḍeya addresses his listener, the brahmin disciple to whom he tells the Purāṇa. It is not the sage approached. ↩
  58. that best of kings: Pārthivasattama is Suratha, the king who was deprived of his kingdom. He is not named in this verse. ↩
  59. The reading: The sage (muni) approached is not named in the verse. The context supplies Medhas, but the text does not say so here. The verse reads 'sahitau' (together) and 'samupasthitau' (approached), both dual. The 'brahmin' is the vocative vipra, addressed to Mārkaṇḍeya's listener. ↩
  60. exchanged conversation: saṃvidam kṛtvā: 'having made an agreement/understanding', here the exchange of courtesies and greetings with the sage; the sense is of mutual conversation. ↩
  61. The reading: saṃvid can mean 'agreement', 'understanding' or 'conversation'. I took it as an exchange of greetings and talk with the sage (tena, 'with him'), which suits the context. The subject of kṛtvā and upaviṣṭau is the two, the Vaiśya Samādhi and the king Suratha, who are the dual subject of cakratuḥ. ↩
  62. in the absence of [its] being under the control of my own thought: The sentence runs on into 81.30, where the thing asked about is named: mamatva, the sense of 'mine', which Suratha still feels for the kingdom he has lost. The mind's sorrow is bound up with its not obeying his own will. ↩
  63. The reading: The construction of 'yan me manasaḥ duḥkhāya ... svacittāyattatāṃ vinā' is compressed and the verb is not stated. I took 'yat' as the object of 'vadasva' and 'duḥkhāya' as a dative of purpose or result ('for the sorrow of my mind'). I read 'svacittāyattatāṃ vinā' as 'without [the mind] being dependent on one's own mind', that is, not under his control. The reading is supported by 81.30, where the thing in question is the sense of 'mine'. 'Manasaḥ' could also be taken with 'svacitta', but the sense is much the same. ↩
  64. the sense of 'mine': Sanskrit mamatva, the feeling of possessive attachment, 'this is mine'. Suratha says he still feels it for what he no longer holds. ↩
  65. limbs of the kingdom: rājyāṅga: the constituent elements of a state, such as the ministers, treasury, fortresses, army and subjects. The text does not list them here. ↩
  66. best of sages: Vocative munisattama, addressed to the sage Sumedhas, whom Suratha and Samādhi have approached. ↩
  67. The reading: Suratha is the speaker, continuing from 81.29. The verb 'know' (jānataḥ) has no stated object; 'their faults' is supplied from 81.32 (dṛṣṭadoṣe viṣaye). The genitives gatarājyasya, jānataḥ and ajñasya all qualify the speaker, as the 'me' of 81.29 (me). Yathā ajñasya is read as 'like one who does not know', i.e. like an ignorant person. ↩
  68. this man: ayam refers to the Vaiśya Samādhi, whom Suratha is describing as he speaks to the sage. ↩
  69. The reading: The text reads hārde tathāpyati. hārde is a locative of hārda ("in affection"), but the construction with ati is awkward. ati seems to stand for atīva, "excessively", or the word is a misprint for hārdi ("tender"). I took hārde as the adjective hārdi in sense, "tender", and ati as "excessively", with teṣu "toward them". nikṛta can mean "wronged, injured" or "deceived, slighted". I chose "wronged". ↩
  70. the sense of 'mine': Sanskrit mamatva, possessive attachment; the glossary's rendering is used here with the quotation marks of 'mine'. ↩
  71. The reading: viṣaya can mean 'object (of sense or attachment)', 'sphere', or 'realm'. I chose 'object' because the verse concerns attachment of the mind, and the preceding verses speak of the king's lost kingdom and the Vaiśya's family. The compound dṛṣṭadoṣe is taken as a bahuvrīhi qualifying viṣaye ('in which fault has been seen'), and the locative with 'api' is rendered concessively as 'toward an object whose fault we have seen'. 'evam' is rendered 'Thus', picking up the previous verse. ↩
  72. greatly fortunate one: Mahābhāga, the form of address to the sage (Medhas), whom Suratha and Samādhi are questioning. ↩
  73. blinded as we are in discernment: The compound vivekāndha is singular and agrees with 'me' and 'this man' taken together; it means blind with respect to viveka, the power of discrimination. ↩
  74. The reading: The Sanskrit ममास्य च is read as 'to me and to this man' (genitives of the person affected), with vivekāndhasya as a singular qualifier of both. The singular does not differ in sense from a dual here. jñāninoḥ is dual: 'of the two men of knowledge', hence 'both of us'. ↩
  75. ṛṣi: The seer who answers the question of the Vaiśya and the king. In this text he is the sage Medhas, to whom Suratha and the Vaiśya have come. The verse itself gives only the title, not his name. ↩
  76. The reading: The verse number 638.1 is a GRETIL reference and the surrounding verses are numbered 81.28 ff. The speaker is named only as ṛṣi. I have kept the term in IAST and not given the sage's name, since the verse does not name him. ↩
  77. knowledge: jñāna: here the ordinary awareness of sense-objects, which belongs to all creatures, not the higher knowledge of liberation. ↩
  78. the objects themselves differ in this way, each going its own separate way: viṣayaś ca ... yāti: literally 'and the object goes thus, separately, separately'. The repetition pṛthak pṛthak means 'each separately'; the sense is that objects, and creatures' awareness of them, vary from one creature to another, as the following verses illustrate. ↩
  79. The reading: The second half is compressed. viṣayaś ca ... yāti could mean the objects 'pass away/vary', or that the creature's awareness 'goes' to objects differently. I read viṣaya as the subject of yāti, 'goes' in the sense of 'takes its course, differs', with evaṃ pṛthak pṛthak 'in this way, separately', which suits the examples of day-blind and night-blind creatures in 81.35. ↩
  80. The reading: tulyadṛṣṭayaḥ is a bahuvrīhi, 'having equal sight'. The reviewer is right that 'equal sight' was ambiguous in English. I now read it as sight that is the same by day and by night, as the contrast with the first two classes requires: the first class is blind by day, the second blind by night, and the third has no such difference between the two. I did not add 'equally well', since the Sanskrit says only 'equal'. A reading of 'equally limited' is grammatically possible but does not suit the sense. I kept 'living being' for prāṇin, to keep it distinct from jantu, 'living creature', in verse 34. ↩
  81. beginning with domestic animals, birds and wild beasts: The compound paśupakṣimṛgādayaḥ ends in ādi, 'and so on': domestic animals, birds and wild beasts are the first of the classes of beings meant, and other creatures are included with them. ↩
  82. The reading: satyam may be taken as an adverb ('indeed, it is true that') or as a predicate ('it is true'). I chose the adverbial concessive sense, which suits the following kintu ('but'). paśu could also be rendered 'beasts' or 'cattle'; I chose 'domestic animals' to keep it distinct from mṛga, 'wild beasts' (mṛga can also mean 'deer'). ↩
  83. whatever else there is, it is alike in both: The words anyat and tathā ubhayoḥ are taken together: 'the other [qualities] likewise are equal in both'. The verse says that human beings and animals are alike in knowledge and also in their other faculties. ↩
  84. The reading: The text reads mṛgṛpakṣiṇām, which looks like a misprint for mṛgapakṣiṇām, 'of beasts and birds'. I read it so. The syntax is also compressed: yat teṣām is repeated for each group, and I take tulyam as the predicate of both halves. The result is that human knowledge is the same as that of the animals, and the reverse. ↩
  85. birds: pataṅga can mean a bird, a moth or an insect. Here it clearly means birds, since they feed fledglings (śāva) from their beaks (cañcu). ↩
  86. tiger among men: manujavyāghra, a common honorific in address. Here it is addressed to the king, Suratha. ↩
  87. The reading: pataṅgān: 'birds' or 'insects/moths'. I chose 'birds' because they feed their young from their beaks (cañcu). In 81.39, 'etān' most naturally refers to the human beings (mānuṣāḥ), which I have rendered as 'these [men]'. ↩
  88. mamatā: The sense of 'mine', possessive attachment to what one takes as one's own. The compound pictures it as a whirlpool (āvarta) that draws beings in. ↩
  89. Mahāmāyā: The Great Illusion, the Goddess. The power (prabhāva) is hers, and it is the subject of the verses that follow. ↩
  90. The reading: Reviewer's finding on mamatā accepted. 'Selfishness' shifted the sense toward egoism, so I now keep mamatā in IAST between asterisks and explain it in a note. Finding on saṃsārasthitikāriṇā accepted. The adjective agrees with prabhāveṇa, so it is now a relative clause on 'power': 'which brings about the continuance of saṃsāra'. Finding on the unexpressed subject accepted. The Sanskrit gives no subject for nipātitāḥ (masculine plural, 'those made to fall'). I supply '[these beings]' in brackets, meaning the humans and birds of the preceding verses. The agent of the casting is the power (prabhāveṇa). On the Mahāmāyā note I accepted the added remark that the power is hers, but I did not change the kind to 'deity'. Mahāmāyā is in the glossary as a deity, and the note's kind labels the note's content, so I used 'epithet' for the descriptive title 'the Great Illusion'. If the series wants every note on a deity labelled 'deity', that label is not among the permitted kinds (name|epithet|te ↩
  91. yoganidrā: The 'sleep of yoga': the divine slumber of Viṣṇu, here identified with the power of the Goddess Mahāmāyā. ↩
  92. Hari: A name of Viṣṇu. 'The lord of the world' (jagatpati) in the same sentence is taken as the same god. ↩
  93. The reading: 1. I do not accept the finding that etat should be taken with jagat. Jagat already stands as the subject of saṃmohyate, and tathā comes between etat and jagat. I take etat as the neuter subject 'this', namely the power just described. It is predicated of two names: 'the yoganidrā of the lord of the world' and 'the Mahāmāyā of Hari'. The draft's reading of Mahāmāyā as subject, with yoganidrā in apposition, did not fit the word order, and I have dropped it. The ca joins the two genitive phrases, and it is now rendered. 2. I accept the finding on the agent. The Sanskrit has only a bare passive, saṃmohyate, so I dropped 'by her'. The agency is clear from the preceding verse. 3. Tathā is rendered 'thus', as a consequence of what has been said. 4. Jagatpati and Hari are probably the same god, Viṣṇu, but this is an inference. The note now says 'taken as'. Two readings of the second half are possible. One takes it as a single identification, 'Hari's yoganidrā, which is Mahāmāyā'. The other tak ↩
  94. the Blessed One: Translates bhagavatī, the feminine of bhagavat, 'blessed, divine, possessing fortune'; here a title of the Goddess. ↩
  95. The reading: Both findings are accepted. The particle hi is causal, giving the reason for the preceding verses (the world is deluded because of Mahāmāyā), so it is rendered 'For'. The gerund ākṛṣya ('having drawn in') is kept as a gerund, with the Goddess as its agent, and balāt ('forcibly') qualifies it. For prayacchati I keep 'delivers [them] to delusion', which suits the dative mohāya. ↩
  96. brought forth: visṛjyate is the passive of vi-√sṛj, 'to emit, create'; it can also mean 'to release, let go'. The sense of creation is chosen here, with tayā as the agent. ↩
  97. release: muktaye: mukti, liberation from the cycle of rebirth (saṃsāra). ↩
  98. The reading: visṛjyate: 'is brought forth' (creation) or 'is let go, dismissed'. I chose creation, which suits viśvaṃ jagat carācaram and the contrast with muktaye in the second half. Also, the neuter accusative viśvaṃ jagat etat carācaram, as the subject of a passive, is nominative in sense. ↩
  99. supreme knowledge: vidyā is saving knowledge, as against ordinary perception (jñāna) and the ignorance (avidyā) that binds; the Goddess is both. ↩
  100. the sovereign lady of all the lords: sarveśvareśvarī: the feminine 'lord of lords', that is, ruler even of the gods who rule the world. ↩
  101. The reading: hetubhūtā 'who is the cause' could also be read as 'become the cause'; I take bhūta as the ordinary 'being, serving as'. The first hetu (mukteḥ) is genitive with hetubhūtā, and saṃsārabandhahetuḥ is a separate nominative predicate joined by ca. ↩
  102. Venerable sir: Vocative of bhagavat, here a respectful address to the sage; not the Goddess's title bhagavatī. ↩
  103. brahmin: Vocative dvija, 'twice-born', addressed to the sage who has been speaking. ↩
  104. The reading: The text has 'karmāsyāśca'; I read karma asyāḥ ca ('and her work'), with 'kiṃ' as the interrogative. 'Sā' after 'utpannā' is read as the Goddess (the same person), and the first 'sā' belongs with 'devī'. 'Iti' closes the name Mahāmāyā, so the name is quoted in the translation. ↩
  105. the best of those who know brahman: brahmavidāṃ vara: vocative addressed to the sage Medhas, here praised as a knower of the supreme reality, brahman. ↩
  106. brahman: The supreme reality, the absolute; to be distinguished from brāhmaṇa, a brahmin. ↩
  107. The reading: The vocative 'brahmavidāṃ vara' is not further identified in the verse; it is addressed to the sage who has been speaking. Verse 81.45 reads yatsvabhāvā etc. as continuing the questions, so 'ca' is rendered 'And'. 'Yadudbhavā' is taken as 'whose origin is what', i.e. 'what her origin is'. ↩
  108. her form is the world: The compound jaganmūrtiḥ is a bahuvrīhi: she whose form or embodiment is the world. The universe is her visible form. ↩
  109. be heard: The text reads śruyatām, a metrical or printing irregularity for the passive imperative śrūyatām. The sense is unaffected. ↩
  110. The reading: bahudhā: it is an adverb, 'in many ways' or 'manifoldly', not a noun. I now render it as 'in its many forms', qualifying the hearing of her origin, and no longer as 'the many ways'. tatsamutpattiḥ: tad refers to the Goddess, so 'her origin'. śruyatāṃ mama: I keep 'let it be heard from me', with mama as the source or agent of the passive, as the reviewer accepts. The reading 'let it be heard by me' is not the sense, since the speaker is the ṛṣi who is about to teach. śruyatām is an irregular form of śrūyatām, now flagged in a textual note. On jaganmūrtiḥ, the draft note already read the compound as a bahuvrīhi ('having the world as her embodiment'). I have only relabelled it more precisely. ↩
  111. kalpa: A day of Brahmā, a vast cosmic age; at its end the world is dissolved into the waters. ↩
  112. Śeṣa: The cosmic serpent, also called Ananta, who serves as Viṣṇu's couch on the waters. ↩
  113. Madhu and Kaiṭabha: Two demons who, in the well-known story, were born from Viṣṇu's ear-wax as he slept and threatened Brahmā, who sat on the lotus from Viṣṇu's navel. ↩
  114. The reading: karṇamala is 'ear-wax' (literally 'dirt of the ear'); I chose 'wax' as the standard sense in the Madhu-Kaiṭabha story. Śeṣam is accusative object of āstīrya and abhajat: 'spread out Śeṣa [as a couch] and rested on him'; the bracket-free rendering takes bhaj as 'resort to' for yoganidrām and 'rest on' for Śeṣa, a slight zeugma. Also, vikhyātau madhukaiṭabhau is a dual compound, 'Madhu and Kaiṭabha'. ↩
  115. the Prajāpati: 'Lord of creatures', a title of Brahmā as the progenitor of beings. ↩
  116. Janārdana: A name of Viṣṇu (Kṛṣṇa); the glossary-listed Viṣṇu is meant. ↩
  117. with his mind fixed on a single point: ekāgrahṛdayasthitaḥ: literally 'abiding with a heart one-pointed'; it describes Brahmā's state of concentration while praising. ↩
  118. The reading: Hari in 'harer' and 'harinetra' is taken as Viṣṇu, who sleeps. The compound 'harinetrakṛtālayām' is read as 'she who has made her dwelling in Hari's eyes' (the sleep resting in his eyes). Janārdana in 81.51 is the same person as Viṣṇu and Hari. ↩
  119. The lord of fiery energy [praised]: The verse has no finite verb. The accusatives depend on 'he praised' (tuṣṭāva) in 81.52. Prabhuḥ tejasaḥ (nominative, 'lord of fiery energy') is Brahmā, the subject there. ↩
  120. Viṣṇu's Sleep: Nidrā is the same goddess as yoganidrā in 81.49 and 81.52, here named as Viṣṇu's own Sleep. ↩
  121. The reading: The pāda viṣṇor atulāṃ tejasaḥ prabhuḥ is awkward. (1) prabhuḥ is nominative masculine and cannot qualify the feminine Nidrā. I take it as Brahmā, the subject of tuṣṭāva in 81.52. (2) I now join tejasaḥ with prabhuḥ ('lord of fiery energy'), as the reviewer proposed. A genitive of respect after atulā ('unequalled in fiery energy') is strained, and it leaves prabhuḥ as a bare 'lord'. Atulām then stands alone as 'the incomparable'. (3) The reading may be a corruption of 'viṣṇor atulatejasaḥ' ('of Viṣṇu of unequalled fiery energy'), but I translate the text as given. (4) On kāriṇī I keep 'who brings about', which is closer than 'maker'. I kept 'its' with maintenance and dissolution because the world is the obvious referent from jagad-dhātrī. ↩
  122. svāhā: The ritual exclamation that accompanies an offering to the gods in the fire; also personified as a goddess, the wife of Agni. Svadhā, the next term, is the corresponding exclamation for offerings to the ancestors (pitṛ), likewise personified. ↩
  123. vaṣaṭkāra: The cry vaṣaṭ, uttered by the hotṛ (or the adhvaryu) as the oblation is offered. ↩
  124. mātrās: Moras, units of measure of vocal sound. The three are those of the syllable oṃ (a, u, m). Verse 81.55 adds the half-mātrā, the nasal resonance beyond them. ↩
  125. The reading: 1. akṣare nitye: I follow the reviewer and take it as a locative, 'in the imperishable, eternal [syllable]'. The draft's nominative was wrong. But akṣare nitye can also be feminine vocatives (of akṣarā and nityā), 'O imperishable, eternal one', and nityā in 81.55 lends that reading some support. I chose the locative because akṣara also means 'syllable' and the three mātrās belong to oṃ. 2. svarātmikā is feminine, so it qualifies the Goddess, not masculine vaṣaṭkāraḥ. The draft was wrong, and I now render 'you whose nature is sound'. svara can mean vowel, tone or sound, and 'sacred' is dropped as an overinterpretation. 3. tridhā is taken adverbially, 'threefold', and mātrātmikā as 'consisting of the mātrās'. 4. sudhā is rendered 'nectar'; it is the nectar of immortality. ↩
  126. half-mātrā: The ardhamātrā is the subtle half-measure that follows the three mātrās of oṃ (a, u, m): the silent resonance of the syllable, beyond ordinary pronunciation. ↩
  127. Sāvitrī: The sacred verse addressed to Savitṛ, the sun, identified with the Gāyatrī and recited at the evening worship; here also a name of the Goddess. ↩
  128. The reading: viśeṣataḥ: it can be read 'especially' or 'in particular', and it could be joined to sthitā or to anuccāryā. I join it to anuccāryā ('especially unutterable') because it stands directly before that word and the relative clause yā ... anuccāryā is complete without it elsewhere. I now use 'especially' as the reviewer asks; my earlier 'in particular' was the same attachment. The finding on yā is accepted: the relative clause is restored ('you who are ...'). anuccāryā is a gerundive and could be 'not to be uttered' (a prohibition) or 'unutterable'; I chose 'unutterable', since the half-mora is by nature not pronounced, and the text states no prohibition. ↩
  129. you always devour [it] at the end: The text reads tvamatsyante, which is to be divided tvam atsy ante: atsi (second person singular of the root ad, 'eat, devour') followed by ante, 'at the end', i.e. at the dissolution of the world. The object 'it' is understood from the preceding clauses. ↩
  130. The reading: tvamatsyante is run together in the e-text. I divide it tvam atsy ante, 'you devour at the end', with atsi before a vowel becoming atsy. Taking it as a future or passive form of another verb makes no sense in the context. sarvadā 'always' could go with the whole verse, but I join it to the last clause. ↩
  131. O you who are the whole world: Vocative of jaganmayā, 'made of the world, consisting of the world': the Goddess is addressed as identical with the universe. ↩
  132. The reading: visṛṣṭau is taken as 'in the creation' (locative of visṛṣṭi, 'emission, creation'). The verse begins with the 'creation' of the world, and sṛṣṭirūpā ('form of creation') echoes it, which makes the repetition slightly redundant. Some may read it as 'at the time of emission'. Also, ante is taken with jagato 'sya as 'at the end of this world'. ↩
  133. Mahāvidyā: 'Great Knowledge'. The 'mahā-' titles in this verse are kept in IAST: Mahāmāyā 'Great Illusion', Mahāmedhā 'Great Intelligence' (or retentive power), Mahāsmṛti 'Great Memory', Mahāmohā 'Great Delusion', Mahādevī 'Great Goddess', Maheśvarī 'Great Sovereign' (feminine). ↩
  134. The reading: bhavatī is the pronoun 'you' (feminine of bhavat), not bhagavatī 'the Blessed One'. The first reading in my draft was a mistake, and the title has been removed. The reviewer's third finding is accepted: the whole verse is one series of predicates with a single 'you are'. The compound mahāvidyā could also be read as the plain phrase 'great knowledge', but as a list of the Goddess's names it is better kept as a title. ↩
  135. prakṛti: Here the primal Nature of Sāṃkhya thought, the source of all that exists, not the 'constituents of the state' of the glossary entry. ↩
  136. guṇas: The three constituent qualities of prakṛti: sattva, rajas and tamas. ↩
  137. Kālarātri, Mahārātri and the dreadful Moharātri: 'Night of Time (or Death)', 'Great Night' and 'Night of Delusion'; names of the Goddess. The first is the night of universal dissolution, the last the night of moha. ↩
  138. The reading: The reading 'prakṛtis tvaṃ ca' is taken as 'you are also prakṛti'. 'Sarvasya' is genitive, 'of everything', and is taken with prakṛti. 'Guṇatrayavibhāvinī' can mean 'the one who manifests' or 'the one who distinguishes' the three guṇas; I chose 'manifests'. 'Dāruṇā' is feminine singular and could qualify only Moharātri or all three Nights. I took it with Moharātri, the nearest, as the Sanskrit order suggests. ↩
  139. Śrī: Prosperity, beauty; the goddess of fortune. In this hymn the Goddess is addressed under the names of these qualities. ↩
  140. Īśvarī: 'Sovereign lady', feminine of Īśvara, 'lord'. ↩
  141. Hrī: Modesty, a sense of shame; here both Hrī and Lajjā are named. ↩
  142. whose mark is awareness: bodhalakṣaṇā: a compound that can also be read 'characterized by awakening' or 'having awareness as her definition'. ↩
  143. Puṣṭi: Nourishment, thriving. Tuṣṭi is contentment, Śānti peace, Kṣānti forbearance. ↩
  144. the bearer of the sword: The feminine possessive forms in -inī (khaḍiganī, śūlinī, gadinī, cakriṇī, śaṅkhinī, cāpinī) all mean 'she who has or bears' the weapon named. The text reads khaḍiganī for khaḍginī. ↩
  145. bhuśuṇḍi: A weapon of uncertain identity, variously explained as a sling, a kind of club, or a firearm-like missile; left untranslated. The compound bāṇabhuśuṇḍī is read here as 'arrow and bhuśuṇḍi'. ↩
  146. The reading: The text has khaḍiganī, apparently a misprint for khaḍginī ('sword-bearer'); I read it so. The compound bāṇabhuśuṇḍī could be taken as one item or as two weapons, 'arrow' and 'bhuśuṇḍi'. I took it as two, since both are weapons and a dvandva is natural in this list. The form as printed is a feminine in -ī, which agrees with parighāyudhā. ↩
  147. gentler than all gentle things: The text reads saumyā saumyatarā aśeṣasaumyebhyaḥ. Saumyatarā is a separate nominative, 'more gentle', and aśeṣasaumyebhyaḥ is the ablative of comparison, 'than all gentle things'. ↩
  148. the higher and the lower: parāparāṇām: of things higher and lower, that is, of all beings and things in their ranks; some take it as 'of causes and effects'. ↩
  149. supreme sovereign lady: Parameśvarī: the feminine of Parameśvara, 'supreme lord'; here the Goddess as highest sovereign. ↩
  150. The reading: I accept the reviewer's splitting of saumyā saumyatarā aśeṣa-saumyebhyaḥ, with saumyatarā as a separate predicate. I also accept deleting 'even the gentlest' and rendering tu ('indeed'). The ablative aśeṣasaumyebhyaḥ stands between saumyatarā and atisundarī, so it could also go with ati-sundarī ('beautiful beyond all gentle things'). A comparative ordinarily takes the ablative, so I attach it to saumyatarā and read atisundarī as 'exceedingly beautiful'. Either way the sense is close. parāparāṇām could also mean 'of causes and effects' or 'of the highest and lowest'; I keep the general 'higher and lower'. ↩
  151. real or unreal: sadasat: 'being and non-being', the manifest and the unmanifest; the whole of existence is meant. ↩
  152. power: The e-text reads śiktiḥ, evidently a misprint for śaktiḥ, 'power'. The Goddess is here Śakti, the power behind all things. ↩
  153. how can you be praised: kiṃ stūyate is passive and impersonal: 'what is praised then?', meaning that no praise can be adequate. ↩
  154. The reading: The e-text's śiktiḥ is read as śaktiḥ ('power'), since śikti has no sense here and the reading of 81.64–65 (śakti) supports it. The phrase 'kiṃ stūyate tadā' is read as a rhetorical question ('how is praise possible?'), not as a literal 'what is praised then?'. The compound sadasadvā is taken as sat and asat with 'vā'. akhilātmike is a vocative: 'O you who are the soul of all'. ↩
  155. he who is the creator of the world: Viṣṇu, who lies asleep in the Madhu-Kaiṭabha episode; verse 81.65 names him. The verse itself does not. ↩
  156. The reading: All four findings are accepted. 'Even he' (so 'pi) is now the subject, and 'by you' (yayā tvayā) is the agent of nītaḥ. The relative yayā is rendered 'by whom'. The yo ... so correlative is kept, and the 'then' (tadā) that wrongly came from 81.63 is removed. Jagatpātyatti is read as jagat pāti atti by sandhi, 'protects the world, devours the world'; the three roles belong to the one person referred to by yo. Īśvaraḥ with the infinitive stotum is taken as 'able', not 'lord'. The verse does not name Viṣṇu, so the translation does not either; the identification is given in a note, from 81.65. ↩
  157. I: The speaker is Brahmā, who is praising the Goddess in this hymn. He names himself with Viṣṇu and Īśāna, the other two gods of the triad. ↩
  158. Īśāna: 'Ruler'; an epithet of Śiva (Rudra). ↩
  159. to take bodies: kāritāḥ is a causative participle in the plural, with śarīragrahaṇam as its object, 'the taking of a body'. The agent is the Goddess, given by te. ↩
  160. The reading: The text reads 'śarīragrahaṇamahāmīśāna', which is apparently a misprint. I read it as śarīragrahaṇam aham īśānaḥ, 'the taking of a body — I [and] Īśāna'. The long ā in 'mahām' does not fit, and 'mahā' would leave the verse without the speaker's 'aham', which the plural kāritāḥ (Viṣṇu, I, Īśāna) requires. ↩
  161. Devī: Vocative of devī, 'goddess', here addressed to the Goddess as Nidrā/Parameśvarī. ↩
  162. asuras: The text has the singular 'asuro' (asuraḥ), which does not agree with the dual 'madhukaiṭabhau'. It is probably a misprint for the dual 'asurau', 'two asuras'. ↩
  163. The reading: 'asuro' (asuraḥ, nominative singular) beside the dual 'madhukaiṭabhau' and 'etau durādharṣau': I read it as an error for the dual 'asurau' (or sandhi-form 'asurau'), taking it in apposition to Madhu and Kaiṭabha. A singular would not agree with the two names. 'sā tvam' is taken as 'you, that [being]', referring back to the Goddess described in the earlier verses. ↩
  164. Acyuta: 'The unfallen, the imperishable'; an epithet of Viṣṇu. ↩
  165. his awakening: bodha here is simply 'awakening, wakefulness', and so repeats prabodha in the first half of the verse. The Goddess is asked to wake Viṣṇu and so enable him to kill the two. ↩
  166. The reading: prabodhañca is read as prabodham ca, 'and to awakening', accusative with nīyatām. jagatsvāmī is nominative, in apposition to Acyuta, the subject of nīyatām. Following the review, asya is taken as a genitive, 'his', and bodha as 'awakening, wakefulness', not 'awareness' or 'intention'. The purpose clause hantum etau mahāsurau is rendered 'in order to slay these two great asuras', without any further sense for bodha. ↩
  167. Vedhas: Vedhas, 'the arranger' or 'creator', is an epithet of Brahmā, who here is the one praising the Goddess. ↩
  168. in her form of Tamas: tāmasī: the Goddess in her aspect of tamas, the quality of darkness and inertia, which is here the Sleep that holds Viṣṇu. ↩
  169. from his eyes, mouths, nostrils, arms, heart and chest: The ablatives are not marked for whose body is meant. It is Viṣṇu's, as the Goddess leaves the sleeping god. The compound's singular words (mouth, nostril) are rendered as plurals, which is a common reading of such a series. ↩
  170. whose birth is unmanifest: avyaktajanman: Brahmā is born from the lotus growing from Viṣṇu's navel, or from the Unmanifest (avyakta). ↩
  171. The reading: Vedhasā: it is the agent of stutā, so Brahmā, as in 81.63-67, where he is the one who praises. Netrāsyanāsikābāhuhṛdayebhyaḥ: the singulars in the compound could be read as singular nouns (the eyes, mouth, nose, arm, heart); the plural is chosen because the context implies the two eyes, nostrils and arms, and the plural reads naturally. The text is also commonly read as 'mouth' in the singular; this is a minor point. Whose body she leaves is not said; Viṣṇu is implied by the context and the verses after. ↩
  172. the lord of the world: Jagannātha, an epithet of Viṣṇu, in apposition to Janārdana. It is translated here, as jagatsvāmī was in 81.67. ↩
  173. serpent-couch: ahiśayana, literally 'snake-bed'. The verse does not name the serpent. That it is Śeṣa (Ananta), on whom Viṣṇu sleeps during the dissolution, is the usual understanding and a gloss, not the text. ↩
  174. single ocean: ekārṇava: the one undivided ocean left when the universe is dissolved. ↩
  175. The reading: ahiśayanāt (ablative) is taken with uttasthau, 'rose up from the serpent-couch', and ekārṇave (locative) is taken as qualifying the couch, 'on the single ocean', in line with the reviewer's finding. It could instead be read with the next clause ('from the serpent-couch he saw them'), but the first reading is more natural. I read mukto as 'freed' by the Devī, who is Tamas as sleep, and not as the technical mukti 'release'. I kept 'those two' for tau, which refers to Madhu and Kaiṭabha. The first ca joins the clauses as 'and'. The second ca is not translated separately. ↩
  176. vigor and prowess: Sanskrit vīrya and parākrama; the compound ativīryaparākrama means 'of excessive vigor and prowess'. Vikrama/parākrama, 'prowess', is distinct from vīrya, 'vigor'. ↩
  177. The reading: janitodyamau, literally 'one who has produced an effort', is taken as 'had made ready' (having set about) with the infinitive attum, 'to devour'. The verse has no finite verb; the English supplies 'had made ready' to render the participial compound. The text has brahmāṇam as the object of attum. ↩
  178. blessed: bhagavān, an honorific of divine beings, used here with Hari, a name of Viṣṇu. ↩
  179. all-pervading lord: vibhu, 'all-pervading' or 'mighty', an epithet of Viṣṇu. ↩
  180. The reading: The word vibhuḥ can be read as 'mighty' or 'all-pervading'. I chose 'all-pervading lord' as the epithet for Viṣṇu. Samutthāya is an absolutive meaning 'having risen up', and its agent is Hari, the subject of the main verb. The verse opens with 'then', and 'rose up' follows on the previous verse's mention of Janārdana's rising. ↩
  181. Keśava: A name of Viṣṇu, here applied to Hari; traditionally explained as 'he of fine hair' or 'slayer of the asura Keśin'. ↩
  182. boon: vara: a gift granted to one who asks, here offered by the two asuras to Viṣṇu. ↩
  183. The reading: atibalonmattau may be read as 'intoxicated with excessive strength' (chosen) or 'mad with excessive pride in their strength'. 'Varo 'smatto vriyatām' is taken as 'let a boon be chosen from us' (ablative of source), the speaker being the two asuras. ↩
  184. The Blessed One: Bhagavān here refers to Viṣṇu (Hari/Keśava), who has just been offered a boon by Madhu and Kaiṭabha and now replies. ↩
  185. The reading: The e-text's verse numbering is irregular (648.1, between 81.73 and 81.74). The speaker is not named in the verse; from context (81.73, Keśava has been offered a boon) he is Viṣṇu, and I have rendered bhagavān as 'The Blessed One' without naming him in the translation. ↩
  186. may you two be pleased: The e-text reads bhavanetām, which gives no sense. The translation follows the emended reading bhavetām adya, an optative dual ('may you two be'). The optative expresses a wish or request. ↩
  187. may you both be slain by me: Sanskrit vadhyau ... ubhau api. The gerundive vadhya means 'to be killed' or 'fit to be killed'. It is still governed by the optative bhavetām, so the request is that they become the ones he may slay. This is the boon he chooses. ↩
  188. The reading: The e-text's bhavanetām is probably a misprint for bhavetām (optative dual of bhū), and the translation follows that emendation. The optative is kept as a wish: 'may you be pleased with me; may you both be slain by me'. This corrects my earlier draft, which turned it into a statement ('you have become pleased', 'you shall be slain'). In ubhāv api, api only reinforces ubhau ('both'). I translate hi in etāvad dhi as 'for', since it explains why no other boon is needed. ↩
