  189. thus deceived: vañcitābhyām and tābhyām are both dual instrumentals of agent, referring to Madhu and Kaiṭabha; they are the agents of vilokya and of the passive gadito. The deception is Viṣṇu's request in 81.74, that they become his victims, in answer to their offer of a boon. ↩
  190. The reading: iti: the reviewer would render it as 'by this', making it the means of the deception. I keep 'thus'. Here iti closes the preceding exchange and qualifies vañcitābhyām adverbially ('deceived in that manner'). The instrumental in vañcitābhyām is the instrumental of agent that goes with gadito, not an instrumental of means, so 'by this' would read a second instrumental into the line. I took 'whole world made wholly of water' as repeating itself and rendered āpomayam as 'consisted of water'. ↩
  191. where the earth is not submerged in water: The printed yatrorvo is read as yatra urvī, 'where the earth', agreeing with the feminine pariplutā. The negative na belongs with pariplutā. ↩
  192. We two are pleased: svastava is read as svaḥ tava, with svaḥ the dual 'we two are'. It could also be a corruption of sma tava, which would give the same sense. ↩
  193. The reading: yatrorvo: read as yatra urvī, 'where the earth', because pariplutā is feminine singular and the verse asks for dry ground. svastava: read as svaḥ tava, 'we two are ... your'. The alternative is a corruption of sma tava, with the same meaning. ↩
  194. the bearer of the conch, discus and mace: Śaṅkhacakragadābhṛt, a standard epithet of Viṣṇu, from the three weapons and emblems he carries: the conch, the discus (cakra) and the mace. ↩
  195. laid [them] on his thigh: Kṛtvā is an absolutive whose agent is bhagavatā, the same agent as uktvā. With the locative jaghane it means 'having placed on the thigh'. The object is not stated; it is the two demons, who asked in 81.76 to be killed where the earth is not under water. ↩
  196. were severed: Cchinne is the dual neuter participle used as the predicate of the main clause, agreeing with śirasī, 'the two heads'. The construction is passive, with cakreṇa as the instrument. The agent, the Blessed One, is understood from the preceding absolutives. ↩
  197. The reading: I accept the reviewer's main correction. Cchinne śirasī is the main-clause predicate, 'the two heads were severed', and not a participle phrase serving as the object of kṛtvā. Kṛtvā with the locative jaghane means 'having placed on the thigh', so the placing comes before the cutting, as 81.76 requires. The object of kṛtvā is not in the Sanskrit; I supply 'them' in square brackets. The third finding misdescribes the construction when it makes śirasī the object of kṛtvā, so I follow its conclusion (cchinne as predicate) and not that description. I leave the particle vai untranslated as purely emphatic, which the reviewer accepts as minor. ↩
  198. she came into being: samutpannā is feminine singular and refers to the Goddess (Tuṣṭi), whom Brahmā hymns in this chapter. ↩
  199. power: prabhāva: the efficacious power or majesty of the Goddess, which the chapters that follow illustrate. ↩
  200. The reading: 1) svayam could be taken with samutpannā ('she came into being of herself') or with brahmaṇā ('Brahmā himself'). I took it with brahmaṇā, the nearer word, since it stresses that the creator god himself praised her. 2) te may be dative or genitive of tvam. I took it as the dative of the person addressed with vadāmi ('I will tell it to you'), which is the usual construction. 3) I follow the reviewer on samutpannā: 'came into being' replaces 'arose', and my earlier reason for it (that she is not born in the ordinary sense) is dropped. 4) The verse names neither speaker nor hearer, so I add no name for the 'you'. ↩
  201. eighty-first chapter: The e-text's own verse numbering reads 81 here, though the running chapter number given in the reference is 77; the colophon's ekāśītitama is rendered as stated. ↩
  202. Devīmāhātmya: "The Greatness of the Goddess": the section of the Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa devoted to the Goddess's exploits, within the account of the eighth Manu's age. ↩
  203. Sāvarṇika: sāvarṇike manvantare: the age of Sāvarṇi, the eighth Manu; the section stands within the account of that age. ↩
  204. The reading: śrī- in śrīmārkaṇḍeyapurāṇe is an honorific prefix ('revered'), not the goddess Śrī. sāvarṇike is taken as a locative adjective of manvantare ('Sāvarṇika'), meaning the age of Sāvarṇi. ↩

Adhyāya 82

Sanskrit

द्व्यशीतितमो ऽध्यायः- ८२

dvyaśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ- 82

English

The eighty-second chapter.1

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

82.1

Sanskrit

देवासुरमभूद् युद्धं पूर्णमब्दशतं पुरा । महिषे ऽसुराणामधिपे देवानां च पुरन्दरे ॥

devāsuramabhūd yuddhaṃ pūrṇamabdaśataṃ purā | mahiṣe 'surāṇāmadhipe devānāṃ ca purandare ||

English

Long ago there was a war between the devas2 and the asuras3 that lasted a full hundred years, when Mahiṣa4 was the overlord of the asuras and Purandara was the lord of the devas.5

82.2

Sanskrit

तत्रासुरैर्महावीर्यैर्देवसैन्यं पराजितम् । जित्वा च सकलान् देवानिन्द्रो ऽबून्महिषासुरः ॥

tatrāsurairmahāvīryairdevasainyaṃ parājitam | jitvā ca sakalān devānindro 'būnmahiṣāsuraḥ ||

English

There the army of the devas was defeated by the asuras, who were of great vigor; and having conquered all the devas, Mahiṣa, the asura, became Indra6.

82.3

Sanskrit

ततः पराजिता देवाः पद्मयोनिं प्रजापतिम् । पुरस्कृत्य गतास्तत्र यत्रेशगरुडध्वजौ ॥

tataḥ parājitā devāḥ padmayoniṃ prajāpatim | puraskṛtya gatāstatra yatreśagaruḍadhvajau ||

English

82.3–4Then the defeated devas, putting the lotus-born Prajāpati7 in front of them, went there, to where Īśa8 and the Garuḍa-bannered one9 were. The devas told the two of them, just as it had happened, and likewise the doings of Mahiṣa, the asura, the full account of the devas' overthrow.1011

82.4

Sanskrit

यथावृत्तं तयोस्तद्वन्महिषासुरचेष्टितम् । त्रिदशाः कथयामासुर्देवाभिभवविस्तरम् ॥

yathāvṛttaṃ tayostadvanmahiṣāsuraceṣṭitam | tridaśāḥ kathayāmāsurdevābhibhavavistaram ||

English

Its English is given with 82.3–4, above.

82.5

Sanskrit

सूर्येन्द्रग्न्यनिलेन्दूनां यमस्य वरुणस्य च । अन्येषां चाधिकारान् स स्वयमेवाधितिष्ठिति ॥

sūryendragnyanilendūnāṃ yamasya varuṇasya ca | anyeṣāṃ cādhikārān sa svayamevādhitiṣṭhiti ||

English

He himself presides over12 the offices of Sūrya, Indra, Agni, Anila13 and Indu14, of Yama and of Varuṇa, and of the others as well.15

82.6

Sanskrit

स्वर्गान्निराकृताः सर्वे तेन देवगणा भुवि । विचरन्ति यथा मर्त्या महिषेण दुरात्मना ॥

svargānnirākṛtāḥ sarve tena devagaṇā bhuvi | vicaranti yathā martyā mahiṣeṇa durātmanā ||

English

Driven out of heaven by that wicked-souled Mahiṣa16, all the hosts of the devas wander on earth like mortals.

82.7

Sanskrit

एतद्वः कथितं सर्वममरारिविचेष्टितम् । शरणं वः प्रपन्नाः स्मो वधस्तस्य विचिन्त्यताम् ॥

etadvaḥ kathitaṃ sarvamamarāriviceṣṭitam | śaraṇaṃ vaḥ prapannāḥ smo vadhastasya vicintyatām ||

English

All this, the doings of the enemy of the immortals17, has been told to you. We have come to you for refuge18; let his slaying be considered.

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

82.8

Sanskrit

इत्थं निशम्य देवानां वचांसि मधुसूदनः । चकार कोपं शम्भुश्च भ्रुकुटीकुटिलाननौ ॥

itthaṃ niśamya devānāṃ vacāṃsi madhusūdanaḥ | cakāra kopaṃ śambhuśca bhrukuṭīkuṭilānanau ||

English

Having heard these words of the devas in this way, Madhusūdana19 grew angry, and so did Śambhu20; their faces were twisted by frowns.21

82.9

Sanskrit

ततो ऽतिकोपपूर्णस्य चक्रिणो वदनात्ततः । निश्चक्राम महत्तेजो ब्रह्मणः शङ्करस्य च ॥

tato 'tikopapūrṇasya cakriṇo vadanāttataḥ | niścakrāma mahattejo brahmaṇaḥ śaṅkarasya ca ||

English

Then, from the face of the discus-bearer22, who was filled with extreme anger, a great radiance23 came forth, and from [the faces] of Brahmā and of Śaṅkara24 as well.25

82.10

Sanskrit

अन्येषाञ्चैव देवानां शक्रादीनां शरीरतः । निर्गतं सुमहत्तेजस्तच्चैक्यं समगच्छत ॥

anyeṣāñcaiva devānāṃ śakrādīnāṃ śarīrataḥ | nirgataṃ sumahattejastaccaikyaṃ samagacchata ||

English

And from the bodies of the other devas as well, Śakra and the rest, a very great radiance came forth, and that radiance came together into one.26

82.11

Sanskrit

अतीव तेजसः कूटं ज्वलन्तमिव पर्वतम् । ददृशुस्ते सुरास्तत्र ज्वालाव्याप्तदिगन्तरम् ॥

atīva tejasaḥ kūṭaṃ jvalantamiva parvatam | dadṛśuste surāstatra jvālāvyāptadigantaram ||

English

There the suras27 saw that exceedingly great mass of radiance28, like a blazing mountain, its flames filling the spaces between the quarters.

82.12

Sanskrit

अतुलं तत्र तत्तेजः सर्वदेवशरीरजम् । एकस्थं तदभून्नारी व्याप्तलोकत्रयं त्विषा ॥

atulaṃ tatra tattejaḥ sarvadevaśarīrajam | ekasthaṃ tadabhūnnārī vyāptalokatrayaṃ tviṣā ||

English

There that incomparable radiance, born from the bodies of all the devas, gathered in one place and became a woman; it filled the three worlds29 with its glow.30

82.13

Sanskrit

यदभूच्छाम्भवं तेजस्तेनाजायत तन्मुखम् । याम्येन चाभवन् केशा बहवो विष्णुतेजसा ॥

yadabhūcchāmbhavaṃ tejastenājāyata tanmukham | yāmyena cābhavan keśā bahavo viṣṇutejasā ||

English

By the fiery energy that was Śambhu's, her face was produced; by Yama's31 [fiery energy] her hair came to be, and by Viṣṇu's fiery energy, her arms32.33†

82.14

Sanskrit

सौम्येन स्तनयोर्युग्मं मध्यं चैन्द्रेण चाभवत् । वारुणेन च जङ्घोरू नितम्बस्तेजसा भुवः ॥

saumyena stanayoryugmaṃ madhyaṃ caindreṇa cābhavat | vāruṇena ca jaṅghorū nitambastejasā bhuvaḥ ||

English

From the fiery energy of Soma34 came the pair of breasts, and from that of Indra the waist; from that of Varuṇa came the shanks and thighs, and from the fiery energy of Bhū35 [Earth] the hips.36†

82.15

Sanskrit

ब्रह्मणस्तेजसा पादौ तदङ्गुल्योर्ऽकतेजसा । वसूनाञ्च कराङ्गुल्यः कौबेरेण च नासिका ॥

brahmaṇastejasā pādau tadaṅgulyor'katejasā | vasūnāñca karāṅgulyaḥ kaubereṇa ca nāsikā ||

English

From the radiance of Brahmā came her two feet; her toes came from the radiance of the sun37; her fingers came from [the radiance] of the Vasus38; and her nose came from that of Kubera39.40

82.16

Sanskrit

तस्यास्तु दन्ताः सम्भूताः प्राजापत्येन तेजसा । नयनत्रितयं जज्ञे तथा पावकतेजसा ॥

tasyāstu dantāḥ sambhūtāḥ prājāpatyena tejasā | nayanatritayaṃ jajñe tathā pāvakatejasā ||

English

Her teeth were produced from the radiance of Prajāpati41, and likewise the triad of her eyes was born from the radiance of Pāvaka42.43

82.17

Sanskrit

भ्रुवौ च सन्ध्ययोस्तेजः श्रवणावनिलस्य च । अन्येषां चैव देवानां सम्भवस्तेजसां शिवा ॥

bhruvau ca sandhyayostejaḥ śravaṇāvanilasya ca | anyeṣāṃ caiva devānāṃ sambhavastejasāṃ śivā ||

English

Her two eyebrows [came] from the radiance of the two twilights44, and her two ears from that of Anila. Thus the auspicious one45 had her origin in the radiances of the other devas as well.46

82.18

Sanskrit

ततः समस्तदेवानां तेजोराशिसमुद्भवाम् । तां विलोक्य मुदं प्रापुरमरा महिषार्दिताः ॥

tataḥ samastadevānāṃ tejorāśisamudbhavām | tāṃ vilokya mudaṃ prāpuramarā mahiṣārditāḥ ||

English

Then the immortals, afflicted by Mahiṣa47, saw her, who had arisen from the mass of the radiances of all the devas, and they were filled with joy.48

82.19

Sanskrit

ततो देवा ददुस्तस्यै स्वानि स्वान्यायुधानि च । ऊचुर्जयजयेत्युच्चैर्जयन्तीं ते जयैषिणः ॥

tato devā dadustasyai svāni svānyāyudhāni ca | ūcurjayajayetyuccairjayantīṃ te jayaiṣiṇaḥ ||

English

Then the devas gave her their own weapons, each his own; and, desiring victory, they cried aloud to her, the victorious one49, "Victory! Victory!"

82.20

Sanskrit

शूलं शूलाद्विनिष्कृष्य ददौ तस्यै पिनाकधृक् । चक्रं च दत्तवान् कृष्णः समुत्पाद्य स्वचक्रतः ॥

śūlaṃ śūlādviniṣkṛṣya dadau tasyai pinākadhṛk | cakraṃ ca dattavān kṛṣṇaḥ samutpādya svacakrataḥ ||

English

The bearer of the Pināka50 drew a spear out of [his] spear and gave it to her; and Kṛṣṇa51 gave a discus, having produced it from his own discus.52†

82.21

Sanskrit

शङ्खञ्च वरुणः शक्तिं ददौ तस्यै हुताशनः । मारुतो दत्तवांश्चापं बाणपूर्णे तथेषुधी ॥

śaṅkhañca varuṇaḥ śaktiṃ dadau tasyai hutāśanaḥ | māruto dattavāṃścāpaṃ bāṇapūrṇe tatheṣudhī ||

English

Varuṇa gave her a conch, and Hutāśana53 gave her a spear54; Māruta55 gave a bow, and likewise two quivers full of arrows.56

82.22

Sanskrit

वज्रमिन्द्रः समुत्पाद्य कुलिशादमराधिपः । ददौ तस्यै सहस्राक्षो घण्टामैरावताद् गजात् ॥

vajramindraḥ samutpādya kuliśādamarādhipaḥ | dadau tasyai sahasrākṣo ghaṇṭāmairāvatād gajāt ||

English

Indra, the lord of the immortals, the thousand-eyed one57, drew a thunderbolt out of his own thunderbolt58 and gave it to her; and from the elephant Airāvata59 he gave her a bell.

82.23

Sanskrit

कालदण्डाद्यमो दण्डं माशं चाम्बुपतिर्ददौ । प्रजापतिश्चाक्षमालां ददौ ब्रह्मा कमण्डलुम् ॥

kāladaṇḍādyamo daṇḍaṃ māśaṃ cāmbupatirdadau | prajāpatiścākṣamālāṃ dadau brahmā kamaṇḍalum ||

English

Yama gave [her] a staff, [drawn] from the staff of Time, and the lord of the waters60 gave a noose61. Prajāpati62 gave a rosary, and Brahmā a water-pot.63

82.24

Sanskrit

समस्तरोमकूपेषु निजरश्मीन् दिवाकरः । कालख्च दत्तवान् खड्गं तस्याश्चर्म च निर्मलम् ॥

samastaromakūpeṣu nijaraśmīn divākaraḥ | kālakhca dattavān khaḍgaṃ tasyāścarma ca nirmalam ||

English

Divākara64 gave his own rays into all her pores65, and Kāla66 gave her a sword and a spotless shield.67

82.25

Sanskrit

क्षीरोदश्चामलं हारमजरे च तथाम्बरे । चूडामणिं तथा दिव्यं कुण्डले कटकानि च ॥

kṣīrodaścāmalaṃ hāramajare ca tathāmbare | cūḍāmaṇiṃ tathā divyaṃ kuṇḍale kaṭakāni ca ||

English

82.25–26Kṣīroda68 [gave her] a spotless necklace, and likewise two undecaying garments, a divine crest-jewel, two earrings and bracelets; and likewise a bright half-moon ornament69, armlets for all [her] arms, and likewise two flawless anklets, an unsurpassed neck-ornament70, and jewelled rings for all [her] fingers.71

82.26

Sanskrit

अर्धचन्द्रं तथा शुभ्रं केयूरान् सर्वबाहुषु । नूपुरौ विमलौ तद्वद् ग्रैवेयकमनुत्तमम् । अङ्गुलीयकरत्नानि समस्तास्वङ्गुलीषु च ॥

ardhacandraṃ tathā śubhraṃ keyūrān sarvabāhuṣu | nūpurau vimalau tadvad graiveyakamanuttamam | aṅgulīyakaratnāni samastāsvaṅgulīṣu ca ||

English

Its English is given with 82.25–26, above.

82.27

Sanskrit

विश्वकर्मा ददौ तस्यै परशुञ्चातिनिर्मलम् । अस्त्राण्यनेकरूपाणि तथाभेद्यं च दंशनम् ॥

viśvakarmā dadau tasyai paraśuñcātinirmalam | astrāṇyanekarūpāṇi tathābhedyaṃ ca daṃśanam ||

English

Viśvakarman72 gave her an axe, perfectly spotless, and missiles of many forms, and likewise an impenetrable armour73.74

82.28

Sanskrit

अम्लानपङ्कजां मालां शिरस्युरसि चापराम् । अददज्जलधिस्तस्यै पङ्कजं चातिशोभनम् ॥

amlānapaṅkajāṃ mālāṃ śirasyurasi cāparām | adadajjaladhistasyai paṅkajaṃ cātiśobhanam ||

English

The ocean75 gave her a garland of unfading lotuses for her head, another for her chest, and a most beautiful lotus as well.76

82.29

Sanskrit

हिमवान् वाहनं सिंहं रत्नानि विविधानि च । ददावशून्यं सुरया पानपात्रं धनाधिपः ॥

himavān vāhanaṃ siṃhaṃ ratnāni vividhāni ca | dadāvaśūnyaṃ surayā pānapātraṃ dhanādhipaḥ ||

English

Himavān77 gave [her] a lion as her mount, and jewels of many kinds; and the lord of wealth78 gave a drinking-vessel that was never empty of wine.79

82.30

Sanskrit

शेषश्च सर्वनागेशो महामणिविभूषितम् । नागहारं ददौ तस्यै धत्ते यः पृथिवीमिमाम् ॥

śeṣaśca sarvanāgeśo mahāmaṇivibhūṣitam | nāgahāraṃ dadau tasyai dhatte yaḥ pṛthivīmimām ||

English

And Śeṣa80, the lord of all the nāgas, who supports this earth, gave her a serpent-necklace81 adorned with great jewels.82

82.31

Sanskrit

अन्यैरपि सुरैर्देवी भूषणैरायुधैस्तथा । संमानिता ननादोच्चैः साट्टहासं मुहुर्मुहुः ॥

anyairapi surairdevī bhūṣaṇairāyudhaistathā | saṃmānitā nanādoccaiḥ sāṭṭahāsaṃ muhurmuhuḥ ||

English

Honoured in this way by other suras as well, with ornaments and likewise with weapons, the Devī roared loudly, again and again, with a peal of laughter.83

82.32

Sanskrit

तस्या नादेन घोरेण कृत्स्त्रमापूरितं नभः । अमायतातिमहता प्रतिशब्दो महानभूत् ॥

tasyā nādena ghoreṇa kṛtstramāpūritaṃ nabhaḥ | amāyatātimahatā pratiśabdo mahānabhūt ||

English

The whole84 sky was filled with her dreadful roar. [The sky] resounded with exceeding force85, and a mighty echo arose.86†

82.33

Sanskrit

चुक्षुभुः सकला लोकाः समुद्राश्च चकम्पिरे । चचाल वसुधा चेलुः सकलाश्च महीधराः ॥

cukṣubhuḥ sakalā lokāḥ samudrāśca cakampire | cacāla vasudhā celuḥ sakalāśca mahīdharāḥ ||

English

All the worlds were shaken, the oceans trembled, the earth quaked, and all the mountains87 moved.

82.34

Sanskrit

जयेति देवाश्च मुदा तामूचुः सिंहवाहिनीम् । तुष्टुवुर्मुनयश्चैनां भक्तिनम्रात्ममूर्तयः ॥

jayeti devāśca mudā tāmūcuḥ siṃhavāhinīm | tuṣṭuvurmunayaścaināṃ bhaktinamrātmamūrtayaḥ ||

English

"Victory!" said the gods with joy to her, the rider of the lion88; and the sages, their whole persons bowed in devotion89, praised her.90

82.35

Sanskrit

दृष्ट्वा समस्तं संक्षुब्धं त्रैलोक्यममरारयः । सन्नद्धाखिलसैन्यास्ते समुत्तस्थुरुदायुधाः ॥

dṛṣṭvā samastaṃ saṃkṣubdhaṃ trailokyamamarārayaḥ | sannaddhākhilasainyāste samuttasthurudāyudhāḥ ||

English

Seeing the whole of the three worlds91 thrown into turmoil, the enemies of the immortals92, with all their armies fully armed, rose up with weapons raised.93

82.36

Sanskrit

आः किमेतदिति क्रोधादाभाष्य महिषासुरः । अभ्यधावत तं शब्दमशेषैरसुरैर्वृतः ॥

āḥ kimetaditi krodhādābhāṣya mahiṣāsuraḥ | abhyadhāvata taṃ śabdamaśeṣairasurairvṛtaḥ ||

English

"Ah! What is this?94" said Mahiṣāsura in anger, and, surrounded by all the asuras, he ran towards that sound.95

82.37

Sanskrit

स ददर्श ततो देवीं व्याप्तलोकत्रयां त्विषा । पादाक्रान्त्या नतभुवं किरीटोल्लिखिताम्बराम् ॥

sa dadarśa tato devīṃ vyāptalokatrayāṃ tviṣā | pādākrāntyā natabhuvaṃ kirīṭollikhitāmbarām ||

English

82.37–38Then he saw the Devī, who filled the three worlds with her radiance, whose pressing feet made the earth sink96, whose crown scraped the sky, who shook all the nether worlds with the twang of her bowstring97, and who stood filling the directions on every side with her thousand arms.98

82.38

Sanskrit

क्षोभिताशेषपातालां धनुर्ज्यानिः स्वनेन ताम् । दिशो भुजसहस्रेण समन्ताद् व्याप्य संस्थिताम् ॥

kṣobhitāśeṣapātālāṃ dhanurjyāniḥ svanena tām | diśo bhujasahasreṇa samantād vyāpya saṃsthitām ||

English

Its English is given with 82.37–38, above.

82.39

Sanskrit

ततः प्रववृते युद्धं तया देव्या सुरद्विषाम् । शस्त्रास्त्रैर्बहुधा मुक्तैरादीपितदिगन्तरम् ॥

tataḥ pravavṛte yuddhaṃ tayā devyā suradviṣām | śastrāstrairbahudhā muktairādīpitadigantaram ||

English

Then a battle began between that Devī and the enemies of the gods99, with weapons and missiles100 hurled in many ways, a battle that set the quarters of the sky ablaze.101

82.40

Sanskrit

महिषासुरसेनानीश्चिक्षुराख्यो महासुरः । युयुधे चामरश्चान्यैश्चतुरङ्गबलान्वितः ॥

mahiṣāsurasenānīścikṣurākhyo mahāsuraḥ | yuyudhe cāmaraścānyaiścaturaṅgabalānvitaḥ ||

English

The great asura called Cikṣura102, the commander of Mahiṣāsura's army, fought, and so did Cāmara, accompanied by the fourfold army103, together with others.104

82.41

Sanskrit

रथानामयुतैः षड्भिरुदग्राख्यो महासुरः । अयुध्यतायुतानाञ्च सहस्रेण महाहनुः ॥

rathānāmayutaiḥ ṣaḍbhirudagrākhyo mahāsuraḥ | ayudhyatāyutānāñca sahasreṇa mahāhanuḥ ||

English

The great asura called Udagra105 fought with six ayutas106 of chariots, and Mahāhanu107 fought with a thousand ayutas [of chariots].108

82.42

Sanskrit

पञ्चाशद्भिश्च नियुतैरसिलोमा महासुरः । अयुतानां शतैः षड्भिर्वाष्कलो युयुधे रणे ॥

pañcāśadbhiśca niyutairasilomā mahāsuraḥ | ayutānāṃ śataiḥ ṣaḍbhirvāṣkalo yuyudhe raṇe ||

English

The great asura Asiloman fought with fifty niyutas109 [of chariots], and Bāṣkala110 fought in the battle with six hundred ayutas.111

82.43

Sanskrit

गजवाजिसहस्रौघैरनेकैरुग्रदर्शनः । वृतो रथानां कोट्या च युद्धे तस्मिन्नयुध्यत ॥

gajavājisahasraughairanekairugradarśanaḥ | vṛto rathānāṃ koṭyā ca yuddhe tasminnayudhyata ||

English

Ugradarśana112, surrounded by many floods of thousands of elephants and horses, and by a koṭi113 of chariots, fought in that battle.114

82.44

Sanskrit

बिडालाक्षो ऽयुतानाञ्च पञ्चाशद्भिरथायुतैः । युयुधे संयुगे तत्र रथानां परिवारितः ॥

biḍālākṣo 'yutānāñca pañcāśadbhirathāyutaiḥ | yuyudhe saṃyuge tatra rathānāṃ parivāritaḥ ||

English

And Biḍālākṣa115, surrounded by fifty ayutas of chariots116, fought there in that battle.117

82.45

Sanskrit

वृतः कालो रथानाञ्च रणे पञ्चाशतायुतैः । युयुधे संयुगे तत्र तावद्भिः परिवारितः ॥

vṛtaḥ kālo rathānāñca raṇe pañcāśatāyutaiḥ | yuyudhe saṃyuge tatra tāvadbhiḥ parivāritaḥ ||

English

And Kāla118, surrounded in battle by fifty ayutas of chariots, fought there in the encounter, surrounded by as many [chariots].119

82.46

Sanskrit

अन्ये च तत्रायुतशो रथनागहयैर्वृताः । युयुधुः संयुगे देव्या सह तत्र महासुराः ॥

anye ca tatrāyutaśo rathanāgahayairvṛtāḥ | yuyudhuḥ saṃyuge devyā saha tatra mahāsurāḥ ||

English

And other great asuras, in tens of thousands, surrounded by chariots, elephants and horses, fought there in that battle with the Goddess120.121

82.47

Sanskrit

कोटिकोटिसहस्रैस्तु रथानां दन्तिनां तथा । हयानाञ्च वृतो युद्धे तत्राभून्महिषासुरः ॥

koṭikoṭisahasraistu rathānāṃ dantināṃ tathā | hayānāñca vṛto yuddhe tatrābhūnmahiṣāsuraḥ ||

English

And in that battle Mahiṣāsura was there, surrounded by thousands of koṭis of koṭis122 of chariots, of elephants, and of horses.123

82.48

Sanskrit

तोमरैर्भिन्दिपालैश्च शक्तिभिर्मुसलैस्तथा । युयुधुः संयुगे देव्या खड्गैः परशुपट्टिशैः ॥

tomarairbhindipālaiśca śaktibhirmusalaistathā | yuyudhuḥ saṃyuge devyā khaḍgaiḥ paraśupaṭṭiśaiḥ ||

English

With lances, bhindipālas124, spears125, and likewise with clubs, with swords, axes and paṭṭiśas126, they fought in the battle against the Goddess.127

82.49

Sanskrit

केचिच्च चिक्षिपुः शक्तीः केचित् पाशांस्तथापरे । देवीं खड्गप्रहारैस्तु ते तां हन्तुं प्रचक्रमुः ॥

kecicca cikṣipuḥ śaktīḥ kecit pāśāṃstathāpare | devīṃ khaḍgaprahāraistu te tāṃ hantuṃ pracakramuḥ ||

English

Some hurled spears128, some nooses129, and others likewise [hurled]; but they set out to kill her, the Goddess, with blows of swords.130

82.50

Sanskrit

सापि देवी ततस्तानि शस्त्राण्यस्त्राणि चण्डिका । लीलयैव प्रचिच्छेद निजशस्त्रास्त्रवर्षिणी ॥

sāpi devī tatastāni śastrāṇyastrāṇi caṇḍikā | līlayaiva praciccheda nijaśastrāstravarṣiṇī ||

English

Then she too, the Goddess Caṇḍikā131, raining down her own weapons and missiles, cut to pieces those weapons and missiles with mere sport132.133

82.51

Sanskrit

अनायस्तानना देवी स्तूयमाना सुरर्षिभिः । मुमोचासुरदेहेषु शस्त्राण्यस्त्राणि चेश्वरी ॥

anāyastānanā devī stūyamānā surarṣibhiḥ | mumocāsuradeheṣu śastrāṇyastrāṇi ceśvarī ||

English

With an untroubled face, the Goddess, praised by the divine sages134, released weapons and missiles upon the bodies of the asuras135—she, the sovereign lady136.137

82.52

Sanskrit

सो ऽपि क्रुद्धो धुतसटो देव्या वाहनकेशरी । चचारासुरसैन्येषु वनेष्विव हुताशनः ॥

so 'pi kruddho dhutasaṭo devyā vāhanakeśarī | cacārāsurasainyeṣu vaneṣviva hutāśanaḥ ||

English

He too, the lion that was the Goddess's mount138, enraged and shaking his mane, ranged through the armies of the asuras like fire139 through forests.140

82.53

Sanskrit

निश्वसान्मुमुचे यांश्च युध्यमाना रणे ऽम्बिका । त एव सद्यः सम्भूता गणाः शतसहस्रशः ॥

niśvasānmumuce yāṃśca yudhyamānā raṇe 'mbikā | ta eva sadyaḥ sambhūtā gaṇāḥ śatasahasraśaḥ ||

English

The breaths that Ambikā141 released as she fought in the battle, those very [breaths] at once became gaṇas142, in hundreds and thousands.143

82.54

Sanskrit

युयुधुस्ते परशुभिर्भिन्दिपालासिपट्टिशैः । नाशयन्तो ऽसुरगणान् देवीशक्त्युपबृंहिताः ॥

yuyudhuste paraśubhirbhindipālāsipaṭṭiśaiḥ | nāśayanto 'suragaṇān devīśaktyupabṛṃhitāḥ ||

English

They fought with axes, bhindipālas, swords and paṭṭiśas, destroying the hosts of the asuras, strengthened by the Goddess's power144.145

82.55

Sanskrit

अवादयन्त पटहान् गणाः शङ्खांस्तथापरे । मृदङ्गांश्च तथैवान्ये तस्मिन् युद्धमहोत्सवे ॥

avādayanta paṭahān gaṇāḥ śaṅkhāṃstathāpare | mṛdaṅgāṃśca tathaivānye tasmin yuddhamahotsave ||

English

In that great festival of battle, some of the gaṇas146 sounded drums147, others likewise sounded conches, and still others in the same way sounded mṛdaṅgas148.

82.56

Sanskrit

ततो देवी त्रिशूलेन गदया शक्तिवृष्टिभिः । शड्गादिभिश्च शतशो निजघान महासुरान् ॥

tato devī triśūlena gadayā śaktivṛṣṭibhiḥ | śaḍgādibhiśca śataśo nijaghāna mahāsurān ||

English

Then the Goddess slew the great asuras by hundreds with her trident, her mace, showers of śaktis149, and swords150 and [other weapons] of that kind.151

82.57

Sanskrit

पातयामास चैवान्यान् घण्टास्वनविमोहितान् । असुरान् भुवि पाशेन बद्ध्वा चान्यानकर्षयत् ॥

pātayāmāsa caivānyān ghaṇṭāsvanavimohitān | asurān bhuvi pāśena baddhvā cānyānakarṣayat ||

English

She also brought down other asuras, bewildered by the sound of her bell; and having bound still others with a noose152 on the ground, she dragged them along.153

82.58

Sanskrit

केचिद् द्विधा कृतास्तीक्ष्णैः खड्गपातैस्तथापरे । विपोथिता निपातेन गदया भुवि शेरते ॥

kecid dvidhā kṛtāstīkṣṇaiḥ khaḍgapātaistathāpare | vipothitā nipātena gadayā bhuvi śerate ||

English

Some were cut in two by sharp sword-strokes, and others, battered by the fall of the mace, lie on the ground154.155

82.59

Sanskrit

वेमुश्च केचिद्रुधिरं मुसले भृशं हताः । केचिन्नपातिता भूमौ भिन्नाः शूलेन वक्षसि ॥

vemuśca kecidrudhiraṃ musale bhṛśaṃ hatāḥ | kecinnapātitā bhūmau bhinnāḥ śūlena vakṣasi ||

English

Some, struck hard with the club156, vomited blood; others, pierced in the chest by the spear, were felled to the ground157.158†

82.60

Sanskrit

निरन्तराः शरौघेण कृताः केचिद्रणाजिरे । शैलानुकारिणः प्राणान् मुमुचुस्त्रिदशार्दनाः ॥

nirantarāḥ śaraugheṇa kṛtāḥ kecidraṇājire | śailānukāriṇaḥ prāṇān mumucustridaśārdanāḥ ||

English

Some, tormentors of the devas159, were pierced all over by a flood of arrows on the battlefield, so that no gap was left in them; resembling mountains160, they gave up their prāṇas.161

82.61

Sanskrit

केषाञ्चिद्वाहवश्छिन्नाश्छिन्नग्रीवास्तथापरे । शिरांसि पेतुरन्येषामन्ये मध्ये विदारिताः ॥

keṣāñcidvāhavaśchinnāśchinnagrīvāstathāpare | śirāṃsi peturanyeṣāmanye madhye vidāritāḥ ||

English

The arms of some were cut off, and others had their necks cut through; the heads of some fell, and others were split open down the middle.162

82.62

Sanskrit

विच्छिन्नजङ्घास्त्वपरे पेतुरुर्व्यां महासुराः । एकबाह्वक्षिचरणाः केचिद्देव्या द्विधा कृताः ॥

vicchinnajaṅghāstvapare petururvyāṃ mahāsurāḥ | ekabāhvakṣicaraṇāḥ keciddevyā dvidhā kṛtāḥ ||

English

Other great asuras, their lower legs163 severed, fell on the earth; and some were cut in two by the Goddess, [each half] with one arm, one eye and one foot164.165

82.63

Sanskrit

छिन्ने ऽपि चान्ये शिरसि पतिताः पुनरुत्थिताः । कबन्धा युयुधुर्देव्या गृहीतपरमायुधाः ॥

chinne 'pi cānye śirasi patitāḥ punarutthitāḥ | kabandhā yuyudhurdevyā gṛhītaparamāyudhāḥ ||

English

And others, though their heads were cut off, fell and rose again; as headless trunks (kabandhas)166 they fought the Goddess, having taken up excellent weapons.167

82.64

Sanskrit

ननृतुश्चापरे तत्र युद्धे तूर्यलयाश्रिताः । कबन्धाश्छिन्नखिरसः खड्गशक्त्यृष्टिपाणयः ॥

nanṛtuścāpare tatra yuddhe tūryalayāśritāḥ | kabandhāśchinnakhirasaḥ khaḍgaśaktyṛṣṭipāṇayaḥ ||

English

And other kabandhas, with their heads severed, danced there in that battle, keeping to the beat of the musical instruments168, with a sword, a javelin169 and a lance in their hands.170

82.65

Sanskrit

तिष्ठ तिष्ठेति भाषन्तो देवीमन्ये महासुराः । रुधिरौघविलुप्ताङ्गाः संग्रामे लोमहर्षणे ॥

tiṣṭha tiṣṭheti bhāṣanto devīmanye mahāsurāḥ | rudhiraughaviluptāṅgāḥ saṃgrāme lomaharṣaṇe ||

English

Other great asuras, their limbs smeared all over with floods of blood, said to the Goddess, "Stand! Stand!" in that battle which made the hair stand on end171.172

82.66

Sanskrit

पातितै रथनागाश्वैरसुरैश्च वसुन्धरा । अगम्या साभवत् तत्र यत्राभूत् स महारणः ॥

pātitai rathanāgāśvairasuraiśca vasundharā | agamyā sābhavat tatra yatrābhūt sa mahāraṇaḥ ||

English

The earth became impassable there, where that great battle took place, because of the chariots, elephants, horses and asuras that had been struck down.173

82.67

Sanskrit

शोणितौघा महानद्यः सद्यस्तत्र विसुस्त्रुवुः । मध्ये चासुरसैन्यस्य वारणासुरवाजिनाम् ॥

śoṇitaughā mahānadyaḥ sadyastatra visustruvuḥ | madhye cāsurasainyasya vāraṇāsuravājinām ||

English

There, in the midst of the army of the asuras, among the elephants, asuras and horses, great rivers of floods of blood174 at once streamed forth.175†

82.68

Sanskrit

क्षणेन तन्महासैन्यमसुराणां तथाम्बिका । निन्ये क्षयं यथा वह्निस्तृणदारुमहाचयम् ॥

kṣaṇena tanmahāsainyamasurāṇāṃ tathāmbikā | ninye kṣayaṃ yathā vahnistṛṇadārumahācayam ||

English

In a moment Ambikā176 brought that great army of the asuras to destruction, as a fire [brings] a great heap of grass and wood [to destruction].177

82.69

Sanskrit

स च सिंहो महानादमुत्सृजन् धुतकेसरः । शरीरेभ्यो ऽमरारीणामसूनिव विचिन्वति ॥

sa ca siṃho mahānādamutsṛjan dhutakesaraḥ | śarīrebhyo 'marārīṇāmasūniva vicinvati ||

English

And that lion, letting out a great roar and shaking his mane, searched out the life-breaths178 from the bodies of the enemies of the immortals179, as it were.180

82.70

Sanskrit

देव्या गणैश्च तैस्तत्र कृतं युद्धं तथासुरैः । यथैषां तुतुषुर्देवाः पुष्पवृष्टिमुचो दिवि ॥

devyā gaṇaiśca taistatra kṛtaṃ yuddhaṃ tathāsuraiḥ | yathaiṣāṃ tutuṣurdevāḥ puṣpavṛṣṭimuco divi ||

English

In that place the Goddess and those gaṇas of hers fought the battle against the asuras in such a way that the gods181 in heaven, showering down flowers182, were delighted with them.183

Sanskrit

इति श्रीमार्कण्डेयमहापुराणे सावर्णिके मन्वन्तरे देवीमाहात्म्ये महिषासुरसैन्यवधो नाम द्व्यशीतितमो ऽध्यायः

iti śrīmārkaṇḍeyamahāpurāṇe sāvarṇike manvantare devīmāhātmye mahiṣāsurasainyavadho nāma dvyaśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ

English

Thus ends the eighty-second chapter184, named "The Slaughter of the Army of Mahiṣāsura," in the Devīmāhātmya, in the Sāvarṇika manvantara, in the revered Mahāpurāṇa185 of Mārkaṇḍeya.186

  1. The reading: The e-text numbers the chapter 82, while the heading of the preceding colophon (verse 654.1) numbers the Madhu–Kaiṭabha chapter 81, so the numbering is consistent. The line is a chapter heading, not a verse of narrative; I render it as a heading and add nothing. ↩
  2. devas: deva: a god, one of the celestial beings who are the traditional enemies of the asuras. ↩
  3. asuras: asura: a class of powerful beings opposed to the devas; here Mahiṣa leads them. ↩
  4. Mahiṣa: The buffalo-demon, called Mahiṣāsura in verse 82.2; his name means 'buffalo'. ↩
  5. The reading: The text has 'devāsuram' as a neuter compound, 'the deva-asura war', and 'yuddham' stands in apposition to it. I translated this as 'a war between the devas and the asuras'. 'Pūrṇam abdaśatam' is an accusative of duration, 'a full hundred years'. The locatives 'mahiṣe' and 'purandare' form locative absolutes ('when Mahiṣa was ... and Purandara was ...'), which I rendered with 'when'. ↩
  6. became Indra: Mahiṣa took the place of Indra as king of the devas; the Sanskrit says indro 'bhūt, 'became Indra', that is, assumed Indra's sovereignty. ↩
  7. the lotus-born Prajāpati: Padmayoni, 'lotus-born', and Prajāpati are two epithets of Brahmā, who arose from the lotus growing from Viṣṇu's navel. ↩
  8. Īśa: 'The Lord'; here Śiva. ↩
  9. the Garuḍa-bannered one: Garuḍadhvaja, Viṣṇu, whose banner bears the eagle Garuḍa. Īśagaruḍadhvajau is a dual dvandva: 'Īśa and Garuḍadhvaja'. ↩
  10. the devas told: The text has tridaśāḥ, 'the thirty', a common name for the devas, from the traditional thirty-three. ↩
  11. The reading: Īśa is taken as Śiva and Garuḍadhvaja as Viṣṇu, the usual identification; the dual compound makes them two persons. I have not put 'Brahmā' into the translation, as the Sanskrit gives only the epithets padmayoni and prajāpati; the note identifies him. 'tayoḥ' is a genitive dual and is read as standing for the person addressed, 'the two of them', with kathayāmāsuḥ. The alternative of reading it with vṛttam, 'what happened to the two', does not suit the sense, since the events concern the devas. 'tridaśāḥ' is rendered 'the devas' because it is the same body that went in 82.3. ↩
  12. presides over: adhitiṣṭhiti is an irregular present of adhi-√sthā, the regular form being adhitiṣṭhati. It is a Purāṇic usage that the metre may have favoured. ↩
  13. Anila: Anila is the wind god, also called Vāyu. ↩
  14. Indu: Indu, 'the bright drop', is the moon god, also called Candra. ↩
  15. The reading: The compound sūryendragnyanilendūnām is a dvandva that splits as sūrya, indra, agni, anila, indu. The sandhi indra + agni gives 'ndragni' here, written as one word. Indra is read as a separate deity, not a part of another compound member. The verb adhitiṣṭhiti is irregular (for adhitiṣṭhati), but its meaning, 'presides over' or 'occupies', is clear. ↩
  16. Mahiṣa: The Sanskrit has the instrumental mahiṣeṇa in apposition to tena ('by him'): the same Mahiṣa as in the preceding verses, with durātmanā ('wicked-souled') as his epithet. ↩
  17. the enemy of the immortals: Amarāri, 'foe of the amara'; here Mahiṣa, the buffalo-demon. ↩
  18. We have come to you for refuge: śaraṇa, 'refuge, shelter'; to 'go to refuge' is the standard idiom of a suppliant seeking protection. ↩
  19. Madhusūdana: 'Slayer of Madhu', an epithet of Viṣṇu, who killed the asura Madhu. ↩
  20. Śambhu: 'the Benevolent', an epithet of Śiva, here the Īśa of verse 82.3. ↩
  21. The reading: The text has no explicit 'their' for bhrukuṭīkuṭilānanau; it is a dual bahuvrīhi agreeing with madhusūdanaḥ and śambhuḥ, 'having faces made crooked by frowns'. Strictly, the singular verb cakāra goes with madhusūdanaḥ, and śambhuś ca is added to it; I read the dual adjective as qualifying both gods. ↩
  22. discus-bearer: Cakrin, Viṣṇu, who bears the discus (cakra); the same god as Madhusūdana in the previous verse. ↩
  23. radiance: tejas: radiant energy, fiery power. Here the combined radiance of the gods, which will take shape as the goddess (see the following verses). ↩
  24. Śaṅkara: 'Beneficent'; a name of Śiva, the same god as Śambhu in the previous verse. ↩
  25. The reading: The Sanskrit has no word for 'faces' with Brahmā and Śaṅkara; vadanāt governs them by implication, so I supplied '[the faces]' in brackets. Another reading would make the radiance 'of Brahmā and Śaṅkara' (genitive) a separate source, but the verse is most naturally read as the radiance issuing from all three. Also, 'tato … tataḥ' is a doubled particle; rendered once as 'Then'. ↩
  26. The reading: śarīrataḥ is ablative of source ('from the bodies'). anyeṣām ... devānām śakrādīnām is genitive, construed with śarīrataḥ. taccaikyam samagacchata: 'that [tejas] came into unity'; samagacchata is an ātmanepada (ārṣa) imperfect, 3rd singular, with the neuter tejas as subject. The reading is not in doubt. ↩
  27. suras: sura: a god, synonym of deva; the word is used here for the same gods who were called devas in the preceding verses. ↩
  28. mass of radiance: kūṭa means a heap, peak or mass. It is chosen to match the simile of the mountain (parvata) that follows. ↩
  29. three worlds: lokatraya: heaven, the atmosphere (or earth), and the netherworld; that is, the whole universe. Here the radiance fills them. ↩
  30. The reading: I accept the reviewer's grammatical corrections. vyāptalokatrayam is neuter singular, so it agrees with tat tejaḥ and not with the feminine nārī. It is a bahuvrīhi, 'by which the three worlds were pervaded', and tviṣā is the instrumental 'by its glow'. My earlier doubt wrongly assumed a feminine form. ekastham is likewise a neuter adjective of tejas, and abhūt is the only verb; the neuter tat is the subject and the feminine nārī its predicate. I have therefore dropped the extra 'stood' and 'and' of the earlier draft and rendered ekastham as 'gathered in one place'. No finding was rejected. ↩
  31. Yama's: yāmyena is the adjective formed from Yama. It has no noun of its own, and tejasā is understood from viṣṇutejasā in the same line. ↩
  32. her arms: The e-text prints bahavo ('many'). I read bāhavaḥ ('arms'), as in the parallel verse of the Devīmāhātmya. Verses 82.14–15 assign no source to the arms, which supports this reading. See doubts. ↩
  33. The reading: The e-text reads keśā bahavo viṣṇutejasā. Read as printed, keśāḥ is the subject and bahavaḥ means 'many', so the hair would come from both Yama's and Viṣṇu's energy. The ca stands only after the first instrumental, which strains that construction. The arms are also left without a source, although verses 82.14–15 give every other limb one. I therefore take bahavo as a misprint, with the long ā lost, for bāhavaḥ, 'arms'. This is the reading of the parallel Devīmāhātmya verse, and the metre is the same either way. The construction is then: hair by Yama's energy, arms by Viṣṇu's. This is an emendation of the printed text, and I have flagged it in the note. The printed reading would give 'her many hairs' from both sources. The reviewer's two findings are accepted, and I have also replaced 'radiance' with the glossary's 'fiery energy' for tejas. ↩
  34. Soma: saumya is the adjective of Soma, the moon-god; the fiery energy is that of Soma (the moon). ↩
  35. Bhū: bhuvaḥ is the genitive of bhū, the earth, here personified as a deity. ↩
  36. The reading: saumya may mean 'of Soma' (the moon) or simply 'gentle, cool'. I chose 'of Soma' because the parallel adjectives aindra and vāruṇa name deities. The word tejasā is read with all the instrumentals in the verse, as in 82.13; only the last has it expressed. The addition 'Bhū [Earth]' in the translation is bracketed for clarity, but 'Earth' is just the plain sense of bhuvaḥ. ↩
  37. the sun: arkatejasā, 'by the radiance of Arka'. Arka, 'the radiant one', is a name of the sun; it is read here as the sun-god Sūrya. ↩
  38. the Vasus: A class of eight devas, attendants of Indra, associated with natural elements such as fire, earth and sky. ↩
  39. Kubera: kaubereṇa, 'by that of Kubera', the lord of wealth and guardian of the northern quarter. ↩
  40. The reading: tadaṅgulyor'katejasā: the printed text appears to be tadaṅgulyo 'rkatejasā, with the sandhi garbled. I read tadaṅgulyaḥ (nominative plural, 'her toes', matching karāṅgulyaḥ in the same verse) and arkatejasā. Verse 82.15 itself is therefore translated with 'her toes' for tadaṅgulyaḥ. Reading it as a dual, 'the toes of the two', is also possible but gives the same sense. Arka could in principle denote another figure, but it is understood here as the sun. ↩
  41. Prajāpati: The text has the adjective prājāpatya, 'belonging to Prajāpati', the lord of creatures; here it refers to the creator deity, often identified with Brahmā. ↩
  42. Pāvaka: 'The purifier', a name of Agni, the god of fire. ↩
  43. The reading: tu in tasyāstu is a mere connective and is left untranslated. nayanatritayam is rendered 'triad of her eyes', i.e. three eyes. The word tejasā is rendered 'radiance', in line with the preceding verses, as the glossary gives tejas as 'fiery energy' but the draft English uses 'radiance'. ↩
  44. the two twilights: sandhyayoḥ is a dual: the two junctures of dawn and dusk. Here they are the powers that give their radiance, not the evening worship of the glossary term. ↩
  45. the auspicious one: śivā, feminine of śiva 'auspicious'. It refers to the goddess formed from the gathered radiances and is also a name of Devī. ↩
  46. The reading: sambhavaḥ is masculine and śivā feminine, so they do not agree. I took śivā as the subject (the goddess) and sambhavaḥ ... tejasām as the predicate 'origin from the radiances', with 'of the other devas' qualifying tejasām. The other possible reading, 'the origin of the other devas' radiances was auspicious', makes poor sense. The verse has no verb for the eyebrows and ears, so 'came' is supplied from the preceding verses. ↩
  47. afflicted by Mahiṣa: mahiṣārditāḥ is read as a tatpuruṣa, 'tormented by Mahiṣa', the buffalo-demon, and not as 'tormented by a buffalo'. The compound qualifies amarāḥ. ↩
  48. The reading: mahiṣārditāḥ: 'afflicted by Mahiṣa' (the demon, as the story requires) or 'by a buffalo'. I chose the proper name, in line with the glossary. The text has no iti or other word that would suggest a different reading. ↩
  49. the victorious one: Sanskrit jayantīm, a present participle of ji, 'to conquer'. It qualifies the goddess and plays on the cry jaya; the devas who desire victory (jayaiṣiṇaḥ) hail her as already conquering. ↩
  50. The bearer of the Pināka: Pinākadhṛk, 'bearer of the Pināka', is Śiva. The Pināka is his bow, though some sources call it his trident. ↩
  51. Kṛṣṇa: Here a name of Viṣṇu, whose characteristic weapon is the discus (cakra). ↩
  52. The reading: Only the first finding called for a change: I now render the epithet as 'the bearer of the Pināka' and the note names Śiva. The Sanskrit has no 'own' with the first spear (śūlāt), so I wrote the implied 'his' in brackets. The other two findings required no change: tasyai is rendered as 'to her', and śūla as 'spear' per the glossary. ↩
  53. Hutāśana: 'Eater of oblations', a name of Agni, the fire god, called Pāvaka earlier in this hymn. ↩
  54. spear: The word is śakti, a pointed missile weapon, here a different weapon from the śūla of the previous verse. ↩
  55. Māruta: 'Of the Maruts', a name of Vāyu, the wind god, called Anila earlier in this hymn. ↩
  56. The reading: The glossary renders Śakti as 'power', but here śaktim is a weapon given by Agni. I took it as the weapon, rendered 'spear', and wrote śūla in the earlier verse as 'spear' too, so the translation may need a distinction such as 'lance' for śakti. Hutāśana is Agni, called Pāvaka in 82.16. Māruta is the wind god, called Anila in 82.17. ↩
  57. the thousand-eyed one: Translates sahasrākṣa, an epithet of Indra. ↩
  58. drew a thunderbolt out of his own thunderbolt: As with Śiva's spear and Viṣṇu's discus in the preceding verses, the god creates a second weapon from his own, so that he does not lose it. Here vajra and kuliśa both mean 'thunderbolt'. ↩
  59. Airāvata: The great white elephant who is Indra's mount. ↩
  60. the lord of the waters: ambupati, an epithet of Varuṇa, who is named in 82.21. ↩
  61. noose: The e-text reads māśaṃ ('bean'), which makes no sense among weapons. Emended to pāśaṃ, 'noose', the weapon of Varuṇa. ↩
  62. Prajāpati: Here named alongside Brahmā as a separate giver, since each gives his own gift. Commentators take him as Dakṣa or the lord of creatures in general; elsewhere the title belongs to Brahmā himself. ↩
  63. The reading: 1. māśaṃ (e-text, 'bean') is emended to pāśaṃ ('noose'), a one-letter misprint; the noose is Varuṇa's weapon, and the verse lists weapons and implements. 2. The verse has only two dadau: one serves Yama and the lord of the waters, the other serves Prajāpati and Brahmā. Prajāpatiś ca ... brahmā could be read as one person (Prajāpati Brahmā giving both gifts) or as two. I follow the reviewer and take two givers. The accusatives akṣamālāṃ and kamaṇḍalum are not joined by ca, and each god in this passage gives a single gift. The ca after prajāpatiḥ joins him to the preceding givers. This differs from the glossary's gloss of Prajāpati as a title of Brahmā; here the two are distinct. ↩
  64. Divākara: 'Maker of the day', a name of the Sun god Sūrya. ↩
  65. pores: Sanskrit romakūpa, literally 'hair-pit', the pore of the skin from which a body-hair grows. ↩
  66. Kāla: Time personified, the power that brings all things to their end. Yama's staff in 82.23 was drawn from his staff. ↩
  67. The reading: The printed 'kālakhca' is a misprint. I read 'kālaś ca' ('and Kāla'), which the metre and sense require. 'Kāla' could also be read as the common noun 'time', but as a giver parallel to Divākara it is a personified god. 'Carma' means hide or leather and so a shield; I translate it as 'shield', as it is paired with the sword. 'Tasyāḥ' is a genitive of the recipient, 'to her', and I render it as 'her'. 'Dattavān' is stated once and serves both clauses. ↩
  68. Kṣīroda: 'The Ocean of Milk', the milk-ocean of Purāṇic cosmology, here acting as a divine giver. The verb 'gave' is understood from 82.24. ↩
  69. half-moon ornament: ardhacandra: a crescent-shaped ornament. Whether it is worn in the hair or on the body is not stated. ↩
  70. neck-ornament: graiveyaka: an ornament for the neck, distinct from the hāra, the 'necklace' of the first line. ↩
  71. The reading: ardhacandra could be an ornament (crescent-shaped jewel) or a crescent-shaped hair or body decoration. I took it as a crescent ornament, without specifying where it is worn. The dual ajare ambare is rendered 'two undecaying garments'; the ambare could also be read as a pair of cloths, upper and lower, and the translation allows both. ↩
  72. Viśvakarman: The divine craftsman of the gods, the architect and maker of their weapons and ornaments. ↩
  73. impenetrable armour: Sanskrit daṃśana means 'armour, coat of mail' (literally 'that which bites or protects'); the neuter adjective abhedya, 'that cannot be pierced', qualifies it. ↩
  74. The reading: The text has viśvakarmā (nominative of Viśvakarman); I give the stem. Daṃśana: 'armour' is chosen over 'biting' or other senses, since it is a gift for battle and abhedya 'unpierceable' fits armour. The e-text's paraśuñcātinirmalam stands for paraśuṃ cātinirmalam. ↩
  75. The ocean: Jaladhi, 'the holder of waters', is a name of the ocean, here personified as a divine giver. ↩
  76. The reading: The verse does not say which garland is for which part: śirasi ('on the head') and urasi ('on the chest') could in principle qualify the garlands as to where they are worn, or the gift. I took 'for her head' and 'for her chest' as where the two garlands are worn. In the first pāda amlānapaṅkajām is an adjective of mālām ('with unfading lotuses'), and in the second pāda aparām ('another') stands for a second garland of the same kind. ↩
  77. Himavān: The Himalaya mountain, personified as a god. ↩
  78. the lord of wealth: Dhanādhipa, a title of Kubera, the god of wealth; the text does not name him. ↩
  79. The reading: surayā is the instrumental of surā ('wine'), not sura ('god'). It is read with aśūnyam as 'not empty of wine', that is, filled with wine. The final compound pānapātram is taken as the object of dadau, as the verse requires. I take dhanādhipaḥ to be Kubera, though the text does not name him. ↩
  80. Śeṣa: The cosmic serpent, who bears the earth on his heads and is Viṣṇu's couch. ↩
  81. serpent-necklace: Sanskrit nāgahāra; read here as a necklace in the form of a serpent, or one made of serpents. The compound allows either. ↩
  82. The reading: nāgahāra can mean 'necklace in the form of a nāga' or 'necklace made of nāgas'; I kept the neutral 'serpent-necklace'. ↩
  83. The reading: saṃmānitā is a feminine participle agreeing with devī: 'honoured'. The instrumentals bhūṣaṇair āyudhaiḥ are read as the means of honouring (with ornaments and weapons), not as a separate group. The Devī is the goddess formed from the gods' energies, who is receiving gifts from them. ↩
  84. whole: The e-text reads kṛtstram, which is not a normal form. Kṛtsnam, 'whole', is the expected word. The e-text may be damaged here as well as at amāyata. ↩
  85. resounded with exceeding force: The text reads amāyatātimahatā. I divide it amāyata atimahatā and take amāyata as the imperfect middle of √mā, 'to bellow, resound'. The subject is probably the sky (nabhaḥ). Atimahatā is an instrumental used adverbially, or it agrees with an unexpressed 'sound'. This is the simplest reading, but the word division and the root are not certain. Some interpreters take the word as a-māyatā, 'immeasurable', or as āyatā, 'extended'. ↩
  86. The reading: amāyatātimahatā. I accept the reviewer's main point: the draft lost the finite verb. (a) amāyata atimahatā, with amāyata as imperfect middle of √mā 'to bellow, resound', is the simplest reading and I now adopt it. The sky is the likely subject, so I supply '[the sky]' in brackets. (b) Taking amāyatā as an adjective does not work, because a feminine instrumental in -ā cannot agree with masculine nāda. (c) The emendations āyatātimahatā and ayāmātimahatā leave the same problem. I have dropped the crux. 'Exceedingly great [roar]' rested on a conjectural agreement with nādena, so I render atimahatā adverbially as 'with exceeding force'. The sense is close either way. The e-text's kṛtstram for kṛtsnam is probably a misprint, and I translate it as 'whole'. ↩
  87. mountains: Sanskrit mahīdhara, literally 'earth-bearer'; a standard word for mountain. ↩
  88. the rider of the lion: Siṃhavāhinī, 'she who has the lion as her mount', refers to the Devī, whose mount the lion was given by Himavān in verse 29. ↩
  89. their whole persons bowed in devotion: The compound bhaktinamrātmamūrtayaḥ is a bahuvrīhi qualifying the sages: their selves and bodies were bowed down through bhakti. ↩
  90. The reading: The compound ātmamūrti may be read as 'mind and body' (ātman as self or mind, mūrti as body) or as 'their own forms'. I took it as 'whole persons': the self and the body together bowed in devotion. 'Jaya' with 'iti' is rendered as a quoted cry, 'Victory!'; it could also be 'Be victorious'. ↩
  91. the three worlds: trailokya: heaven, earth and the netherworld (or atmosphere). ↩
  92. the enemies of the immortals: amarāri, here in the plural: the asuras, the followers of Mahiṣa, who are the foes of the gods. ↩
  93. The reading: amarārayaḥ is plural and could be read as 'the enemies of the immortals' in general; the glossary's singular sense 'Mahiṣa' does not fit here, since the plural verb samuttasthuḥ and 'all their armies' point to the asura host (with Mahiṣa among them). Chosen: the asuras collectively. ↩
  94. Ah! What is this?: The words āḥ kim etad iti are his spoken exclamation; ābhāṣya, 'having addressed' or 'having spoken', is rendered 'said'. ↩
  95. The reading: ābhāṣya normally means 'having addressed'; no addressee is named, so I take it as 'having exclaimed' and render it 'said'. Taṃ śabdam is the accusative object of abhyadhāvata ('ran towards'), the sound of the Devī's roar. ↩
  96. whose pressing feet made the earth sink: pādākrāntyā natabhuvam: literally 'having the earth bowed down by the pressure of her feet'; a bahuvrīhi qualifying the Devī. ↩
  97. the twang of her bowstring: The text reads dhanurjyāniḥsvanena; the sense is 'by the sound of the bowstring', and jyā-niḥsvana is the expected compound. ↩
  98. The reading: dhanurjyāniḥ svanena in the e-text is probably a misprint or sandhi-split for dhanurjyā-niḥsvanena ('by the twang of the bowstring'), which I have followed. Taking niḥ as a separate word gives no sense. ↩
  99. the enemies of the gods: Suradviṣ, 'hater of the gods', here means the asuras, Mahiṣa's host. ↩
  100. weapons and missiles: śastra is a weapon held in the hand, such as a sword or club; astra is a hurled or mantra-empowered missile. ↩
  101. The reading: ādīpitadigantaram is neuter singular and agrees with yuddham, so I render it as a relative clause on the battle ('a battle that set the quarters of the sky ablaze'), with the weapons and missiles as the means by which it was hurled. Digantara can also mean 'the intervals between the directions' or 'the ends of the directions'; I take it in the general sense of the quarters of the sky. For tayā I keep 'that Devī', as the reviewer suggests. The reviewer also asks for 'weapons and missiles' consistently and 'in many ways' with muktaiḥ; both are done. ↩
  102. Cikṣura: One of the chief generals of Mahiṣa. The name is given here as 'called Cikṣura' (cikṣurākhya). ↩
  103. fourfold army: caturaṅgabala: the traditional four divisions of an army, namely elephants, chariots, cavalry and infantry. ↩
  104. The reading: The syntax is loose. 'anyaiḥ' (instrumental plural, 'with others') has no explicit noun. It most naturally goes with yuyudhe, 'fought with others', meaning other opponents or in company with other asuras. I have rendered it 'together with others'. 'Accompanied by the fourfold army' (caturaṅgabalānvitaḥ) could qualify Cāmara alone or both warriors. I took it with Cāmara, as the nearest noun in the compound-bearing clause, though the sense may cover Cikṣura too. ↩
  105. Udagra: A commander of Mahiṣa's army. His name means 'towering' or 'fierce'. ↩
  106. ayutas: ayuta is a large number, usually ten thousand. Six ayutas are therefore sixty thousand chariots, and a thousand ayutas are ten million. ↩
  107. Mahāhanu: A commander of Mahiṣa's army. His name means 'big-jawed'. ↩
  108. The reading: In ayutānāṃ ca sahasreṇa the noun counted is not stated. I take it to be chariots (rathānām) carried over from the first half, and I mark this with square brackets. Another possible reading is an army of mixed forces, but the construction favours chariots. ↩
  109. niyutas: niyuta is a large number, conventionally one hundred thousand (some reckonings differ). The text gives no count word of its own for the unit. ↩
  110. Bāṣkala: Bāṣkala is an asura commander in Mahiṣa's host. He is named here without epithet. ↩
  111. The reading: The verse does not say what the numbers count. Following the sense of 82.41 (rathānām), I supply 'of chariots' in brackets for the niyutas; for Bāṣkala the ayutas are likewise understood as chariots, though the text leaves it implied. The brackets are not placed on the second number in the translation, because the clause is parallel and no extra word is needed for English to parse. Asilomā is a nominative singular of the stem Asiloman, the name being the asura's. ↩
  112. Ugradarśana: An asura commander. The word means 'of fearsome aspect', but here it is taken as his name, in a list of Mahiṣa's generals. ↩
  113. koṭi: A very large number, conventionally ten million. ↩
  114. The reading: ugradarśanaḥ can be an adjective, 'of fearsome appearance', qualifying an unnamed subject, or a proper name. I took it as a name, Ugradarśana, because the verses around it list asura commanders by name and this verse has no other subject. ↩
  115. Biḍālākṣa: An asura commander on Mahiṣa's side. The name means 'cat-eyed'. ↩
  116. fifty ayutas of chariots: The text reads ayutānāṃ ca pañcāśadbhir athāyutaiḥ. Ayutānām and ayutaiḥ both appear, so the phrase seems doubled, and atha is probably a metrical filler. The sense is taken as 'fifty ayutas'. The genitive rathānām qualifies the whole number phrase. ↩
  117. The reading: The phrase ayutānāṃ ca pañcāśadbhir athāyutaiḥ looks faulty or redundant. Readings: (a) ayutānām pañcāśadbhiḥ + atha + ayutaiḥ, with the second ayuta a duplicate or filler; (b) the multiplicative 'fifty ayutas of ayutas'; (c) emending to pañcāśadbhiś cāyutaiḥ. I chose 'fifty ayutas of chariots'. The preceding verses (82.41–42) use the same pattern, a number joined with ayutānām, to give a plain count of ayutas, and the text gives no second quantity. The draft's 'again [further] ayutas' invented an unquantified second host and is dropped. I leave atha unrendered as a filler, and ca is absorbed in the opening 'And' that links Biḍālākṣa to the preceding list. Rathānām is read with both the number phrase and parivāritaḥ: 'surrounded by ... of chariots'. ↩
  118. Kāla: Here the name of an asura commander in Mahiṣa's army, not the common noun kāla, 'time' or 'death'. ↩
  119. The reading: kālaḥ could be the common noun 'Time/Death' or a proper name. I took it as the name of an asura because it stands among the asura commanders listed in 82.40–44 and 82.46. tāvadbhiḥ ('by as many') has no noun of its own. The context points to the chariots just mentioned, so I supplied 'chariots' in brackets. The verse says twice that he was surrounded (vṛtaḥ and parivāritaḥ), and I kept both. I corrected my earlier 'in that engagement' to 'in the encounter', since the Sanskrit has only tatra ('there') and saṃyuge, with no demonstrative on saṃyuge. ↩
  120. the Goddess: devī: the Goddess, Durgā/Caṇḍikā, who is fighting Mahiṣa's host in this chapter. ↩
  121. The reading: tatra occurs twice in the verse (tatrāyutaśaḥ and tatra mahāsurāḥ), probably as a metrical filler; I render it once as 'there', and 'in that battle' renders saṃyuge. ayutaśaḥ ('by tens of thousands') could qualify either the asuras or the chariots, elephants and horses; I take it with the asuras, which is the more natural reading of the word order. ↩
  122. thousands of koṭis of koṭis: Literally 'by thousands of koṭi-koṭis': a hyperbolic number, each koṭi being ten million, multiplied again by koṭi and by thousands. ↩
  123. The reading: The verb 'abhūt' with 'vṛtaḥ' (literally 'was surrounded' or 'was there surrounded') is rendered 'was there, surrounded by'. The compound koṭikoṭisahasraiḥ could also be read as 'thousands of crores of crores' or 'thousands of pairs of koṭis'; I take it as thousands of koṭi-koṭis. The case of the genitives rathānām, dantinām and hayānām depends on this compound. ↩
  124. bhindipālas: A short missile weapon, usually taken to be a sling-staff or a hand-thrown javelin; the exact form is uncertain. ↩
  125. spears: Sanskrit śakti, here a hurled spear or lance as a weapon, not the Goddess as power; the glossary's 'Śakti' does not apply. ↩
  126. paṭṭiśas: A three-edged or double-edged battle-weapon, often rendered as a spear or a sharp-edged lance. ↩
  127. The reading: The subject is not stated in the verse; it continues from 82.46–47 (the great asuras and Mahiṣa's host), so I render 'they'. Śakti here is the weapon (spear), not the glossary's 'power'; I translate it as 'spears'. Paraśu is rendered 'axes' in the plural, as the compound paraśupaṭṭiśaiḥ is read as paraśu-paṭṭiśa, 'axes and paṭṭiśas'; the glossary's 'axe' as Viśvakarman's gift refers to the Goddess's own weapon, not these. ↩
  128. spears: śakti here is a thrown weapon, a spear or dart, not the divine power. The same rendering is used for śaktibhiḥ in 82.48. ↩
  129. nooses: pāśa: a noose or snare used as a weapon, as by Varuṇa and Yama. ↩
  130. The reading: The syntax is loose. (1) I accepted the reviewer's reading and take khaḍgaprahāraiḥ with pracakramuḥ: 'with sword-blows they set out to kill her'. This removes the verb I had invented for apare. The tu then marks the contrast between the missile-throwers and the sword-strikers, and I render it 'but'. (2) tathāpare has no verb of its own. I supply 'hurled' from cikṣipuḥ, and the object is left unstated because the Sanskrit does not give one. (3) The doubled devīm ... tām is rendered 'her, the Goddess'. (4) For śaktīḥ I follow the previous verse's 'spears', so that one rendering is used in both places. The note marks it as a weapon and not the glossary's Śakti = power. ↩
  131. Caṇḍikā: 'The Fierce One', a name of the Goddess (Devī) in her warlike aspect. ↩
  132. with mere sport: līlā: effortless play; the Goddess destroys the enemy's arms as though in sport, without strain. ↩
  133. The reading: Nijaśastrāstravarṣiṇī ('raining down her own weapons and missiles') is a nominative feminine compound qualifying the Goddess, so I take it as describing her as she cuts, not as an instrument. Śastrāṇi and astrāṇi are the enemy's weapons (tāni), as the glossary distinguishes hand-held weapons from hurled missiles; I rendered them as 'weapons and missiles'. ↩
  134. divine sages: surarṣi: a compound of sura and ṛṣi, sages of the gods' own order (like Nārada), as distinct from human sages. ↩
  135. asuras: Asuras are the rivals of the gods (devas/suras); here Mahiṣa's host. ↩
  136. the sovereign lady: Īśvarī, feminine of Īśvara, 'lord, sovereign'; here a title of the Goddess, who is also called Devī and Caṇḍikā. ↩
  137. The reading: anāyastānanā (anāyasta + ānanā): 'whose face was not strained/wearied', i.e. showing no exertion or distress. Chosen 'untroubled face'. The e-text's ceśvarī is ca + īśvarī; 'ca' is rendered by the dash-linked apposition, joining 'weapons and missiles'. The 'and' between śastrāṇi and astrāṇi is kept as 'weapons and missiles'. ↩
  138. the lion that was the Goddess's mount: Sanskrit vāhanakeśarī, 'the lion who is her vehicle'; the lion is Devī's mount in the battle. ↩
  139. like fire: Sanskrit hutāśana, 'eater of oblations', a name of Agni, here used for fire. ↩
  140. The reading: so 'pi ('he too') refers back to the lion, which the verse itself names in the compound vāhanakeśarī; the verse before concerns the Goddess, so 'he' is the lion, not Mahiṣa. The compound devyā vāhanakeśarī is read as 'the lion who is the Goddess's mount' (devyā as genitive-sense instrumental/genitive-type relation in the compound); no emendation needed. ↩
  141. Ambikā: 'Mother'; a name of the Goddess, here the same as Caṇḍikā and Devī. ↩
  142. gaṇas: Troops or hosts of attendants; the retinue of divine followers, as of Śiva and the Goddess. ↩
  143. The reading: niśvasān (accusative plural of niśvāsa, 'breath') is read with yān as the object of mumuce; 'ta eva ... gaṇāḥ' is the correlative, so the breaths themselves became the gaṇas. The form mumuce is an irregular middle perfect, used here with active sense ('released'). The word niśvasān is not entirely certain as the sandhi form niśvasān + mumuce, but the sense is clear. ↩
  144. strengthened by the Goddess's power: The compound devīśaktyupabṛṃhitāḥ can also be read 'reinforced by the Goddess's Śakti'. Here it refers to the gaṇas, born from her breaths in the previous verse, and the sense is that they draw strength from her. ↩
  145. The reading: The subject 'they' (te) is the gaṇas of 82.53. The compound bhindipālāsipaṭṭiśaiḥ is read as bhindipāla-asi-paṭṭiśaiḥ, 'bhindipālas, swords and paṭṭiśas'. The word asi, 'sword', is a synonym of khaḍga, and I render it 'swords'. ↩
  146. gaṇas: The gaṇas are the troops or attendants, here the hosts born from the breaths of Ambikā (verse 82.53). ↩
  147. drums: paṭaha: a large drum, often a war drum. ↩
  148. mṛdaṅgas: A mṛdaṅga is a double-headed drum played with the hands. ↩
  149. śaktis: Here śakti is a weapon, a spear or javelin hurled in volleys, not the Goddess's power as in 82.54. ↩
  150. swords: The e-text reads śaḍga, evidently a misprint for khaḍga, 'sword'. ↩
  151. The reading: śaḍgādibhiḥ: śaḍga has no meaning as a Sanskrit word and is almost certainly a misprint for khaḍga ('sword'), as in 82.58 (khaḍgapātaiḥ). I read khaḍgādibhiḥ, 'with swords and the like'. ↩
  152. noose: pāśa: a noose or snare, a binding weapon carried by deities such as Varuṇa and Yama; the Goddess is the implied subject. ↩
  153. The reading: The subject is not stated in the verse; it is the Goddess (Devī) of 82.56, so 'she' is supplied. The compound ghaṇṭāsvana-vimohitān could also be read as 'a bell's sound' in general, but the context indicates the Goddess's bell. The sense of bhuvi ('on the ground') could attach to baddhvā or to akarṣayat; I take it with the binding and the dragging together, reading 'on the ground' with baddhvā as the more natural position in the Sanskrit. ↩
  154. lie on the ground: The verb śerate is present tense ("lie"), though the participles are past; the verse shifts from the deeds to the resulting state of the fallen. ↩
  155. The reading: The e-text reads gadayā (instrumental, singular), "with the mace", and nipātena is also instrumental, so I take nipātena gadayā as "by the fall (blow) of the mace". Nipāta could also mean the "stroke" of the mace; "fall" is kept as the literal sense. The subject "others" (apare) is the same class as "some" (kecid), namely the asuras of the previous verses. ↩
  156. struck hard with the club: musale is a locative used where an instrumental is expected, 'on/by means of the club'. ↩
  157. were felled to the ground: The e-text reads kecinnapātitā; this is taken as a misprint for kecin nipātitā. ↩
  158. The reading: The e-text reads 'kecinnapātitā'. Read literally it could be kecin na pātitā ('some were not felled'), which makes no sense here and contradicts the sense of the passage. I have emended to kecin nipātitā, 'some were felled', parallel to the preceding verses. ↩
  159. tormentors of the devas: Tridaśārdanāḥ: the asuras, called 'tormentors of the thirty' (the devas). ↩
  160. resembling mountains: Śailānukāriṇaḥ: 'imitating mountains', that is, huge and bristling with arrows, or fallen like mountains. ↩
  161. The reading: Nirantarāḥ ... kṛtāḥ: 'made without interval' is read as 'closely studded with arrows, leaving no gap'; another possible sense is 'made continuous (a mass)'. Tridaśārdanāḥ is taken as an epithet of the asuras (nominative plural, agreeing with kecit), not of the Goddess's side. Śailānukāriṇaḥ may refer to their huge bodies or to their fall like mountains; I keep the literal 'resembling mountains'. ↩
  162. The reading: 'chinnagrīvāḥ' is a bahuvrīhi, 'those whose necks were cut'; I render it 'had their necks cut through'. 'Madhye vidāritāḥ' is taken as 'split through the middle' (the body or trunk, with no object stated); 'madhye' could also be read as 'in the midst [of the battle]', but the sense of cleaving fits the preceding context better. ↩
  163. lower legs: Sanskrit jaṅghā is the part of the leg from knee to ankle (shank). The thigh is ūru, a different word. ↩
  164. [each half] with one arm, one eye and one foot: The compound ekabāhvakṣicaraṇāḥ means 'having one arm, one eye and one foot'. Combined with dvidhā kṛtāḥ, 'cut in two', it describes bodies split lengthwise, so that each part keeps one of each member. ↩
  165. The reading: ekabāhvakṣicaraṇāḥ: the reviewer is right that it is a bahuvrīhi containing a dvandva, 'having one arm, one eye, one foot', and my earlier 'or' (distributive) reading was wrong. I now take it as each part having one arm, one eye and one foot. This is the natural result of being cut in two (dvidhā), with the bracketed '[each half]' supplied to make the English parse. A less likely reading takes it as a plain description of the asuras, who were then cut in two. jaṅghā: I changed 'thighs' to 'lower legs' because the thigh is ūru. 'Severed' replaces 'cut through' for vicchinna. ↩
  166. headless trunks (kabandhas): kabandha: a headless trunk, which in epic battle descriptions is said to rise and go on moving or fighting after the head has been severed. ↩
  167. The reading: 'anye' is taken as the subject of the sentence, continuing the list of asuras in the previous verses. 'paramāyudha' could be 'excellent weapons' or 'supreme weapons'; I chose 'excellent weapons'. 'chinne śirasi' is a locative absolute and is rendered as a concessive clause with 'api'. ↩
  168. musical instruments: tūrya means the instruments played in battle, such as drums and horns; laya is their beat or rhythm. ↩
  169. javelin: Sanskrit śakti, here a hurled or thrusting spear used as a weapon; it is not the Goddess as Power. ↩
  170. The reading: The e-text reads chinnakhirasaḥ, which is not a word; I take it as a misprint for chinnaśirasaḥ, 'with severed heads', which suits the context (kabandha, 'headless trunk'). ↩
  171. which made the hair stand on end: lomaharṣaṇa: literally 'causing the hair to bristle', said of something terrifying or thrilling. ↩
  172. The reading: viluptāṅgāḥ: 'smeared' (from vi-lip) is the sense I took; the form is from lup ('torn, cut'), so 'their limbs torn by floods of blood' is a possible alternative, but 'smeared' (viliptāṅgāḥ) fits the context better and is probably the intended reading. The e-text may preserve a variant. Also, 'anye' could be taken as 'others' contrasting with the earlier groups; I kept it so. ↩
  173. The reading: pātitai rathanāgāśvairasuraiśca: the instrumental plural could be read as 'by the fallen chariots, elephants, horses and asuras' with the earth as the thing covered; I took it as the cause of impassability ('because of'). Also, 'vasundharā' is rendered 'the earth' rather than kept in IAST, as it is a common epithet of the earth. ↩
  174. great rivers of floods of blood: The compound śoṇitaughā mahānadyaḥ is read as 'rivers made of floods of blood'; the floods of blood are themselves the great rivers. ↩
  175. The reading: The syntax of 'madhye ... vāraṇāsuravājinām' is loose: 'in the midst of the army of the asuras' is joined with the genitive plural 'of the elephants, asuras and horses'. I take the latter as 'among the elephants, asuras and horses' (the contents of the army). Another reading would make the rivers flow from the slain elephants, asuras and horses; the Sanskrit does not say so. ↩
  176. Ambikā: 'Little Mother'; a name of the Goddess (Devī), who is the agent in the preceding verses. ↩
  177. The reading: Verse 67 is untranslated in the working draft; this verse has no grammatical dependence on it. The verb ninye (passive, 'was led') is rendered actively with Ambikā as the agent, as the construction requires: ambikā is nominative, so the original is a passive with Ambikā as subject, which is an irregular but attested usage; the sense is that she led the army to destruction. The second 'brings ... to destruction' in the comparison is supplied in brackets for the English to parse. ↩
  178. searched out the life-breaths: The verb is the present vicinvati ('searches out, gathers'), used in a narrative past context; the construction is a vivid historic present. ↩
  179. enemies of the immortals: Amarāri, 'foe of the immortals', is a common designation of the asuras, the enemies of the devas. ↩
  180. The reading: The verb vicinvati is present tense, though the surrounding narrative is past. I kept it as a past narrative present rendered 'searched out'. Reading 'sa ca siṃho ... vicinvati' as a single clause, the iva ('as it were') qualifies the searching for life-breaths, which suggests the lion sought out the lives, not literally the breaths. ↩
  181. the gods: The Sanskrit has devāḥ, 'gods', the same class as sura; "them" (eṣām) most naturally refers to the Goddess and her gaṇas, who fought on the gods' side. ↩
  182. showering down flowers: puṣpavṛṣṭimucaḥ, 'releasing a rain of flowers', the usual sign of divine approval of a deed. ↩
  183. The reading: eṣām ('of/with them') is ambiguous. It could refer to the Goddess and her gaṇas, whose fighting pleased the gods, or to the asuras. I chose the Goddess and her gaṇas, since the gods rejoice at the fight on their side. Also, tutuṣuḥ is perfect 3rd plural of √tuṣ, 'were pleased'; the genitive eṣām is read loosely as 'with them' (the Goddess's side). ↩
  184. eighty-second chapter: The e-text's own chapter heading reads 78, but the colophon and the neighbouring verse numbers (82.65-70) give this as chapter 82 of the Purāṇa; the next colophon begins chapter 83. ↩
  185. Mahāpurāṇa: 'Great Purāṇa'; the title given to the Mārkaṇḍeya as one of the eighteen major Purāṇas. ↩
  186. The reading: The word śrī in the compound śrīmārkaṇḍeyamahāpurāṇe is an honorific ('revered'), not the goddess Śrī. Compounds in the colophon are read as locatives with iti ... adhyāyaḥ: the chapter is 'in' the Purāṇa, within the Devīmāhātmya and the Sāvarṇika manvantara. The chapter number in the source's header (78) differs from the colophon's dvyaśīti (82); I follow the colophon. ↩

Adhyāya 83

Sanskrit

त्र्यशीतितमो ऽध्यायः- ८३

tryaśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ- 83

English

The eighty-third chapter: 83.1

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:23

83.1

Sanskrit

निहन्यमानं तत्सैन्यमवलाक्य महासुरः । सेनानीश्चिक्षुरः कोपाद्ययौ योद्धुमथाम्बिकाम् ॥

nihanyamānaṃ tatsainyamavalākya mahāsuraḥ | senānīścikṣuraḥ kopādyayau yoddhumathāmbikām ||

English

Seeing that army being struck down, the great asura Cikṣura, the commander of the army, went in anger to fight Ambikā.

83.2

Sanskrit

स देवीं शरवर्षेण ववर्ष समरे ऽसुरः । यथा मेरुगिरेः शृङ्गं तोयवर्षेण तोयदः ॥

sa devīṃ śaravarṣeṇa vavarṣa samare 'suraḥ | yathā merugireḥ śṛṅgaṃ toyavarṣeṇa toyadaḥ ||

English

In the battle that asura rained a shower of arrows on the Goddess, as a rain-cloud rains a shower of water on the peak of the mountain Meru4.

83.3

Sanskrit

तस्य च्छित्त्वा ततो देवी लीलयैव शरोत्करान् । जघान तुरगान् बाणैर्यन्तारं चैव वाजिनाम् ॥

tasya cchittvā tato devī līlayaiva śarotkarān | jaghāna turagān bāṇairyantāraṃ caiva vājinām ||

English

Then the Goddess, having cut down his volleys of arrows with mere sport5, struck down his horses with her arrows, and also the driver of his horses.6

83.4

Sanskrit

चिच्छेद च धनुः सद्यो ध्वजं चातिसमुच्छ्रितम् । विव्याध चैव गात्रेषु च्छिन्नधन्वानमाशुगैः ॥

ciccheda ca dhanuḥ sadyo dhvajaṃ cātisamucchritam | vivyādha caiva gātreṣu cchinnadhanvānamāśugaiḥ ||

English

And she at once cut down his bow, and his exceedingly lofty banner, and she pierced his limbs with swift arrows7, though his bow was already cut.8

83.5

Sanskrit

स च्छिन्नधन्वा विरथो हताश्वो हतसारथिः । अभ्यधावत तां देवीं खड्गचर्मधरो ऽसुरः ॥

sa cchinnadhanvā viratho hatāśvo hatasārathiḥ | abhyadhāvata tāṃ devīṃ khaḍgacarmadharo 'suraḥ ||

English

With his bow cut, his chariot lost, his horses and his charioteer slain, that asura, bearing a sword and a shield9, rushed at the Goddess.

83.6

Sanskrit

सिंहमाहत्य खड्गेन तीक्ष्णधारेण मूर्धनि । आजघान भुजे सव्ये देवीमप्यतिवेगवान् ॥

siṃhamāhatya khaḍgena tīkṣṇadhāreṇa mūrdhani | ājaghāna bhuje savye devīmapyativegavān ||

English

Having struck the lion on the head with his sharp-edged sword, he, exceedingly swift, struck the Goddess too on her left arm.10

83.7

Sanskrit

तस्याः खड्गो भुजं प्राप्य पफाल नृपनन्दन । ततो जग्राह शूलं स कोपादरुणलोचनः ॥

tasyāḥ khaḍgo bhujaṃ prāpya paphāla nṛpanandana | tato jagrāha śūlaṃ sa kopādaruṇalocanaḥ ||

English

O delight of kings11, when it reached her arm, his sword shattered. Then, in anger, with eyes red, he seized a spear.12

83.8

Sanskrit

चिक्षेप च ततस्तत्तु भद्रकाल्यां महासुरः । जाज्वल्यमानं तेजोभी रविबिम्बमिवाम्बरात् ॥

cikṣepa ca tatastattu bhadrakālyāṃ mahāsuraḥ | jājvalyamānaṃ tejobhī ravibimbamivāmbarāt ||

English

And then the great asura hurled that [spear] at Bhadrakālī13, blazing with its radiances, like the orb of the sun14 [fallen] from the sky.15

83.9

Sanskrit

दृष्ट्वा तदापतच्छूलं देवी शूलममुञ्चत । तच्छूलं शतधा तेन नीतं स च महासुरः ॥

dṛṣṭvā tadāpatacchūlaṃ devī śūlamamuñcata | tacchūlaṃ śatadhā tena nītaṃ sa ca mahāsuraḥ ||

English

Then, seeing that spear flying toward her, the Goddess released a spear. By it that spear was brought to a hundred pieces, and that great asura too [was brought to a hundred pieces]16.17

83.10

Sanskrit

हते तस्मिन् महावीर्ये महिषस्य चमूपतौ । आजगाम गजारूढश्चामरस्त्रिदशार्दनः ॥

hate tasmin mahāvīrye mahiṣasya camūpatau | ājagāma gajārūḍhaścāmarastridaśārdanaḥ ||

English

When that one of great vigor, the commander of Mahiṣa's army18, had been slain, Cāmara, the tormentor of the devas19, came up, mounted on an elephant.20

83.11

Sanskrit

सो ऽपि शक्तिं मुमोचाथ देव्यास्तामम्बिका द्रुतम् । हुङ्काराभिहतां भूमौ पातयामास निष्प्रभाम् ॥

so 'pi śaktiṃ mumocātha devyāstāmambikā drutam | huṅkārābhihatāṃ bhūmau pātayāmāsa niṣprabhām ||

English

He too then hurled a śakti21 at the Goddess; Ambikā quickly struck it down with a roar of "hūṃ"22 and made it fall to the ground, its splendor gone.23

83.12

Sanskrit

भग्नां शक्तिं निपतितां दृष्ट्वा क्रोधसमन्वितः । चिक्षेप चामरः शूलं बाणैस्तदपि साच्छिनत् ॥

bhagnāṃ śaktiṃ nipatitāṃ dṛṣṭvā krodhasamanvitaḥ | cikṣepa cāmaraḥ śūlaṃ bāṇaistadapi sācchinat ||

English

Seeing the śakti broken and fallen, Cāmara, filled with anger, hurled a spear; she cut that down too with arrows.24

83.13

Sanskrit

ततः सिंहः समुत्पत्य गजकुम्भान्तरे स्थितः । बाहुयुद्धेन युयुधे तेनोच्चैस्त्रिदशारिणा ॥

tataḥ siṃhaḥ samutpatya gajakumbhāntare sthitaḥ | bāhuyuddhena yuyudhe tenoccaistridaśāriṇā ||

English

Then the lion leapt up and took his stand in the hollow between the elephant's frontal globes25, and fought loudly with that enemy of the devas26 in arm-to-arm combat.27

83.14

Sanskrit

युधायमानो ततस्तौ तु तस्मान्नागान्महीं गतौ । युयुधाते ऽतिसंरब्धौ प्रहारैरतिदारुणैः ॥

yudhāyamāno tatastau tu tasmānnāgānmahīṃ gatau | yuyudhāte 'tisaṃrabdhau prahārairatidāruṇaiḥ ||

English

Then, as the two were fighting28, they went from that elephant down to the ground, and, exceedingly enraged, they fought with extremely terrible blows.29

83.15

Sanskrit

ततो वेगात्खमुत्पत्य निपत्य च मृगारिणा । करप्रहारेण शिरश्चामरस्य पृथक्कृतम् ॥

tato vegātkhamutpatya nipatya ca mṛgāriṇā | karaprahāreṇa śiraścāmarasya pṛthakkṛtam ||

English

Then, springing up swiftly into the sky and falling down again, the foe of beasts30 severed Cāmara's head with a blow of the paw.31

83.16

Sanskrit

उदग्रश्च रणे देव्या शिलावृक्षादिभिर्हतः । दन्तमुष्टितलैश्चैव करालश्च निपातितः ॥

udagraśca raṇe devyā śilāvṛkṣādibhirhataḥ | dantamuṣṭitalaiścaiva karālaśca nipātitaḥ ||

English

And Udagra was slain in the battle by the Goddess with rocks, trees and the like; and Karāla32 too was brought down by blows of teeth, fists and palms33.34

83.17

Sanskrit

देवी क्रुद्धा गदापातैश्चूर्णयामास चोद्धतम् । वाष्कलं भिन्दिपालेन बाणैस्ताम्रं तथान्धकम् ॥

devī kruddhā gadāpātaiścūrṇayāmāsa coddhatam | vāṣkalaṃ bhindipālena bāṇaistāmraṃ tathāndhakam ||

English

83.17–18The Goddess, enraged, crushed the arrogant Uddhata35 with blows of the mace, Vāṣkala with a bhindipāla, and likewise Tāmra and Andhaka with arrows; and Ugrāsya, Ugravīrya, and Mahāhanu too, the three-eyed36 Parameśvarī slew with her trident.37

83.18

Sanskrit

उग्रास्यमुग्रवीर्यञ्च तथैव च महाहनुम् । त्रिनेत्रा च त्रिशूलेन जघान परमेश्वरी ॥

ugrāsyamugravīryañca tathaiva ca mahāhanum | trinetrā ca triśūlena jaghāna parameśvarī ||

English

Its English is given with 83.17–18, above.

83.19

Sanskrit

बिडालस्यासिना कायात् पातयामास वै शिरः । दुर्धरं दुर्मुखं चोभौ शरैर्निन्ये यमक्षयम् । कालं च कालदण्डेन कालरात्रिरपातयत् ॥

biḍālasyāsinā kāyāt pātayāmāsa vai śiraḥ | durdharaṃ durmukhaṃ cobhau śarairninye yamakṣayam | kālaṃ ca kāladaṇḍena kālarātrirapātayat ||

English

She made the head of Biḍāla38 fall from his body with a sword; and she led both Durdhara and Durmukha with arrows to the abode of Yama; and Kāla39rātri brought Kāla down with the staff of Kāla40.41

83.20

Sanskrit

अग्रदर्शनमत्युग्रैः खड्गपातैरताडयत् । असिनैवासिलोमानमच्छिदत् सा रणोत्सवे । गणैः सिंहेन देव्या च जयक्ष्वेडाकृतोत्सवैः ॥

agradarśanamatyugraiḥ khaḍgapātairatāḍayat | asinaivāsilomānamacchidat sā raṇotsave | gaṇaiḥ siṃhena devyā ca jayakṣveḍākṛtotsavaiḥ ||

English

She struck Agradarśana with extremely fierce sword-blows, and in that festival of battle42 she cut down Asiloman43 with a sword alone, while the gaṇas, the lion and the Goddess made a festival of cries of victory and roars.44

83.21

Sanskrit

एवं संक्षीयमाणे तु स्वसैन्ये महिषासुरः । माहिषेण स्वरूपेण त्रासयामास तान् गणान् ॥

evaṃ saṃkṣīyamāṇe tu svasainye mahiṣāsuraḥ | māhiṣeṇa svarūpeṇa trāsayāmāsa tān gaṇān ||

English

While his own army was being destroyed in this way, Mahiṣa, the asura, in his own buffalo form45, terrified those gaṇas.46

83.22

Sanskrit

कांश्चित्तुण्डप्रिहारेण खुरक्षेपैस्तथापरान् । लाङ्गूलताडितांश्चान्यान् शृङ्गाभ्याञ्च विदारितान् ॥

kāṃścittuṇḍaprihāreṇa khurakṣepaistathāparān | lāṅgūlatāḍitāṃścānyān śṛṅgābhyāñca vidāritān ||

English

83.22–23Some he brought down to the ground with blows of his snout47, others with thrusts of his hooves; others, struck with his tail, and still others, torn open with his two horns; some with his rush, others with his bellowing and his whirling, and still others with the wind of his breath.48

83.23

Sanskrit

वेगेन कांश्चिदपरान् नादेन भ्रमणेन च । निश्वासपवनेनान्यान् पातयामास भूतले ॥

vegena kāṃścidaparān nādena bhramaṇena ca | niśvāsapavanenānyān pātayāmāsa bhūtale ||

English

Its English is given with 83.22–23, above.

83.24

Sanskrit

निपात्य प्रमथानीकमभ्यधावत सो ऽसुरः । सिंहं हन्तुं महादेव्याः कोपं चक्रे ततो ऽम्बिका ॥

nipātya pramathānīkamabhyadhāvata so 'suraḥ | siṃhaṃ hantuṃ mahādevyāḥ kopaṃ cakre tato 'mbikā ||

English
