Having struck down the army of the pramathas49, that asura charged forward to kill the lion of the Great Goddess; then Ambikā50 grew angry.

83.25

Sanskrit

सो ऽपि कोपान्महावीर्यः खुरक्षुण्णमहीतलः । शृङ्गाभ्यां पर्वतानुच्चैश्चिक्षेप च ननाद च ॥

so 'pi kopānmahāvīryaḥ khurakṣuṇṇamahītalaḥ | śṛṅgābhyāṃ parvatānuccaiścikṣepa ca nanāda ca ||

English

He too, in anger, of great vigor, with the surface of the earth trampled by his hooves, hurled lofty mountains with his two horns, and he bellowed.51†

83.26

Sanskrit

वेगभ्रमणविक्षुण्णा मही तस्य व्यशीर्यत । लाङ्गूलेनाहतश्चाब्धिः प्लावयामास सर्वतः ॥

vegabhramaṇavikṣuṇṇā mahī tasya vyaśīryata | lāṅgūlenāhataścābdhiḥ plāvayāmāsa sarvataḥ ||

English

The earth, crushed by his rushing and whirling, split apart; and the ocean, struck by his tail, flooded everywhere.52

83.27

Sanskrit

धुतशृङ्गविभिन्नाश्च खण्डं खण्डं ययुर्घना । श्वासानिलास्ताः शतशो निपेतुर्नभसो ऽचलाः ॥

dhutaśṛṅgavibhinnāśca khaṇḍaṃ khaṇḍaṃ yayurghanā | śvāsānilāstāḥ śataśo nipeturnabhaso 'calāḥ ||

English

Split apart by his shaking horns, the clouds went off in pieces, fragment by fragment; and, by the winds of his breath, those mountains fell from the sky in their hundreds.53

83.28

Sanskrit

इति क्रोधसमाध्मातमापतान्तं महासुरम् । दृष्ट्वा सा चण्डिका कोपं तद्वधाय तदाकरोत् ॥

iti krodhasamādhmātamāpatāntaṃ mahāsuram | dṛṣṭvā sā caṇḍikā kopaṃ tadvadhāya tadākarot ||

English

Seeing the great asura thus charging at her, swollen with rage, Caṇḍikā54 then grew angry in order to kill him.55

83.29

Sanskrit

सा क्षिप्त्वा तस्य वै पाशं तं बबन्ध महासुरम् । तत्याज माहिषं रूपं सो ऽपि बद्धो महामृधे ॥

sā kṣiptvā tasya vai pāśaṃ taṃ babandha mahāsuram | tatyāja māhiṣaṃ rūpaṃ so 'pi baddho mahāmṛdhe ||

English

Having flung her noose56 at him, she bound that great asura. Bound in that great battle, he too abandoned his buffalo form.57

83.30

Sanskrit

ततः सिंहो ऽभवत् सद्यो यावत्तस्याम्बिका शिरः । छिनत्ति तावत्पुरुषः खड्गपाणिरदृश्यत ॥

tataḥ siṃho 'bhavat sadyo yāvattasyāmbikā śiraḥ | chinatti tāvatpuruṣaḥ khaḍgapāṇiradṛśyata ||

English

Then he at once became a lion. As soon as Ambikā58 cut off its head, a man with a sword in his hand appeared.59

83.31

Sanskrit

तत एवाशु पुरुषं देवी चिच्छेद सायकैः । तं खड्गचर्मणा सार्धं ततः सो ऽभून्महागजः ॥

tata evāśu puruṣaṃ devī ciccheda sāyakaiḥ | taṃ khaḍgacarmaṇā sārdhaṃ tataḥ so 'bhūnmahāgajaḥ ||

English

Then the Goddess at once cut down that man, together with his sword and shield, with her arrows. Then he became a great elephant60.61

83.32

Sanskrit

करेण च महासिंहं तं चकर्ष जगर्ज च । कर्षतस्तु करं देवी खड्गेन निरकृन्तत ॥

kareṇa ca mahāsiṃhaṃ taṃ cakarṣa jagarja ca | karṣatastu karaṃ devī khaḍgena nirakṛntata ||

English

With his trunk he dragged that great lion62, and he roared. But as he dragged, the Goddess cut off his trunk with her sword.63

83.33

Sanskrit

ततो महासुरो भूयो माहिषं वपुरास्थितः । तथैव क्षोभयामास त्रैलोक्यं सचराचरम् ॥

tato mahāsuro bhūyo māhiṣaṃ vapurāsthitaḥ | tathaiva kṣobhayāmāsa trailokyaṃ sacarācaram ||

English

Then the great asura, having taken up his buffalo body once more, threw the three worlds, with all that moves and does not move64, into turmoil as before.

83.34

Sanskrit

ततः क्रुद्धा जगन्माता चण्डिका पानमुत्तमम् । पपौ पुनः पुनश्चैव जहासारुणलोचना ॥

tataḥ kruddhā jaganmātā caṇḍikā pānamuttamam | papau punaḥ punaścaiva jahāsāruṇalocanā ||

English

Then Caṇḍikā, the mother of the world65, grew angry. She drank the supreme drink66 again and again, and laughed, her eyes red.67

83.35

Sanskrit

ननर्द चासुरः सो ऽपि बलवीर्यमदोद्धतः । विषाणाब्याञ्च चिक्षेप चण्डिकां प्रति भूधरान् ॥

nanarda cāsuraḥ so 'pi balavīryamadoddhataḥ | viṣāṇābyāñca cikṣepa caṇḍikāṃ prati bhūdharān ||

English

That asura too roared, swollen with the intoxication of his strength and vigor68, and hurled mountains at Caṇḍikā with his horns69.70

83.36

Sanskrit

सा च तान् प्रहितांस्तेन चूर्णयन्ती शरोत्करैः । उवाच तं मदोद्धूतमुखरागाकुलाक्षरम् ॥

sā ca tān prahitāṃstena cūrṇayantī śarotkaraiḥ | uvāca taṃ madoddhūtamukharāgākulākṣaram ||

English

And she, crushing those [mountains] hurled by him with volleys of arrows, spoke to him, the flush of intoxication risen on her face and her syllables faltering71.72

Sanskrit

देव्युवाच

devyuvāca

English

83.37The Goddess73 said: "Roar, roar for a moment, you fool, while I drink madhu74. When I have slain you right here, the gods will soon roar."75

83.37

Sanskrit

गर्ज गर्ज क्षणं मूढ मधु यावत्पिबाम्यहम् । मया त्वयि हते ऽत्रैव गर्जिष्यन्त्याशु देवताः ॥

garja garja kṣaṇaṃ mūḍha madhu yāvatpibāmyaham | mayā tvayi hate 'traiva garjiṣyantyāśu devatāḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 83.37, above.

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

83.38The ṛṣi said: Having spoken thus and leapt up, she mounted that great asura76 and, pressing [him] down with her foot on the neck, struck him with her spear77.78

83.38

Sanskrit

एवमुक्त्वा समुत्पत्य सारूढा तं महासुरम् । पादेनाक्रम्य कण्ठे च शूलेनैनमताडयत् ॥

evamuktvā samutpatya sārūḍhā taṃ mahāsuram | pādenākramya kaṇṭhe ca śūlenainamatāḍayat ||

English

Its English is given with 83.38, above.

83.39

Sanskrit

ततः सो ऽपि पदाक्रान्तस्तया निजमुखात्ततः । अर्धनिष्क्रान्त एवासीद् देव्या वीर्येण संवृतः ॥

tataḥ so 'pi padākrāntastayā nijamukhāttataḥ | ardhaniṣkrānta evāsīd devyā vīryeṇa saṃvṛtaḥ ||

English

Then he too, trampled by her under foot, had come half out of his own mouth79, held fast by the Goddess80's vigor.81

83.40

Sanskrit

अर्धनिष्क्रान्त एवासौ युध्यमानो महासुरः । तया महासिना देव्या शिरश्छित्त्वा निपातितः ॥

ardhaniṣkrānta evāsau yudhyamāno mahāsuraḥ | tayā mahāsinā devyā śiraśchittvā nipātitaḥ ||

English

That great asura, half emerged82 and still fighting, was felled by her, the Goddess, who cut off his head with her great sword.83

83.41

Sanskrit

एवं स महिषो नाम ससैन्यः ससुहृद्गणः । त्रैलोक्यं मोहयित्वा तु तया देव्या विनाशितः ॥

evaṃ sa mahiṣo nāma sasainyaḥ sasuhṛdgaṇaḥ | trailokyaṃ mohayitvā tu tayā devyā vināśitaḥ ||

English

Thus that one named Mahiṣa, together with his army and his troop of friends, having bewildered the three worlds, was yet destroyed by her, the Goddess.84

83.42

Sanskrit

त्रैलोक्यस्थैस्तदा भूतैर्महिषे विनिपातिते । जयेत्युक्तं ततः सर्वैः सदेवासुरमानवैः ॥

trailokyasthaistadā bhūtairmahiṣe vinipātite | jayetyuktaṃ tataḥ sarvaiḥ sadevāsuramānavaiḥ ||

English

When Mahiṣa had been struck down, the beings who dwelt in the three worlds then cried out "Victory!"; and thereupon so did all of them, together with the devas, the asuras and men85.86

83.43

Sanskrit

ततो हाहाकृतं सर्वं दैत्यसैन्यं ननाश तत् । प्रहर्षञ्च परं जग्मुः सकला देवतागणाः ॥

tato hāhākṛtaṃ sarvaṃ daityasainyaṃ nanāśa tat | praharṣañca paraṃ jagmuḥ sakalā devatāgaṇāḥ ||

English

Then that whole army of the Daityas, crying "Alas, alas!", perished; and all the hosts of the gods87 attained supreme joy.88

83.44

Sanskrit

तुष्टुवुस्तां सुरा देवीं सह दिव्यैर्महर्षिभिः । जगुर्गन्धर्वपतयो ननृतुश्चाप्सरोगणाः ॥

tuṣṭuvustāṃ surā devīṃ saha divyairmaharṣibhiḥ | jagurgandharvapatayo nanṛtuścāpsarogaṇāḥ ||

English

The suras praised her, the Goddess, together with the divine great sages89; the lords of the Gandharvas sang, and the hosts of Apsarases90 danced.91

Sanskrit

इति श्रीमार्कण्डेयपुराणे सावर्णिके मन्वन्तरे देवीमाहात्म्ये महिषासुरवधो नाम त्र्यशीतितमो ऽध्यायः

iti śrīmārkaṇḍeyapurāṇe sāvarṇike manvantare devīmāhātmye mahiṣāsuravadho nāma tryaśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ

English

Thus ends the eighty-third chapter92, named "The Slaying of Mahiṣāsura," in the Devīmāhātmya, in the Sāvarṇika manvantara, in the revered93 Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa.94

  1. The reading: The line is a chapter heading, not a verse: tryaśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ is 'the eighty-third chapter', and '83' is the chapter number written in numerals (८३). The ordinal is tryaśīti-tama, 'eighty-third'. The numeral is rendered as it stands. The preceding colophon (662.1) closes chapter 82 and this heading opens chapter 83. The verse numbering 663.1 comes from the e-text's running count and does not match the chapter's own verse numbers. ↩
  2. Ambikā: 'Little Mother'; a name of the Goddess (Devī), here the one Cikṣura goes to fight. ↩
  3. The reading: The text reads avalākya, which is a misprint for avalokya ('having seen'); I translate it as avalokya. Also, 663.2 is the printed verse number of the speaker's line, which belongs with chapter 83, and 83.1 follows it. ↩
  4. the mountain Meru: Meru is the mythical golden mountain at the centre of the world; the Sanskrit says merugiri, 'Meru-mountain'. ↩
  5. with mere sport: līlā: effortless play; the Goddess fights as though in sport, without strain. ↩
  6. The reading: tasya is read with śarotkarān ('his volleys of arrows'), not with the verb. The yantṛ (driver, charioteer) is the same as the sārathi of 83.5. līlayā eva is rendered 'with mere sport'. ↩
  7. swift arrows: āśuga, 'going swiftly', is a common poetic word for an arrow. ↩
  8. The reading: The subject of ciccheda and vivyādha is the Goddess (Devī, subject of 83.3), carried over from the previous verse. chinnadhanvānam is accusative, 'him whose bow was cut', and agrees with the unexpressed Cikṣura. I rendered it concessively ('though his bow was already cut') because the bow is cut in the same verse and the piercing follows. It could also be read as simply descriptive. ↩
  9. a sword and a shield: khaḍgacarma: the sword and the leather shield carried together by a warrior; the compound khaḍgacarmadhara means 'bearing sword and shield'. ↩
  10. The reading: The verse is a single sentence with the absolutive āhatya ('having struck') and the main verb ājaghāna ('struck'), both with the same agent, the asura Cikṣura (the subject carried over from 83.5). The adverbial api is rendered 'too', marking the second target after the lion. The compound tīkṣṇadhāreṇa ('sharp-edged') qualifies khaḍgena. ↩
  11. O delight of kings: Vocative nṛpanandana, addressed by the narrator to King Suratha, who is hearing the story. ↩
  12. The reading: nṛpanandana could mean 'son of a king' or 'delight of kings'. I chose 'delight of kings' as the more natural sense of nandana in a vocative address. Since the glossary does not say it was Suratha who was addressed, I also kept the identification in the note and did not put it in the translation. ↩
  13. Bhadrakālī: A name of the Goddess (Devī), here the same figure as the Devī of the preceding verses. ↩
  14. orb of the sun: Sanskrit ravibimba. Here bimba means a round disc or orb, not the red fruit. ↩
  15. The reading: tejobhī: the printed form is metrically and grammatically irregular. I read it as tejobhiḥ, instrumental plural, and render 'with its radiances' (the reviewer's point on the plural is accepted). tatastattu is read as tataḥ tat tu. Tat refers to the spear (śūla) of 83.7, and jājvalyamānam agrees with it. I leave tu untranslated: it is a mere expletive here, filling the metre, and there is no contrast to mark. Ambarāt is an ablative, 'from the sky'. The Sanskrit has no participle, so I supply '[fallen]' in brackets to make the simile parse. This is the reviewer's suggestion, and it is more literal than the earlier 'falling'. ↩
  16. and that great asura too [was brought to a hundred pieces]: The verse has the participle nītam ('brought') only with the spear. The clause sa ca mahāsuraḥ is nominative with no verb, so the same predicate is understood for the asura: he too was reduced to a hundred pieces. ↩
  17. The reading: The last clause, sa ca mahāsuraḥ, has no verb. I supplied 'was brought to a hundred pieces' from nītam, taking tena as the Goddess's spear. The line may be a corrupt or compressed form of a reading in which the Goddess's spear splits both the asura's spear and the asura himself. ↩
  18. the commander of Mahiṣa's army: The one slain is the great asura of the preceding verses, who is here called Mahiṣa's army-commander; the text does not name him in this verse. ↩
  19. the tormentor of the devas: Tridaśārdana: an epithet of Cāmara, from tridaśa ('the thirty', the devas) and ardana ('tormentor'). It marks him as one of the asuras. ↩
  20. The reading: tridaśārdanaḥ is taken as a nominative epithet of Cāmara (tridaśa + ardana, 'tormentor of the devas'), consistent with the glossary. mahāvīrye is taken as a bahuvrīhi qualifying tasmin ('of great vigor'), referring to the asura just slain in 83.9, who is the commander (camūpati) of Mahiṣa's army. ↩
  21. śakti: A spear-like missile or javelin, here a weapon. It is not the same as Śakti, the Goddess as divine power, which the glossary lists. ↩
  22. roar of "hūṃ": huṅkāra: the sound "hūṃ", uttered as a fierce cry. Here it acts as a weapon in its own right. ↩
  23. The reading: The word śakti is ambiguous between 'power' and 'javelin'. Since Cāmara hurls it and it falls to the ground, I take it as a weapon and keep it in IAST to avoid confusion with the glossary's Śakti. Grammar: 'so 'pi' refers to Cāmara of 83.10. 'devyāḥ' is read with 'mumoca' as the Goddess's target, though it could be read with 'śaktim' as 'the Goddess's power'; the first fits the sense, since Ambikā is the one who strikes it down. Ambikā is the Goddess named in the glossary. ↩
  24. The reading: The subject of 'sācchinat' (sā + acchinat) is the Goddess (Devī/Ambikā), implied from 83.11; 'sā' is the feminine pronoun, so the cutting is hers, not Cāmara's. 'tadapi' (tad api) is taken as 'that too', referring to the spear. The śakti is described as 'bhagnām' (broken) and 'nipatitām' (fallen), both qualifying śaktim. ↩
  25. frontal globes: gajakumbha: the two rounded swellings on the upper forehead of an elephant; the lion stands in the hollow between them. ↩
  26. enemy of the devas: tridaśāri, a synonym of Tridaśārdana: the same Cāmara, the asura, who came mounted on an elephant in 83.10. ↩
  27. The reading: uccaiḥ (in the compound-like sandhi tenoccaistridaśāriṇā) is an adverb, not a qualifier of Cāmara. It can mean 'loudly', 'fiercely' or 'from on high'. I took it with yuyudhe as 'loudly', following the reviewer's reading that it qualifies the fighting. 'From on high' would also suit the lion's position on the elephant's head. The draft's 'who was shouting loudly' is dropped, as the text does not say Cāmara shouts. The reviewer is right that the instrumental tena with yuyudhe is ordinary (comitative: 'fought with him'), so the draft's doubt on that point is withdrawn. ↩
  28. as the two were fighting: The text reads yudhāyamāno (singular) with tau (dual); the participle is taken as agreeing with the two combatants, the lion and Cāmara, and as irregular for yudhyamānau. ↩
  29. The reading: yudhāyamāno is singular and, as printed, does not agree with the dual tau; probably a corruption or metrical licence for yudhyamānau ('fighting'). I take it as describing both combatants. Also, nāgāt 'from the elephant' is understood as the elephant Cāmara rode (gaja in the preceding verse); the two (the lion and Cāmara) are the subject of gatau and yuyudhāte. ↩
  30. the foe of beasts: Mṛgāri, a common name of the lion; here it denotes the Goddess's lion (siṃha). The compound mṛgāriṇā is the agent of both absolutives and of the severing. ↩
  31. The reading: 1) mṛga: 'deer' or 'wild beasts' in general. I accepted the finding and chose 'beasts', since in the epithet mṛgāri the word means wild animals generally; the draft's 'deer' was too narrow. 2) Voice: the Sanskrit is passive (pṛthakkṛtam, with the agent in the instrumental mṛgāriṇā). I render it actively, with the foe of beasts as subject of both absolutives and the main verb, so that the absolutives keep their true agent. This is a permitted change of voice and the sense is the same. 3) 'The lion' is not repeated as a separate noun; the epithet alone carries the subject, and the note identifies it as the lion. 4) 'From his body' is removed, as pṛthakkṛtam says only that the head was made separate. 5) kara is 'hand', here the lion's forepaw; I rendered it 'paw' because the actor is a lion. ↩
  32. Karāla: An asura commander in Mahiṣa's army, named here for the first time in this passage. ↩
  33. blows of teeth, fists and palms: The compound dantamuṣṭitalaiḥ is an instrumental plural, 'with teeth, fists and palms'. Some take the teeth as the Goddess's own, others as the lion's; the verse does not say whose. ↩
  34. The reading: dantamuṣṭitalaiḥ: the verse does not say whose teeth are meant, the Goddess's or her lion's. I translate literally and leave the agent as the Goddess (devyā), which governs the whole verse. The compound can also be read as 'teeth, fists and palms', as given, with no further sense implied. ↩
  35. Uddhata: Text: coddhatam (ca + uddhatam). Uddhata is taken as the name of an asura warrior; uddhata can also mean 'arrogant', which is why the translation adds 'arrogant' before the name. ↩
  36. three-eyed: Trinetrā: the Goddess is three-eyed, like Śiva, whose power she is. ↩
  37. The reading: coddhatam: either the name Uddhata (chosen, since it stands in a list of asura names with Vāṣkala, Tāmra, Andhaka) or the adjective 'arrogant' agreeing with an unnamed foe. The translation keeps the name and adds 'arrogant' before it to cover the second sense. ugrāsyam ugravīryaṃ ca could also be read as epithets ('fierce-faced', 'of fierce vigor') of Mahāhanu; I take them as two separate names, as the list context suggests. ↩
  38. Biḍāla: An asura commander of Mahiṣa's army. The word means 'cat'. ↩
  39. Kāla: Here the name of an asura warrior, not the god Time. The wordplay with kāladaṇḍa and Kālarātri is intended. ↩
  40. the staff of Kāla: kāladaṇḍa: the rod of Time or Death, the weapon with which Yama (Kāla) punishes. The text uses the name of the victim, Kāla, for the weapon too. ↩
  41. The reading: The subject of the first two verbs is not stated. I take it to be the Goddess (Devī) of the preceding verses, so that Kālarātri in the last clause is the same Goddess under another epithet. Kāla is taken as an asura's name because the glossary's god Kāla would not be 'brought down' (apātayat) by the Goddess. ↩
  42. festival of battle: raṇotsava: battle is spoken of as a festival, a conventional image of warriors' joy in fighting; the same image recurs in the closing phrase. ↩
  43. Asiloman: The name means 'sword-haired' (asiloman); this asura is named in the lists of Mahiṣa's warriors. ↩
  44. The reading: 1. The subject is not named. 'She' (sā) is taken to be the Goddess, as Kālarātri in the preceding verse; but devyā in the last pāda stands as a separate instrumental, so the text may be slightly disordered. 2. The final pāda has plural instrumentals (gaṇaiḥ ... siṃhena devyā ca ... kṛtotsavaiḥ), with no finite verb to govern them. I read them as an accompanying circumstance ('while they made a festival ...'). The plural kṛtotsavaiḥ agreeing with a mixed group is irregular, and the text may be faulty. 3. The compound jayakṣveḍā is read as a dvandva, 'cries of victory and roars'. It could also be a tatpuruṣa, 'victory-roars'. ↩
  45. in his own buffalo form: māhiṣeṇa svarūpeṇa: 'his own form, the buffalo one'. Mahiṣa returns to his natural buffalo shape, which he had evidently left for a human or other form in the fighting. ↩
  46. The reading: svarūpeṇa māhiṣeṇa could mean either 'in his own form, the buffalo form' (chosen) or 'in a buffalo form of his own'. The sense is the same: he resumes the buffalo shape. The particle tu is left untranslated as merely transitional. ↩
  47. blows of his snout: The text reads tuṇḍaprihāreṇa; prihāra is most likely a corruption or variant of prahāra, 'blow'. Tuṇḍa is the snout or muzzle of the buffalo. ↩
  48. The reading: tuṇḍaprihāreṇa (83.22): prihāra is not a regular word; I take it as a misprint or variant of prahāra, 'blow, stroke'. Also, vidāritān and tāḍitān are accusative participles agreeing with the objects, and I render them as 'struck' and 'torn open'. Mahiṣa, the subject, is carried over from 83.21. ↩
  49. pramathas: The pramathas are the troops of Śiva's attendants. Here they are the same host that the preceding verses call the gaṇas of the Goddess. ↩
  50. Ambikā: Ambikā, 'Little Mother', is a name of the Goddess. She is the same person as the Great Goddess named earlier in the verse. ↩
  51. The reading: The verse has no stated subject beyond so 'pi, which refers to Mahiṣa, the asura, who is the subject of 83.24. The word 'api' ('too') points back to Ambikā's anger in 83.24: he too grew angry. I took mahāvīryaḥ and khurakṣuṇṇamahītalaḥ as epithets agreeing with the subject, and parvatān uccaiḥ as 'lofty mountains'. The adverbial reading, 'hurled mountains high [into the air]', is also possible. ↩
  52. The reading: The verse has no variant problems. 'tasya' is read with 'vegabhramaṇavikṣuṇṇā' ('crushed by his rushing and whirling') and 'mahī' ('the earth'), and refers to Mahiṣa. The compound 'vegabhramaṇa' is taken as 'rush and whirl' (dvandva), as in the preceding verse, where 'vega' and 'bhramaṇa' appear as separate instruments. ↩
  53. The reading: The text reads śvāsānilāḥ tāḥ ... acalāḥ. Taken literally, śvāsānilāḥ is nominative plural ('the winds of his breath') and tāḥ is feminine, so it cannot agree with the masculine acalāḥ. The line looks corrupt, perhaps for something like śvāsānilāhatāḥ ('struck by the winds of his breath'). I took śvāsānilāḥ as instrumental in sense ('by the winds of his breath') and tāḥ as a loose 'those' pointing to the mountains he had hurled up in 83.25. Also, ghanā is read as ghanāḥ (nominative plural, 'clouds') with the visarga lost, to agree with yayuḥ. ↩
  54. Caṇḍikā: 'The Fierce One'; a name of the Goddess, here the same figure as Ambikā and the Great Goddess of the preceding verses. ↩
  55. The reading: The printed reading āpatāntam is irregular; I take it as a corrupt or metrically lengthened form of āpatantam, the accusative present participle of ā-√pat, 'rushing at, charging', agreeing with mahāsuram. Also, tadvadhāya could mean 'for his killing' (the asura's) or, less naturally, something else; I take tad- as the asura, and kopam akarot as 'grew angry' (literally 'made anger'). ↩
  56. her noose: The pāśa is the Goddess's weapon, a rope or snare for binding an enemy. ↩
  57. The reading: tasya pāśam: 'tasya' is most naturally read with 'pāśam' as 'at him' (dative sense, 'cast at him'), but could be read as 'his noose'. I chose 'at him' because the noose is the Goddess's weapon and 'taṃ babandha' follows. The particle 'vai' is emphatic and is left out. The subject of 'tatyāja' is the asura (so 'pi baddhaḥ); 'mahāmṛdhe' is a locative of the great battle, taken with 'baddhaḥ'. ↩
  58. Ambikā: 'Mother'; a name of the Goddess, here identical with Caṇḍikā and Mahādevī. ↩
  59. The reading: The subject of 'abhavat' is not stated; it is the asura who was bound in 83.29, who now takes the form of a lion. 'Tasya' ('his', i.e. the lion's) goes with 'śiraḥ'; the head cut off is that of the lion. 'Yāvat ... tāvat' is rendered 'as soon as ... [then]', expressing immediate succession. 'Puruṣa' is taken as a man, the asura's second form, not as the Cosmic Person. ↩
  60. Then he became a great elephant: The verse is syntactically compressed: taṃ khaḍgacarmaṇā sārdhaṃ is the object of ciccheda, 'cut him together with sword and shield'. The subject of 'became' is the asura (so), who changes form again. ↩
  61. The reading: The word order is unusual: 'taṃ' follows puruṣaṃ in a way that could seem redundant. I read puruṣaṃ ... taṃ as one object, 'that man', with khaḍgacarmaṇā sārdhaṃ ('together with sword and shield'). 'Tata eva' is read as 'then, immediately', with 'āśu' reinforcing it. ↩
  62. great lion: mahāsiṃha is the Goddess's lion mount, which the elephant seizes with his trunk; the compound could also be read as a lion in general, but the context supports the Goddess's mount. ↩
  63. The reading: mahāsiṃham: 'great lion' is taken as the Goddess's lion (siṃha), which the elephant drags. karaṃ: 'trunk', the primary sense of 'hand' when said of an elephant. karṣatas is a genitive absolute ('as he dragged'), and the Goddess is the agent of nirakṛntata. ↩
  64. with all that moves and does not move: sacarācaram: together with the moving (cara) and the unmoving (acara), that is, all living beings and all things; a stock phrase for the whole creation. ↩
  65. the mother of the world: Jaganmātā: the Goddess as the mother of the universe. ↩
  66. the supreme drink: Pāna: the drink is understood as wine or a similar intoxicating liquor; the verse does not name it. 'Uttama' may also be read as 'excellent'. ↩
  67. The reading: The word uttama in pānamuttamam can mean 'supreme' or 'excellent'. I have kept 'supreme', the primary sense of the word. The drink itself is not named. ↩
  68. swollen with the intoxication of his strength and vigor: The compound balavīryamadoddhataḥ can be read as 'puffed up by the pride/intoxication of strength and vigor'; mada means both pride and intoxication. ↩
  69. with his horns: The printed viṣāṇābyāñ ca is corrupt; viṣāṇābhyāṃ ca, 'and with his two horns', is the evident reading. ↩
  70. The reading: The text reads viṣāṇābyāñca, evidently a corruption of viṣāṇābhyāṃ ca ('and with the two horns'). I follow this emendation, with the instrumental dual rendered 'with his horns'. Since bhūdharān is accusative plural, the mountains are what is hurled. Also, balavīryamadoddhataḥ could be taken as a tatpuruṣa 'swollen with the intoxication of strength and vigor' or as 'swollen with strength, vigor and intoxication'; I chose the first, as the more natural construal. ↩
  71. the flush of intoxication risen on her face and her syllables faltering: The compound madoddhūtamukharāgākulākṣaram is taken as an adverbial accusative (neuter) describing how the Goddess speaks: mada-uddhūta-mukha-rāga, 'the flush of the face stirred up by intoxication', and ākula-akṣara, 'with confused syllables', that is, slurred speech. The intoxication is the madhu she drank in 83.34 and is still drinking in 83.37. The same ending could also be read as masculine, agreeing with taṃ. ↩
  72. The reading: I accept the reviewer's correction. The ending -m allows two readings: (a) a masculine bahuvrīhi agreeing with taṃ, the asura, which was my earlier reading, and (b) a neuter adverbial accusative with uvāca, or a qualifier of an implied vākyam, describing the Goddess's manner of speaking. I now choose (b). In 83.34 the Goddess drinks the supreme drink again and again, and in 83.37 she says 'while I drink the wine'. Her flushed face and slurred speech therefore follow naturally. The asura's 'intoxication' in 83.35 is the pride of his strength and vigor (balavīryamadoddhata), not wine. My earlier claim that the grammar forbids reading (b) was wrong. taṃ is now simply the object of uvāca, 'spoke to him'. mada-uddhūta is taken as 'stirred up, raised by intoxication', qualifying mukharāga, 'flush of the face'. The bracketed 'mountains' for tān is supplied from bhūdharān in 83.35. ↩
  73. The Goddess: The speaker's line is numbered 666.1 in the e-text; it is a heading, not a verse of the chapter, and the text of the Goddess's speech continues the sentence begun in 83.36 ('spoke to him'). ↩
  74. madhu: Madhu here is the common noun for an intoxicating drink (honey-wine, mead), not the asura Madhu of the glossary; it refers to the 'supreme drink' (pānam uttamam) of 83.34. ↩
  75. The reading: madhu could be the asura Madhu (glossary) or the common noun 'wine, honey-drink'. I chose the common noun because the Goddess is drinking (pibāmi) and 83.34 speaks of her drinking pānam uttamam. 'Madhu' as an asura could not be the object of pibāmi. Also, devatāḥ is rendered 'gods' (the glossary has deva and sura, and devatā is a synonym), and garjiṣyanti is read as 'will roar' in the sense of rejoicing, in contrast to the asura's roaring. ↩
  76. asura: asura: a class of powerful beings, often hostile to the gods (deva); here the buffalo-demon Mahiṣa. ↩
  77. spear: śūla: a spike, pike or lance, often a trident. Here it is the Goddess's weapon; 'spear' is used throughout. ↩
  78. The reading: sārūḍhā is read as sā + ārūḍhā ('she mounted'), with taṃ mahāsuram as the object of ārūḍhā. I agree with the reviewer that the gerund ākramya has the asura, not the neck, as its object, and that kaṇṭhe is a locative ('on the neck'). The object of ākramya is not expressed, so I supply [him] in brackets. Enam, the object of atāḍayat, is the same asura. The draft's two separate sentences are now one, and ca ('and') joins the pressing and the blow. Śūla can mean 'spear' or 'trident'; I chose 'spear' because the verse gives no further detail. ↩
  79. half out of his own mouth: The buffalo-asura, when pressed down by the Goddess, begins to issue from the mouth of his buffalo form in his own demonic shape; verse 83.40 shows him still fighting in this half-emerged state before she beheads him. ↩
  80. the Goddess: devyā vīryeṇa is read together as an instrumental, 'by the Goddess's vigor', governing saṃvṛtaḥ ('enveloped, overpowered, held fast'). ↩
  81. The reading: saṃvṛtaḥ ('covered, enveloped, restrained') could mean 'hemmed in' or 'overpowered'. I chose 'held fast' as fitting the context of his being stuck half-emerged. The word nijamukhāt tataḥ has a redundant second tataḥ; I take it as a mere filler (ablative sense 'from his own mouth') and do not render it separately. Mahiṣa is not named in this verse, so the glossary entry is only for later use. ↩
  82. half emerged: The asura, Mahiṣa (named in 83.41), was half out of his own mouth, that is, leaving his buffalo form, held down by the Goddess (83.39). ↩
  83. The reading: mahāsinā: read as mahā + asi, 'with a great sword', instrumental of the sword, not a name. 'Her' comes from tayā and needs no bracket. śiraś chittvā: the absolutive belongs to the agent tayā devyā, and nipātitaḥ is passive with the asura as subject. The translation now says this ('was felled by her, the Goddess, who cut off his head'). The draft had the Goddess as subject. tayā and devyā are in apposition, now rendered 'by her, the Goddess'. The earlier note wrongly said 'new form' and 'buffalo-mouth'. 83.39 says only 'from his own mouth', so the note is corrected. The verse does not name Mahiṣa; the note cites 83.41 for the name. ↩
  84. The reading: mohayitvā is the causative absolutive, 'having caused to be bewildered', and its agent is Mahiṣa (with his army and allies), the subject of vināśitaḥ. I now render it as a participial phrase, as the reviewer asked, to keep the sequence of bewildering and then destruction. The particle tu is weakly contrastive, and I have rendered it with a light 'yet'. I deleted the note on nāma, since it is a common indeclinable meaning 'by name' and needs no note. I kept 'troop' for gaṇa in sasuhṛdgaṇaḥ. ↩
  85. the devas, the asuras and men: The compound sadevāsuramānavaiḥ is read as 'together with gods, asuras and men', in apposition to 'all' (sarvaiḥ), who are the same beings as the dwellers of the three worlds. ↩
  86. The reading: jayetyuktam is impersonal: 'it was said "Victory"'. I render it with an active sense, 'cried out', for the English to parse. The compound sadevāsuramānavaiḥ could also be taken as 'with gods, asuras and men' qualifying the beings, as I have taken it. ↩
  87. hosts of the gods: devatāgaṇa: the gaṇa here are the collective bodies of divinities, not the Goddess's own attendants, which the glossary's gaṇa denotes elsewhere. ↩
  88. The reading: nanāśa (perfect of √naś) means 'perished', 'was lost' or 'vanished'. I had rendered it 'fled', which the Sanskrit does not say; the reviewer is right, and I now use 'perished', which keeps the sense of the army's destruction after Mahiṣa's fall (83.41). 'Vanished' is the other possible reading. hāhākṛtam is neuter and agrees with daityasainyam. I take it as 'having uttered hāhā', not 'made to cry hāhā'. ↩
  89. divine great sages: maharṣi: the great seers of heaven, here called divya, 'divine' or 'celestial', as distinct from earthly sages. ↩
  90. Apsarases: Apsaras: celestial female dancers, companions of the Gandharvas. ↩
  91. The reading: The word divya is rendered 'divine' and construed with maharṣibhiḥ. Gandharvapatayaḥ could also mean 'husbands of the Gandharvas' (of Gandharva women), but 'lords of the Gandharvas' is the natural reading and is chosen. ↩
  92. eighty-third chapter: The GRETIL chapter number here is 79, but the colophon itself says tryaśītitama, 'eighty-third', which agrees with the verse numbering 83.39–44 and the next heading, 'eighty-fourth'. The number 79 appears to be the e-text's own running count. ↩
  93. revered: Translates śrī, an honorific prefixed to the title of the work. ↩
  94. The reading: The chapter number in the header (79) disagrees with the colophon (tryaśītitama, 83rd). I follow the Sanskrit text, which is 83rd. ↩

Adhyāya 84

Sanskrit

चतुरशीतितमो ऽध्यायः- ८४

caturaśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ- 84

English

The eighty-fourth chapter: 84.1

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:2

84.1

Sanskrit

ततः सुरगणाः सर्वे देव्या हन्द्रपुरोगमाः । स्तुतिमारेभिरे कर्तुं निहते महिषासुरे ॥

tataḥ suragaṇāḥ sarve devyā handrapurogamāḥ | stutimārebhire kartuṃ nihate mahiṣāsure ||

English

Then, when Mahiṣāsura had been slain by the Goddess, all the hosts of the suras, with Indra at their head, began to offer a hymn of praise.

84.2

Sanskrit

शक्रादयः सुरगणा निहते ऽतिवीर्ये तस्मिन् दुरात्मनि सुरारिबले च देव्या । तां तुष्टुबुः प्रणतिनम्रशिरोधरांसा वाग्भिः प्रहर्षपुलकोद्गमचारुदेहाः ॥

śakrādayaḥ suragaṇā nihate 'tivīrye tasmin durātmani surāribale ca devyā | tāṃ tuṣṭubuḥ praṇatinamraśirodharāṃsā vāgbhiḥ praharṣapulakodgamacārudehāḥ ||

English

Śakra and the others, the hosts of the suras, praised her with their words, when that exceedingly mighty and evil-minded one and the army of the enemies of the suras3 had been slain by the Goddess. Their necks, shoulders and heads were bowed low in homage, and their bodies were beautiful with the bristling of their hair4 from great joy.5

Sanskrit

देवा ऊचुः

devā ūcuḥ

English

84.3The devas said: We have bowed in devotion to her, Ambikā, who is worshipped by all the devas and great sages: the Goddess by whom this world is pervaded, by her own power6, which takes form as the whole mass of the powers of the hosts of devas without remainder. May she bring about good things for us.7

84.3

Sanskrit

देव्या यया ततमिदं जगदात्मशक्त्या निः शेषदेवगणशक्तिसमूहमूर्त्या । तामम्बिकामखिलदेवमर्षिपूज्यां भक्त्या नताः स्म विदधातु शुभानि सा नः ॥

devyā yayā tatamidaṃ jagadātmaśaktyā niḥ śeṣadevagaṇaśaktisamūhamūrtyā | tāmambikāmakhiladevamarṣipūjyāṃ bhaktyā natāḥ sma vidadhātu śubhāni sā naḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 84.3, above.

84.4

Sanskrit

यस्याः प्रभावमतुलं भगवाननन्तो ब्रह्मा हरश्च नहि वक्तुमलं बलं च । सा चण्डिकाखिलजगत्परिपालनाय नाशाय चाशुभयस्य मतिं करोतु ॥

yasyāḥ prabhāvamatulaṃ bhagavānananto brahmā haraśca nahi vaktumalaṃ balaṃ ca | sā caṇḍikākhilajagatparipālanāya nāśāya cāśubhayasya matiṃ karotu ||

English

May that Caṇḍikā turn her mind to the protection of the whole world and to the destruction of the fear of evil8—she whose incomparable majesty and strength neither the blessed Ananta9, nor Brahmā, nor Hara10 is able to describe.11†

84.5

Sanskrit

या श्रीः स्वयं सुकृतिनां भवनेष्वलक्ष्मीः पापात्मनां कृतधियां हृदयेषु बुद्धिः । श्रद्धां सतां कुलजनप्रभवस्य लज्जा तां त्वां नताः स्म परिपालय देवि विश्वम् ॥

yā śrīḥ svayaṃ sukṛtināṃ bhavaneṣvalakṣmīḥ pāpātmanāṃ kṛtadhiyāṃ hṛdayeṣu buddhiḥ | śraddhāṃ satāṃ kulajanaprabhavasya lajjā tāṃ tvāṃ natāḥ sma paripālaya devi viśvam ||

English

You who are Śrī herself in the houses of the virtuous, Alakṣmī12 [in those] of the evil-minded, Buddhi in the hearts of those of cultivated mind, Śraddhā13 in the good, and Lajjā in one born of a noble family: to you, such as you are, we have bowed. Protect the universe14, Goddess.15

84.6

Sanskrit

किं वर्णयाम तव रूपमचिन्त्यमेतत् किं चातिवीर्यमसुरक्षयकारि भूरि । किं चाहवेषु चरितानि तवाद्भुतानि सर्वेषु देव्यसुरदेवगणादिकेषु ॥

kiṃ varṇayāma tava rūpamacintyametat kiṃ cātivīryamasurakṣayakāri bhūri | kiṃ cāhaveṣu caritāni tavādbhutāni sarveṣu devyasuradevagaṇādikeṣu ||

English

How are we to describe this inconceivable form of yours? How your abundant, surpassing vigor, which brings about the destruction of the asuras16? And how your wondrous deeds in the battles, O Goddess, among all the hosts of asuras, devas and the rest?17

84.7

Sanskrit

हेतुः समस्तजगतां त्रिगुणापि दोषैर् न ज्ञायसे हरिहरादिभिरप्यपारा । सर्वाश्रयाखिलमिदं जगदंशभूतम् अव्याकृता हि परमा प्रकृतिस्त्वमाद्या ॥

hetuḥ samastajagatāṃ triguṇāpi doṣair na jñāyase hariharādibhirapyapārā | sarvāśrayākhilamidaṃ jagadaṃśabhūtam avyākṛtā hi paramā prakṛtistvamādyā ||

English

You are the cause of all the worlds. Though you possess the three guṇas18, you are not known to have faults. You are not known even by Hari, Hara19 and the others, for you are boundless. You are the support of all, and this entire world is a part of you. For you are the unevolved, supreme, primal prakṛti20.21

84.8

Sanskrit

यस्याः समस्तसुरता समुदीरणेन तृप्तिं प्रियाति सकलेषु मखेषु देवि । स्वाहासि वै पितृगणस्य च तृप्तिहेतुर् उच्चार्यसे त्वमत एव जनैः स्वधा च ॥

yasyāḥ samastasuratā samudīraṇena tṛptiṃ priyāti sakaleṣu makheṣu devi | svāhāsi vai pitṛgaṇasya ca tṛptihetur uccāryase tvamata eva janaiḥ svadhā ca ||

English

O Goddess, you are svāhā, by whose utterance the entire body of the gods22 attains satisfaction in all the sacrifices, and you are the cause of satisfaction of the host of ancestors. For this very reason you are uttered by people as svadhā too.23

84.9

Sanskrit

या मुक्तिहेतुरविचिन्त्यमहाव्रता त्वम् अभ्यस्यसे सुनियतेन्द्रियतत्त्वसारैः । मोक्षार्थिभिर्मुनिभिरस्तसमस्तदोषैर् विद्यासि सा भगवती परमा हि देवि ॥

yā muktiheturavicintyamahāvratā tvam abhyasyase suniyatendriyatattvasāraiḥ | mokṣārthibhirmunibhirastasamastadoṣair vidyāsi sā bhagavatī paramā hi devi ||

English

You are the cause of release, and your great vow is inconceivable24. You are practised25 by sages who seek release, whose senses are well restrained, who hold to the essence of the truths26, and who have cast away all faults. You are knowledge, that supreme Blessed One, O Goddess.27

84.10

Sanskrit

शब्दात्मिका सुविमलर्ग्यजुषां निधानम् उद्गीथरम्यपदपाठवतां च साम्नाम् । देवी त्रयी भगवती भवभावनाय वार्ता च सर्वजगतां परमार्तिहन्त्री ॥

śabdātmikā suvimalargyajuṣāṃ nidhānam udgītharamyapadapāṭhavatāṃ ca sāmnām | devī trayī bhagavatī bhavabhāvanāya vārtā ca sarvajagatāṃ paramārtihantrī ||

English

You are of the nature of sound (śabda), the repository of the very pure ṛc and yajus texts and of the sāmans28, whose recitation of words is delightful with the udgītha. You are the Goddess, the trayī29, the Blessed One, for the sustaining of existence; and you are vārtā30, the destroyer of the supreme distress of all the worlds.31

84.11

Sanskrit

मेधासि देवि विदिताखिलशास्त्रसारा दुर्गासि दुर्गभवसागरनौरसङ्गा । श्रीः कैटभारिहृदयैककृताधिवासा गौरी त्वमेव शशिमौलिकृतप्रतिष्ठा ॥

medhāsi devi viditākhilaśāstrasārā durgāsi durgabhavasāgaranaurasaṅgā | śrīḥ kaiṭabhārihṛdayaikakṛtādhivāsā gaurī tvameva śaśimaulikṛtapratiṣṭhā ||

English

You are Medhā, O Goddess, by whom the essence of all the śāstras is known. You are Durgā32, the boat, free of attachment, for the ocean of existence so hard to cross. You are Śrī, who has made her one dwelling in the heart of the foe of Kaiṭabha33. You alone are Gaurī, who has made her abode in the one who wears the moon on his head34.35

84.12

Sanskrit

ईषत्सहासममलं परिपूर्णचन्द्रबिम्बानुकारि कनकोत्तमकान्तिकान्तम् । अत्यद्भुतं प्रहृतमात्तरुषा तथापि वक्त्रं विलोक्य सहसा महिषासुरेण ॥

īṣatsahāsamamalaṃ paripūrṇacandrabimbānukāri kanakottamakāntikāntam | atyadbhutaṃ prahṛtamāttaruṣā tathāpi vaktraṃ vilokya sahasā mahiṣāsureṇa ||

English

84.12–13Even so, a blow was struck36 suddenly by Mahiṣāsura, who was seized by anger, on seeing your face, faintly smiling, spotless, resembling the orb of the full moon, lovely with the radiance of the finest gold, and utterly wondrous. And, O Devī, that Mahiṣa did not at once give up his life-breaths on seeing that [face] enraged, terrible with knitted brows, with a luster like that of the rising moon. That is exceedingly strange. For who can live on seeing Antaka37 enraged?38

84.13

Sanskrit

दृष्ट्वा तु देवि कुपितं भ्रुकुटीकरालम् उद्यच्छशाङ्कसदृशच्छवि यन्न सद्यः । प्राणान्मुमोच महीषस्तदतीव चित्रं कैर्जोव्यते हि कुपितान्तकदर्शनेन ॥

dṛṣṭvā tu devi kupitaṃ bhrukuṭīkarālam udyacchaśāṅkasadṛśacchavi yanna sadyaḥ | prāṇānmumoca mahīṣastadatīva citraṃ kairjovyate hi kupitāntakadarśanena ||

English

Its English is given with 84.12–13, above.

84.14

Sanskrit

देवि प्रसीद परमा भवती भवाय सद्यो विनाशयसि कोपवती कुलानि । विज्ञातमेतदधुनैव यदस्तमेतन् नीतं बलं सुविपुलं महिषासुरस्य ॥

devi prasīda paramā bhavatī bhavāya sadyo vināśayasi kopavatī kulāni | vijñātametadadhunaiva yadastametan nītaṃ balaṃ suvipulaṃ mahiṣāsurasya ||

English

O Devī, be gracious. You are supreme, for the welfare of the world39; when you are angry you destroy whole families40 at once. This has been understood just now, in that the exceedingly vast strength of Mahiṣāsura has been brought to destruction.41

84.15

Sanskrit

ते संमता जनपदेषु धनानि तेषां तेषां यशांसि न च सीदति धर्मवर्गः । धन्यास्त एव निभृतात्मजभृत्यदारा येषां सदाभ्युदयदा भवती प्रसन्ना ॥

te saṃmatā janapadeṣu dhanāni teṣāṃ teṣāṃ yaśāṃsi na ca sīdati dharmavargaḥ | dhanyāsta eva nibhṛtātmajabhṛtyadārā yeṣāṃ sadābhyudayadā bhavatī prasannā ||

English

They are honoured in the realms; theirs is wealth, theirs is fame, and their dharma-group42 does not decline. They alone are blessed, whose sons, servants and wives are steadfast43, and with whom you, the giver of prosperity, are always pleased.44

84.16

Sanskrit

धर्म्याणि देवि सकलानि सदैव कर्माण्य् अत्यादृतः प्रतिदिनं सुकृती करोति । स्वर्गं प्रयाति च ततो भवतीप्रसादाल् लोकत्रये ऽपि फलदा ननु देवि तेन ॥

dharmyāṇi devi sakalāni sadaiva karmāṇy atyādṛtaḥ pratidinaṃ sukṛtī karoti | svargaṃ prayāti ca tato bhavatīprasādāl lokatraye 'pi phaladā nanu devi tena ||

English

O Devī, the righteous person of good deeds45, highly devoted, performs every dharma-conforming rite always, day after day; and then, by your grace, he goes to heaven. Surely, O Devī, you are therefore the giver of fruit even in the three worlds46.47

84.17

Sanskrit

दुर्गे स्मृता हरसि भीतिमशेषजन्तोः स्वस्थैः स्मृता मतिमतीव शुभां ददासि । दारिद्रयदुः खभयहारिणि का त्वदन्या सर्वोपकारकरणाय सदार्ऽऽद्रचित्ता ॥

durge smṛtā harasi bhītimaśeṣajantoḥ svasthaiḥ smṛtā matimatīva śubhāṃ dadāsi | dāridrayaduḥ khabhayahāriṇi kā tvadanyā sarvopakārakaraṇāya sadār''dracittā ||

English

O Durgā, when remembered you remove the fear of every living creature; when remembered by those who are at ease48, you give a most auspicious mind. O remover of poverty, suffering and fear, who other than you has a heart always moved with compassion, to do good to all?49

84.18

Sanskrit

एभिर्हतैर्जगदुपैति सुखं तथैते कुर्वन्तु नाम नरकाय चिराय पापम् । संग्राममृत्युमधिगम्य दिवं प्रयान्तु मत्वेति नूनमहितान् विनिहंसि देवि ॥

ebhirhatairjagadupaiti sukhaṃ tathaite kurvantu nāma narakāya cirāya pāpam | saṃgrāmamṛtyumadhigamya divaṃ prayāntu matveti nūnamahitān vinihaṃsi devi ||

English

The world gains happiness when these are slain; and let these too, if they will50, go on committing sin for a long time, sin that leads to hell; having gained death in battle51, let them go to heaven. Surely thinking thus, O Devī, you strike down your enemies.52

84.19

Sanskrit

दृष्ट्वैव किं न भवती प्रकरोति भस्म सर्वासुरानरिषु यत्प्रहिणोषि शस्त्रम् । लोकान् प्रयान्तु रिपवो ऽपि हि शस्त्रपूता इत्थं मतिर्भवति तेष्वपि ते ऽतिसाध्वी ॥

dṛṣṭvaiva kiṃ na bhavatī prakaroti bhasma sarvāsurānariṣu yatprahiṇoṣi śastram | lokān prayāntu ripavo 'pi hi śastrapūtā itthaṃ matirbhavati teṣvapi te 'tisādhvī ||

English

Why do you not reduce all the asuras53 to ashes by a mere glance, when you hurl a weapon at your enemies? Even enemies, purified by the weapon, may go to the [higher] worlds54. Such is your intention, exceedingly virtuous55, even toward them.56

84.20

Sanskrit

खड्गप्रभानिकरविस्फुरणैस्तथोग्रैः शूलाग्रकान्तिनिवहेन दृशो ऽसुराणाम् । यन्नागता विलयमंशुमदिन्दुखण्डयोग्याननं तव विलोकयतां तदेतत् ॥

khaḍgaprabhānikaravisphuraṇaistathograiḥ śūlāgrakāntinivahena dṛśo 'surāṇām | yannāgatā vilayamaṃśumadindukhaṇḍayogyānanaṃ tava vilokayatāṃ tadetat ||

English

That the eyes of the asuras, as they gazed at your face, which is fit to be likened to a bright fragment of the moon57, did not come to destruction from the fierce flashes of masses of sword-radiance, and likewise from the mass of lustre at the tips of the tridents, is due to this58.59

84.21

Sanskrit

दुर्वृत्तवृत्तशमनं तव देवि शीलं रूपं तथैतदविचिन्त्यमतुल्यमन्यैः । वीर्यं च हन्तृ हृतदेवपराक्रमाणां वैरिष्वपि प्रकटितैव दया त्वयेत्थम् ॥

durvṛttavṛttaśamanaṃ tava devi śīlaṃ rūpaṃ tathaitadavicintyamatulyamanyaiḥ | vīryaṃ ca hantṛ hṛtadevaparākramāṇāṃ vairiṣvapi prakaṭitaiva dayā tvayettham ||

English

O Devī, your character quells the conduct of evildoers; likewise this form of yours is inconceivable and unequalled by others; and your vigor is the slayer of those who have taken away the prowess of the devas. In this way you have made your compassion manifest even toward your enemies.60

84.22

Sanskrit

केनोपमा भवतु ते ऽस्य पराक्रमस्य रूपं च शत्रुभयकार्यतिहारि कुत्र । चित्ते कृपा समरनिष्ठुरता च दृष्टा त्वय्येव देवि वरदे भुवनत्रये ऽपि ॥

kenopamā bhavatu te 'sya parākramasya rūpaṃ ca śatrubhayakāryatihāri kutra | citte kṛpā samaraniṣṭhuratā ca dṛṣṭā tvayyeva devi varade bhuvanatraye 'pi ||

English

With what could there be a comparison for this prowess of yours? And where is there a form that causes fear to enemies and yet is exceedingly captivating61? Compassion in the heart and ruthlessness in battle have been seen in you alone, O Devī, giver of boons, even in the three worlds.62

84.23

Sanskrit

त्रैलोक्यमेतदखिलं रिपुनाशनेन त्रातं त्वया समरमूर्धनि ते ऽपि हत्वा । नीता दिवं रिपुगणा भयमप्यपास्तम् अस्माकमुन्मदसुरारिभवं नमस्ते ॥

trailokyametadakhilaṃ ripunāśanena trātaṃ tvayā samaramūrdhani te 'pi hatvā | nītā divaṃ ripugaṇā bhayamapyapāstam asmākamunmadasurāribhavaṃ namaste ||

English

This entire three-worlds has been protected by you at the head of battle, through the destruction of [your] enemies. Those hosts of enemies too, once you had slain them, were led to heaven; and our fear, born of the frenzied enemies of the gods63, has also been cast away. Salutation to you.64

84.24

Sanskrit

शूलेन पाहि नो देवि पाहि खड्गेन चाम्बिके । घण्टास्वनेन नः पाहि चापज्यानिः स्वनेन च ॥

śūlena pāhi no devi pāhi khaḍgena cāmbike | ghaṇṭāsvanena naḥ pāhi cāpajyāniḥ svanena ca ||

English

With the trident protect us, O Devī; with the sword protect us, O Ambikā65; protect us with the sound of your bell, and with the twang of your bowstring66.67

84.25

Sanskrit

प्राच्यां रक्ष प्रतीच्यां च चण्डिके रक्ष दक्षिणे । भ्रामणेनात्मशूलस्य उत्तरस्यां तथेश्वरि ॥

prācyāṃ rakṣa pratīcyāṃ ca caṇḍike rakṣa dakṣiṇe | bhrāmaṇenātmaśūlasya uttarasyāṃ tatheśvari ||

English

Protect us in the east, and in the west; protect us in the south, O Caṇḍikā; and likewise in the north, O Īśvarī68, by the whirling of your own spear69.70

84.26

Sanskrit

सौम्यानि यानि रूपाणि त्रैलोक्ये विचरन्ति ते । यानि चात्यर्थघोराणि तै रक्षास्मांस्तथा भुवम् ॥

saumyāni yāni rūpāṇi trailokye vicaranti te | yāni cātyarthaghorāṇi tai rakṣāsmāṃstathā bhuvam ||

English

With those gentle forms71 of yours that move about in the three worlds, and with those that are exceedingly terrible, protect us, and likewise the earth.

84.27

Sanskrit

खड्गशूलगदादीनि यानि चास्त्राणि ते ऽम्बिके । करपल्लवसङ्गीनि तैरस्मान् रक्ष सर्वतः ॥

khaḍgaśūlagadādīni yāni cāstrāṇi te 'mbike | karapallavasaṅgīni tairasmān rakṣa sarvataḥ ||

English

And with those weapons of yours, O Ambikā72, beginning with the sword, spear and mace, which cling to the tender-leaf-like sprays of your hands73, protect us on every side.74

Sanskrit

ऋषीरुवाच

ṛṣīruvāca

English

84.28The ṛṣi said: Thus praised by the suras, the Sustainer of the worlds75 was worshipped with divine flowers born in Nandana76, and likewise with scented unguents,77†

84.28

Sanskrit

एवं स्तुता सुरैर्दिव्यैः कुसुमैर्नन्दनोद्भवैः । अर्चिता जगतां धात्री तथा गन्धानुलेपनैः ॥

evaṃ stutā surairdivyaiḥ kusumairnandanodbhavaiḥ | arcitā jagatāṃ dhātrī tathā gandhānulepanaiḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 84.28, above.

84.29

Sanskrit

भक्त्या समस्तैस्त्रिदशार्दिव्यैर्धूपैस्तु धूपिता । प्राह प्रसादसुमुखी समस्तान् प्रणतान् सुरान् ॥

bhaktyā samastaistridaśārdivyairdhūpaistu dhūpitā | prāha prasādasumukhī samastān praṇatān surān ||

English

Censed with divine incense by all the devas in devotion, she, her face made gracious by favour, spoke to all the suras who had bowed down before her.78

Sanskrit

देव्युवाच

devyuvāca

English

84.30The Devī79 said: Let all of you, O tridaśas80, choose whatever you desire from me. Well worshipped by these hymns of praise, I give it with great joy.

84.30

Sanskrit

व्रियतां त्रिदशाः सर्वे यदस्मत्तो ऽभिवाञ्छितम् । ददाम्यहमतिप्रीत्या स्तवैरेभिः सुपूजिता ॥

vriyatāṃ tridaśāḥ sarve yadasmatto 'bhivāñchitam | dadāmyahamatiprītyā stavairebhiḥ supūjitā ||

English

Its English is given with 84.30, above.

84.31

Sanskrit

कर्तव्यमपरं यच्च दुष्करं तन्न विद्महे । इत्याकर्ण्ये वचो देव्याः प्रत्यूचुस्ते दिवौकसः ॥

kartavyamaparaṃ yacca duṣkaraṃ tanna vidmahe | ityākarṇye vaco devyāḥ pratyūcuste divaukasaḥ ||

English

"Whatever else is to be done, and is hard to do, that we do not know." Having heard these words of the Devī, those dwellers in heaven replied.81

Sanskrit

देवा ऊचुः

devā ūcuḥ

English

The devas said:

84.32

Sanskrit

भगवत्या कृतं सर्वं न किञ्चिदवशिष्यते । यदयं निहतः शत्रुरस्माकं महिषासुरः ॥

bhagavatyā kṛtaṃ sarvaṃ na kiñcidavaśiṣyate | yadayaṃ nihataḥ śatrurasmākaṃ mahiṣāsuraḥ ||

English

Everything has been done by the Blessed One; nothing remains [to be done], since this Mahiṣāsura, our enemy, has been slain.82

84.33

Sanskrit

यदि चापि वरो देयस्त्वयास्माकं महेश्वरि । संस्मृता संस्मृता त्वं नो हिंसेथाः परमापदः ॥

yadi cāpi varo deyastvayāsmākaṃ maheśvari | saṃsmṛtā saṃsmṛtā tvaṃ no hiṃsethāḥ paramāpadaḥ ||

English

84.33–34And if a boon is to be given to us by you, O Maheśvarī83, may you, each time you are remembered, destroy our greatest calamities. And whatever mortal will praise you with these hymns of praise, O you of spotless face, be gracious to him always, O Ambikā84, for this reason, for the increase of his endowments, wealth, wife85 and the rest, through riches, prosperity and power.86

84.34

Sanskrit

यश्च मर्त्यः स्तवैरेभिस्त्वां स्तोष्यत्यमलानने । तस्य वित्तर्धिविभवैर्धनदारादिसम्पदाम् । वृद्धये ऽस्मात्प्रसन्ना त्वं भवेथाः सर्वदाम्बिके ॥

yaśca martyaḥ stavairebhistvāṃ stoṣyatyamalānane | tasya vittardhivibhavairdhanadārādisampadām | vṛddhaye 'smātprasannā tvaṃ bhavethāḥ sarvadāmbike ||

English

Its English is given with 84.33–34, above.

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

84.35

Sanskrit

इति प्रसादिता देवैर्जगतोर्ऽथे तथाऽत्मनः । तथेत्युक्त्वा भद्रकाली बभूवान्तर्हिता नृप ॥

iti prasāditā devairjagator'the tathā'tmanaḥ | tathetyuktvā bhadrakālī babhūvāntarhitā nṛpa ||

English

Thus propitiated by the devas, for the sake of the world and likewise for her own sake87, Bhadrakālī88 said, "So be it," and vanished from sight, O king.89

84.36

Sanskrit

इत्येतत्कथितं भूप सम्भूता सा यथा पुरा । देवी देवशरीरेभ्यो जगत्त्रयहितैषिणी ॥

ityetatkathitaṃ bhūpa sambhūtā sā yathā purā | devī devaśarīrebhyo jagattrayahitaiṣiṇī ||

English

84.36–38Thus this has been told, O king: how the Devī, who desires the welfare of the three worlds90, was born in former times from the bodies of the devas; and how she, the benefactress of the devas, arose again from the body of Gaurī91, for the slaying of the wicked Daityas and likewise of Śumbha and Niśumbha, and for the protection of the worlds. Listen to that, as told by me; I relate it to you exactly.92

84.37

Sanskrit

पुनश्च गौरीदेहात् सा समुद्भूता यथाभवत् । वधाय दुष्टदैत्यानां तथा शुम्भनिशुम्भयोः ॥

punaśca gaurīdehāt sā samudbhūtā yathābhavat | vadhāya duṣṭadaityānāṃ tathā śumbhaniśumbhayoḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 84.36–38, above.

84.38

Sanskrit

रक्षणाय च लोकानां देवानामुपकारिणी । तच्छृणुष्व मयाऽख्यातं यथावत्कथयामि ते ॥

rakṣaṇāya ca lokānāṃ devānāmupakāriṇī | tacchṛṇuṣva mayā'khyātaṃ yathāvatkathayāmi te ||

English

Its English is given with 84.36–38, above.

Sanskrit

इति श्रीमार्कण्डेयमहापुराणे सावर्णिके मन्वन्तरे देवीमाहत्म्ये चतुरशीतितमो ऽद्यायः

iti śrīmārkaṇḍeyamahāpurāṇe sāvarṇike manvantare devīmāhatmye caturaśītitamo 'dyāyaḥ

English

Thus ends the eighty-fourth chapter93, the Devīmāhātmya94, in the Sāvarṇika manvantara, in the revered Mārkaṇḍeya Mahāpurāṇa.95

  1. The reading: The line is a chapter heading only: 'caturaśītitamaḥ adhyāyaḥ' with the numeral '84' repeated in Devanagari and IAST. The dash after 'adhyāyaḥ' is a GRETIL formatting device. The preceding colophon (670.1) calls the chapter the eighty-third, so the numbering of the verse labels (83.x, 670.x, 671.x) is inconsistent in the e-text; I have rendered the Sanskrit as given. ↩
  2. The reading: The text reads 'handrapurogamāḥ', a misprint for 'indrapurogamāḥ' ('led by Indra'), which the sense and the following verses confirm. 'devyā' is instrumental, so I took it with 'nihate' ('slain by the Goddess'). The alternative is to read it as a misprint for the genitive 'devyāḥ', 'a hymn of praise to the Goddess'; I kept the instrumental as the text has it. ↩
  3. enemies of the suras: Sura-ari, the asuras (here the Daitya host of Mahiṣa), the traditional foes of the gods. ↩
  4. bristling of their hair: Pulaka, the standing of the body-hair on end, a sign of intense joy or devotion. ↩
  5. The reading: The compound praṇatinamraśirodharāṃsāḥ is read as 'with necks/heads and shoulders bowed low in homage' (śirodhara 'neck', or 'head and neck'; aṃsa 'shoulder'). Syntactically the verse has a locative absolute (nihate ... tasmin durātmani ... surāribale ca), which I render as a 'when' clause with devyā as agent. The text's 'sura' in 'suragaṇāḥ' is a plain apposition to Śakrādayaḥ. ↩
  6. by her own power: ātmaśaktyā stands in apposition to yayā and is itself qualified by the compound ending in mūrtyā. The Goddess's own power is said to be the embodied sum of the powers of all the gods, which is how she was formed in the Devīmāhātmya. ↩
  7. The reading: The printed compound akhiladevamarṣipūjyām is irregular: the m is not a regular sandhi. I read it as akhila-deva-maharṣi-pūjyām, 'worshipped by all the devas and great sages', with maharṣi shortened for the metre. This is the reading I take the line to intend. The alternative, akhiladeva-ṛṣi, 'all the devas and sages', differs little in sense. Also, natāḥ sma can be rendered as a present ('we bow') or a perfect ('we have bowed'). I chose the perfect to reflect the participle with sma. ↩
  8. the fear of evil: The text reads aśubhayasya; the sense requires aśubhabhayasya, 'of the fear of the inauspicious'. ↩
  9. Ananta: 'The Endless', a name of the cosmic serpent Śeṣa, Viṣṇu's couch. ↩
  10. Hara: 'The Remover', a name of Śiva. ↩
  11. The reading: The printed aśubhayasya is probably a corruption or misprint of aśubhabhayasya ('of the fear of evil/the inauspicious'), which the metre and sense support. I translated that reading. Taken literally, aśubhayasya has no clear meaning. ↩
  12. Alakṣmī: 'Misfortune' or 'Ill-fortune', the opposite of Śrī (Lakṣmī); she is the goddess of poverty and ruin. ↩
  13. Śraddhā: Faith, trust, reverent confidence; here a form of the Goddess, like Śrī, Buddhi and Lajjā. ↩
  14. Protect the universe: paripālaya is an imperative addressed to the Goddess (devi); viśvam is its object. ↩
  15. The reading: 1) śraddhāṃ is accusative, but the syntax needs a nominative parallel to śrīḥ, alakṣmīḥ, buddhiḥ and lajjā. I read śraddhā, as in the usual text of the hymn, and take the accusative as a misprint. 2) alakṣmīḥ stands after sukṛtināṃ bhavaneṣu and before pāpātmanām. I supply 'in those' and take pāpātmanām as the possessor of the houses (the evil-minded), balancing sukṛtinām. 3) kṛtadhiyām could also mean 'of those of resolved mind' or 'of the learned'. I chose 'of cultivated mind', a literal 'those whose intellect has been made or trained'. ↩
  16. asuras: Here the asuras are the demonic enemies of the devas, whom the Goddess destroyed in battle. ↩
  17. The reading: The compound asuradevagaṇādikeṣu ("among the hosts of asuras, devas and the rest") is read as locative plural: 'among all the hosts of asuras and devas and the others'. The word sarveṣu qualifies the whole compound. The sense could also be 'in all battles, among the asuras, the host of devas, and others', but I kept the more natural reading. The verse has no verb: kiṃ varṇayāma governs all three clauses, with the second and third kiṃ ca clauses elliptical (varṇayāma understood); I supplied 'How' and 'how' accordingly. ↩
  18. guṇas: The three constituent strands of nature in Sāṃkhya thought: sattva, rajas and tamas. The 'faults' (doṣa) are defects in general, such as passion and delusion. ↩
  19. Hara: 'The Remover', a name of Śiva. ↩
  20. prakṛti: Here primal Nature, the root-substance from which the world evolves, not 'subjects' as elsewhere. avyākṛtā, 'unevolved', means undifferentiated, not yet developed into distinct forms. ↩
  21. The reading: doṣair na jñāyase: I now follow the reviewer and read 'you are not known to have faults', i.e. you are untainted though you possess the three guṇas. I drop my earlier alternative, which attached the faults to Hari, Hara and the others, as unnatural. The sense 'not known by faults' is rejected as obscure. In na jñāyase … apārā I take apārā as the reason for the preceding statement, as the reviewer suggests. jagadaṃśabhūtam: 'is a part of you', as the participle needs no later transformation. hi is rendered by 'For'. ↩
  22. the entire body of the gods: Sanskrit samastasuratā, literally 'the state of being all the gods', i.e. the gods taken together as a whole. ↩
  23. The reading: priyāti is not a regular form; it is probably a misprint or corruption for prayāti, 'goes to, attains'. I read tṛptiṃ prayāti, 'attains satisfaction'. samastasuratā is read as samasta-sura-tā, 'the totality of the gods'. Another reading, 'godhood of all', is less natural with the verb of being satisfied. ↩
  24. your great vow is inconceivable: The compound avicintyamahāvratā is read as a bahuvrīhi qualifying the Goddess. Vrata here means a sacred undertaking or discipline. ↩
  25. You are practised: abhyasyase is passive, 'you are repeatedly practised or cultivated'. It implies constant meditation on her as knowledge. ↩
  26. who hold to the essence of the truths: The compound suniyatendriyatattvasāraiḥ is open to more than one analysis. See doubts. ↩
  27. The reading: suniyatendriyatattvasāraiḥ can be read in two ways. (a) 'whose senses are well restrained and whose essence/goal is the truth (tattva)'. (b) 'for whom the restrained senses and the essence of the principles are [their practice]'. I chose (a), rendering it as 'senses well restrained, who hold to the essence of the truths', because it fits munibhiḥ as a descriptive epithet. Also, avicintyamahāvratā could be taken as 'of inconceivable great vows', or as 'whose great vow is inconceivable'. I chose the second. 'Vidyā' is rendered 'knowledge', following the glossary. ↩
  28. sāmans: ṛc are the verses of the Ṛgveda, yajus the sacrificial formulas of the Yajurveda, sāman the chanted melodies of the Sāmaveda. The udgītha is the loud chant sung by the Udgātṛ priests, the principal part of a sāman. ↩
  29. trayī: The 'triple' knowledge, that is the three Vedas (Ṛg, Yajus, Sāman), here identified with the Goddess. ↩
  30. vārtā: The science of livelihood (agriculture, cattle-keeping and trade), which sustains life. Here the Goddess is identified with it. ↩
  31. The reading: Bhavabhāvanāya: bhava may mean 'existence, the world' (chosen), or Bhava as a name of Śiva; 'for the sustaining of existence' fits the context. Also, trayī could be taken as a predicate in apposition to devī (chosen), and vārtā as 'livelihood' or as 'news, tidings'; 'livelihood' is the standard sense with trayī. ↩
  32. Durgā: The name is linked with durga, 'hard to pass, difficult', which is taken up in the phrase durgabhavasāgara, 'the ocean of existence so hard to cross'. ↩
  33. the foe of Kaiṭabha: Kaiṭabhāri, a name of Viṣṇu, who slew the asura Kaiṭabha. Śrī, as Lakṣmī, dwells on his chest. ↩
  34. the one who wears the moon on his head: Śaśimauli, 'moon-crested', a name of Śiva. Gaurī is his consort. ↩
  35. The reading: (1) viditākhilaśāstrasārā: it may mean 'by whom the essence of all the śāstras is known' (Medhā as the faculty of grasping), or 'whose essence, known through all the śāstras'. I chose the first as better fitting Medhā, intelligence. (2) naur asaṅgā: asaṅgā may mean 'unattached, unobstructed' (a boat that does not stick or fail), or 'without a companion'. I chose 'free of attachment'. ↩
  36. a blow was struck: prahṛtam is an impersonal passive participle, with Mahiṣāsura as agent. The object of the blow is not stated; it is the Goddess, or her face. ↩
  37. Antaka: 'the Ender', a name of Yama, god of death. ↩
  38. The reading: 1. Sahasā ('suddenly') may go with vilokya ('suddenly seeing') or with prahṛtam ('struck suddenly'). I took it with the blow. 2. The text has mahīṣaḥ with a long ī in 84.13; I read it as Mahiṣa (metrical or scribal lengthening). 3. Kupitaṃ in 84.13 agrees with vaktram ('face') from 84.12, not with Mahiṣa. This matches the contrast with the smiling face of 84.12, so I read the enraged face as hers. The grammar would also allow it to describe the angry Mahiṣa, but that reading fits the sense less well. 4. Jovyate is read as jīvyate, an impersonal passive ('by whom is it lived?'). ↩
  39. for the welfare of the world: bhavāya may also be read as 'for existence' or 'for prosperity'; the object of the welfare is not stated, so 'of the world' is supplied from the context of the hymn. ↩
  40. families: kulāni: 'families' or 'lineages', here the families of the asuras and any who oppose the Goddess. ↩
  41. The reading: bhavāya (dative) can be read as 'for existence/the world's welfare' or 'for prosperity'; I chose 'for the welfare of the world', with the first clause as a statement that she is supreme for that purpose. Bhavatī is taken as the pronoun 'you' (feminine, honorific), not as a verb. Astam etat nītam: 'brought to destruction' (literally 'brought to setting/end'). Kopavatī: 'when angry'. The syntax of paramā bhavatī bhavāya is compressed and could also be read 'you, supreme, [are] for the welfare [of all]'. ↩
  42. dharma-group: dharmavarga is the set or body of things belonging to dharma, that is, the pious acts and duties. It is sometimes read as the three aims of life (trivarga), in which dharma comes first. ↩
  43. steadfast: nibhṛta is a compound adjective qualifying sons, servants and wives. It means humble, steady or devoted. ↩
  44. The reading: dharmavarga: read either as the group of pious acts or as the trivarga headed by dharma; I took the first as the simpler sense. nibhṛta: 'humble', 'devoted' or 'steady'; I chose 'steadfast'. Bhavatī is the respectful 'you' addressed to the Goddess. ↩
  45. the righteous person of good deeds: Sanskrit sukṛtī, one who has done good deeds (sukṛta); the subject of both 'performs' and 'goes'. ↩
  46. the three worlds: lokatraya: heaven, earth and the netherworld (or atmosphere). ↩
  47. The reading: The word 'tena' at the end is read as 'therefore' (by that reason), referring to the preceding statement; 'phaladā' is taken as a predicate of bhavatī (understood), 'you are the giver of fruit'. 'Atyādṛtaḥ' is taken as 'highly respectful/devoted' (pious), qualifying the sukṛtin. ↩
  48. those who are at ease: svasthaiḥ (instrumental plural) can mean 'the healthy', 'the self-possessed' or 'the prosperous'. The sense is that even those who are not in distress remember the Goddess, and she gives them a good mind. ↩
  49. The reading: svasthaiḥ: 'healthy', 'self-possessed' or 'well-off'. I chose 'at ease', which covers these and contrasts with the afflicted of the first half. The e-text's 'dāridraya' is a misprint for dāridrya, and 'sadār''dracittā' stands for sadā ārdracittā, 'always soft-hearted'. I read both so. ↩
  50. if they will: nāma with the imperative kurvantu is concessive: 'let them sin, if they choose'. ↩
  51. death in battle: A warrior who dies facing the enemy in battle was believed to reach heaven. The verse gives this as the Goddess's supposed reason for killing them. ↩
  52. The reading: I accept the first finding: in 'tathā ete' the 'ete' are the same enemies slain in the first half, so I drop '[others]' and write 'these too'. I accept the second: 'nāma' is rendered 'if they will', not 'indeed'. On the third, 'narakāya' is a dative of purpose or result, 'sin that leads to hell', and 'cirāya' is a dative of duration, 'for a long time'. The sense is that although their sin would last long enough to condemn them to hell, death in battle sends them to heaven. 'Matvā iti' is rendered 'thinking thus', giving the Goddess's presumed thought. ↩
  53. asuras: The asuras are the enemies of the gods, here the followers of Mahiṣa. ↩
  54. [higher] worlds: The text has only 'worlds' (lokān); the sense, as the preceding verse about going to heaven shows, is the worlds won by those who die in battle. ↩
  55. exceedingly virtuous: atisādhvī is feminine, agreeing with matiḥ, 'intention'; it is not the glossary's sādhvī, 'faithful wife'. ↩
  56. The reading: 'prakaroti bhasma' is read as 'reduce to ashes' (bhasma with prakaroti, 'make into ash'). 'sarvāsurān' is taken as sarva + asurān. 'Lokān' is left without a qualifier in the Sanskrit; the bracketed 'higher' is supplied from the context of 84.18 (heaven for those slain in battle). ↩
  57. fit to be likened to a bright fragment of the moon: The compound aṃśumad-indu-khaṇḍa-yogya-ānana can also be read as 'a face suited to [i.e. resembling] a piece of the radiant moon'. ↩
  58. is due to this: The sentence is built as yad ... tad etat, 'that ... [is] this'. The verse does not say what 'this' stands for. The sense is that the asuras' eyes were spared because they were fixed on the Goddess's face, which was as gentle as the moon. ↩
  59. The reading: 1) nāgatā (na āgatā) is feminine singular, but its subject dṛśaḥ is plural; I read it as a loose or misprinted agreement for nāgatāḥ and translated it as plural. 2) yogya in the compound 'yogyānanam' could mean 'fit for/worthy of' or 'comparable to'; I chose 'fit to be likened to', which suits the context of the moon-like face. 3) vilayam means 'dissolution' or 'destruction'; I took it as the destruction of the eyes (blinding), and the genitive vilokayatām as agreeing with asurāṇām. ↩
  60. The reading: hṛtadevaparākramāṇām: I take it as a compound meaning 'those by whom the prowess of the gods was taken away' (the asuras who robbed the gods). It could also be read as 'those whose prowess has been taken', but the former suits the context of the gods' defeat. hantṛ is neuter, agreeing with vīryam. Also, anyaiḥ with atulyam is taken as 'unequalled by others'. ↩
  61. exceedingly captivating: Sanskrit śatrubhayakāryatihāri splits as śatrubhayakāri (causing fear to enemies) and atihāri (very charming, ravishing). The two qualities are set side by side, as in the compassion and ruthlessness of the next line. ↩
  62. The reading: atihāri: it could be read as 'exceedingly captivating' (ati + hārin) or as 'removing fear to excess'. I chose 'captivating' because it gives the contrast with śatrubhayakāri, 'causing fear to enemies', and it matches the contrast of compassion and harshness in the next line. ↩
  63. enemies of the gods: Surāri (sura + ari) means the asuras, the foes of the gods. It is not to be read as 'enemy of surā', wine. ↩
  64. The reading: Hatvā ("having slain") has no stated agent. I take it as Durgā, the subject of the preceding tvayā, and nītā as passive, so that she slew the enemies and they were led to heaven. Unmadasurāribhavam could also be read as 'born of the intoxicated enemies of the gods'. I chose 'frenzied' for unmada, which means 'mad' or 'arrogant', because it suits the asuras' rage in battle. ↩
  65. Ambikā: 'Little mother', a name of the Goddess (Durgā/Caṇḍikā). ↩
  66. twang of your bowstring: Sanskrit cāpajyāniḥ svanena: cāpa-jyā-niḥ-svana, 'the sound of the bow's string'; the reading follows the edition's text (cāpajyāniḥ), which is likely a corrupt form of cāpajyā-niḥsvanena. ↩
  67. The reading: The e-text reads cāpajyāniḥ svanena ca, which is metrically and grammatically awkward. I take it as a corrupt form of cāpajyā-niḥsvanena ('with the twang of the bowstring'), the sense required by the parallel with ghaṇṭāsvanena. ↩
  68. O Īśvarī: 'Sovereign Lady', a name of the Goddess, feminine of Īśvara. ↩
  69. by the whirling of your own spear: The instrumental phrase bhrāmaṇenātmaśūlasya may be construed with all four quarters; it is placed here with the last, as the verse's word order suggests. ↩
  70. The reading: The Sanskrit reads bhrāmaṇenātmaśūlasya (genitive); the instrumental bhrāmaṇena governs the genitive śūlasya, 'by the whirling of the spear'. ātma- is taken as 'your own', referring to the Goddess. The instrumental may govern all four quarters, but I attach it to the last, as the word order suggests. ↩
  71. gentle forms: saumyāni and atyarthaghorāṇi both qualify rūpāṇi; the verse contrasts the Goddess's benign and her dreadful forms, and tai ("with those") takes up both. ↩
  72. Ambikā: 'Little Mother', a name of the Goddess. ↩
  73. tender-leaf-like sprays of your hands: The compound karapallava is read as a simile: the hands are compared to tender shoots, and the weapons are said to cling to them. ↩
  74. The reading: karapallavasaṅgīni: it can be read as a karmadhāraya, 'hands that are like sprouts' (chosen), or as a tatpuruṣa, 'clinging to the sprouts of the hands'. Either gives the same sense. The form saṅgīni (from saṅgin, 'clinging') is the reading of the text, and I keep it. ↩
  75. Sustainer of the worlds: Dhātrī, 'she who bears, nurses or sustains', here the Goddess; the word can also mean 'nurse' or 'mother'. ↩
  76. Nandana: Indra's pleasure garden in heaven, whose flowers are divine. ↩
  77. The reading: 1) ṛṣīruvāca is the sandhi form of ṛṣir uvāca ('the ṛṣi said'), and I read it so. 2) divyaiḥ is syntactically ambiguous: it could qualify surair ('divine gods') or kusumair ('divine flowers'). I follow the reviewer and take it with the flowers, since 84.29 has the same construction with dhūpaiḥ (divyair dhūpaiḥ, 'divine incense'), where tridaśaiḥ is separate. 3) gandhānulepanaiḥ may be a dvandva ('perfumes and unguents') or a tatpuruṣa ('scented unguents'). I chose the latter, as the reviewer suggests, with tathā ('likewise') linking it to the flowers. 4) The reviewer asks for a dash or comma at the end instead of a full stop. I end with a comma before ' //', because the sentence continues in 84.29 and the series rule requires the closing ' //'. ↩
  78. The reading: The printed 'samastaistridaśārdivyair' does not parse as it stands. I read it as samastaiḥ tridaśair divyair, with 'tridaśair' for 'tridaśār': 'by all the devas, with divine [incense]'. The glossary's Tridaśāri (Cāmara) does not fit, since the enemy of the devas would not be censing the Goddess. The subject is the Goddess, jagatāṃ dhātrī of 84.28. The participle 'dhūpitā' means 'fumigated' or 'censed with incense', and I kept that sense. ↩
  79. The Devī: The Goddess, Caṇḍikā/Ambikā, who has just been praised by the gods. ↩
  80. tridaśas: 'The thirty', i.e. the devas, who are traditionally thirty-three in number, counted here as thirty. The vocative addresses the gods who have just praised her. ↩
  81. The reading: The first half-verse has no speaker's marker. The verb vidmahe is first person plural, which suits the devas, but ity ākarṇya vaco devyāḥ ('having heard the words of the Devī') seems to refer to the Devī's speech in 84.30. So the half-verse may be the Devī's own words (with 'we' as a plural of self-reference) or a displaced reply of the devas. I translate it literally without assigning a speaker, and take the quotation to be what the devas heard. The devas' actual reply begins at 84.32. ↩
  82. The reading: The line bhagavatyā kṛtaṃ sarvam is read as a passive construction with the agent in the instrumental, 'by the Blessed One'. The words 'to be done' are implied by avaśiṣyate and supplied in brackets. The causal yat is taken as 'since', giving the reason, though 'that' is also possible. Cāmara is not named here; Mahiṣāsura is the enemy meant. ↩
  83. Maheśvarī: 'Great Goddess', feminine of Maheśvara; here a title of the Devī. ↩
  84. Ambikā: 'Little mother', a name of the Goddess (Durgā/Caṇḍikā). ↩
  85. wife: dhanadārādisampadām: 'of the endowments beginning with wealth and wife'; dāra is a plural noun in Sanskrit, but the sense is the household in general. ↩
  86. The reading: asmāt in 'vṛddhaye 'smāt prasannā' can be read as the ablative of idam, 'for this reason, on this account', or as a mistake for asmat ('towards us', as in asmatprasannā, 'gracious to us'). I took 'for this reason' (because he praises you with these hymns) as the plainest reading of the text as given. The other reading would give 'be gracious to us'. Also hiṃsethāḥ (optative of hiṃs, 'harm') is taken in the sense 'destroy' with paramāpadaḥ as object. ↩
  87. for her own sake: ātmanaḥ is read with arthe: 'for the sake of the world and likewise of herself'. ↩
  88. Bhadrakālī: A name of the Goddess, here the Devī who has slain Mahiṣa. ↩
  89. The reading: The text reads jagator'the; I read jagato 'rthe ("for the sake of the world"), the sandhi form being a misprint of jagato. The nominative prasāditā is feminine and agrees with Bhadrakālī. ↩
  90. the three worlds: jagattraya: heaven, atmosphere and earth, that is, the whole universe. ↩
  91. the body of Gaurī: The Goddess issues from the body of Gaurī (Pārvatī) to destroy Śumbha and Niśumbha. This story is told in the following chapters. ↩
  92. The reading: mayākhyātam (84.38) may be read as a participle qualifying tat ("that which has been told by me") or taken more loosely as "as I have outlined it". I took it as a participle qualifying tat, rendered "as told by me". The verse is a transition to the next chapter, and no textual problem is evident. ↩
  93. eighty-fourth chapter: The e-text's own chapter numbering is irregular; the colophon says caturaśītitama, 'eighty-fourth'. The text reads 'dyāyaḥ for 'dhyāyaḥ, a misprint. ↩
  94. Devīmāhātmya: 'Greatness of the Devī', the title of the section of the Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa devoted to the Goddess's exploits. The text reads māhatmye, a form of māhātmya. ↩
  95. The reading: The Sanskrit reads 'dyāyaḥ (for 'dhyāyaḥ) and māhatmye (for māhātmye); both look like misprints, and I have read them as adhyāya and māhātmya. śrī- in the compound is taken as an honorific prefix ('revered'), not as the goddess Śrī. ↩

Adhyāya 85

Sanskrit

पञ्चाशीतितमो ऽध्यायः- ८५

pañcāśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ- 85

English

The eighty-fifth chapter.1

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

85.1

Sanskrit

पुरा शुम्भनिशुम्भाभ्यामसुराभ्यां शचीपतेः । त्रैलोक्यं यज्ञभागाश्च हृता मदबलाश्रयात् ॥

purā śumbhaniśumbhābhyāmasurābhyāṃ śacīpateḥ | trailokyaṃ yajñabhāgāśca hṛtā madabalāśrayāt ||

English

Long ago the three worlds and the shares of the yajña2 were taken from the lord of Śacī3 by the two asuras Śumbha and Niśumbha, through their reliance on pride and strength.4

85.2

Sanskrit

तावेव सूर्यतां तद्वदधिकारं तथैन्दवम् । कौबेरमथ याम्यं च चक्राते वरुणस्य च ॥

tāveva sūryatāṃ tadvadadhikāraṃ tathaindavam | kauberamatha yāmyaṃ ca cakrāte varuṇasya ca ||

English

Those same two took over the office of the sun5, and likewise the office of the moon, and that of Kubera6, and then that of Yama, and that of Varuṇa as well.7

85.3

Sanskrit

तावेव पवनर्धि च चक्रतुर्वह्निकर्म च । अन्येषाञ्चाधिकारान् स स्वयमेवाधितिष्ठति । ततो देवा विनिर्धूता भ्रष्टराज्याः पराजिताः ॥

tāveva pavanardhi ca cakraturvahnikarma ca | anyeṣāñcādhikārān sa svayamevādhitiṣṭhati | tato devā vinirdhūtā bhraṣṭarājyāḥ parājitāḥ ||

English

Those same two also took over the power of the wind8 and the function of fire. And he himself presides9 over the offices of others as well. Then the devas were driven out, fallen from their kingdom, and defeated.10

85.4

Sanskrit

हृताधिकारास्त्रिदशास्ताभ्यां सर्वे निराकृताः । महासुराभ्यां तां देवीं संस्मरन्त्यपराजिताम् ॥

hṛtādhikārāstridaśāstābhyāṃ sarve nirākṛtāḥ | mahāsurābhyāṃ tāṃ devīṃ saṃsmarantyaparājitām ||

English

85.4–5All the devas, deprived of their offices and driven away by those two great asuras, remember that unconquered11 Devī: 'By her a boon was given to us: "When I am remembered12 in calamities, I shall destroy at that very moment all your worst calamities."'13

85.5

Sanskrit

तयास्माकं वरो दत्तो यथाऽपत्सु स्मृताखिलाः । भवतां नाशयिष्यामि तत्क्षणात् परमापदः ॥

tayāsmākaṃ varo datto yathā'patsu smṛtākhilāḥ | bhavatāṃ nāśayiṣyāmi tatkṣaṇāt paramāpadaḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 85.4–5, above.

85.6

Sanskrit

इति कृत्वा मतिं देवा हिमवन्तं नगेश्वरम् । जग्मुस्तत्र ततो देवीं विष्णुमायां प्रतुष्टुवुः ॥

iti kṛtvā matiṃ devā himavantaṃ nageśvaram | jagmustatra tato devīṃ viṣṇumāyāṃ pratuṣṭuvuḥ ||

English

Having made this resolve, the devas went to Himavān14, the lord of mountains. There they praised the Devī, Viṣṇumāyā15.16

Sanskrit

देवा ऊचुः

devā ūcuḥ

English

The devas said:

85.7

Sanskrit

नमो देव्यै महादेव्यै शिवायै सततं नमः । नमः प्रकृत्यै भद्रायै नियताः प्रणताः स्म ताम् ॥

namo devyai mahādevyai śivāyai satataṃ namaḥ | namaḥ prakṛtyai bhadrāyai niyatāḥ praṇatāḥ sma tām ||

English

Homage to the Devī, to the Mahādevī, to Śivā; homage to her always. Homage to Prakṛti17, to the auspicious one18. Disciplined19, we have bowed down to her.20

85.8

Sanskrit

रौद्रायै नमो नित्यायौ गौर्यै धात्र्यै नमो नमः । नमो जगत्प्रतिष्ठायै देव्यै कृत्यै नमो नमः ॥

raudrāyai namo nityāyau gauryai dhātryai namo namaḥ | namo jagatpratiṣṭhāyai devyai kṛtyai namo namaḥ ||

English

Homage to the fierce one21, to the eternal one; to Gaurī, to Dhātrī22, homage, homage. Homage to her who is the foundation of the world; to the Devī, to Kṛti23, homage, homage.24

85.9

Sanskrit

द्योत्स्त्रायै चेन्दुरूपिण्यै सुखायै सततं नमः । कल्याण्यै प्रणतां वृद्ध्यै सिद्ध्यै कुर्मो नमो नमः ॥

dyotstrāyai cendurūpiṇyai sukhāyai satataṃ namaḥ | kalyāṇyai praṇatāṃ vṛddhyai siddhyai kurmo namo namaḥ ||

English

Homage always to Moonlight25 and to her who has the form of the moon, to Happiness. Bowed down26, we offer homage, homage to Kalyāṇī27, to Vṛddhi, to Siddhi.28

85.10

Sanskrit

नैरृत्यै भूभृतां लक्ष्म्यै शर्वाण्यै ते नमो नमः । दुर्गायै दुर्गपारायै सारायै सर्वकारिण्यै । ख्यात्यै तथैव कृष्णायै धूम्रायै सततं नमः ॥

nairṛtyai bhūbhṛtāṃ lakṣmyai śarvāṇyai te namo namaḥ | durgāyai durgapārāyai sārāyai sarvakāriṇyai | khyātyai tathaiva kṛṣṇāyai dhūmrāyai satataṃ namaḥ ||

English

Homage, homage to Nairṛtī29, to Lakṣmī of kings30, to Śarvāṇī; to you31, homage, homage. Homage always to Durgā, to her who takes one across difficulties, to Sārā, to her who does all things, to Khyāti, and likewise to Kṛṣṇā and to Dhūmrā.32

85.11

Sanskrit

अतिसौम्यातिरौद्रायै नतास्तस्यै नमो नमः । मनो जगत्प्रतिष्ठायै देव्यै कृत्यै नमो नमः ॥

atisaumyātiraudrāyai natāstasyai namo namaḥ | mano jagatpratiṣṭhāyai devyai kṛtyai namo namaḥ ||

English
