  31. The reading: talaprihāreṇa is metrically and orthographically irregular; I read it as talaprahāreṇa, 'with a blow of the paw' (tala, flat of the hand). Also, 'pṛthak' is taken with śirāṃsi kṛtavān as 'made the heads separate', i.e. severed them from the bodies; 'others' is implied by keṣāñcit ... tathā. ↩
  32. were left with their arms and heads severed: The text reads sṛtāḥ, 'gone, moved', which gives no sense with tena and the bahuvrīhi vicchinnabāhuśirasaḥ. It is probably a corruption of kṛtāḥ, 'made', as in 86.13 (kṛtavān). ↩
  33. The reading: sṛtāḥ: the word means 'gone, flowed, moved', which does not suit 'by him' (tena) with 'having severed arms and heads'. I read it as kṛtāḥ, 'made [to be]', a likely misprint or scribal error, and translate 'were left with their arms and heads severed'. ↩
  34. The reading: tadbalam: 'that force' (bala as army, 'strength') is chosen over 'that strength' because the whole host has just been destroyed; the glossary's 'strength' sense is kept in the rendering 'force'. mahātmanā is rendered 'great-souled', qualifying the lion, in agreement with kesariṇā. The compound beginning with devyā vāhanena is read as 'the Goddess's mount', in apposition to the lion. ↩
  35. The reading: tamasuraṃ is read as tam asuraṃ ('that asura'), not as a compound with tamas. In 86.16, śrutvā has no stated agent; it is taken to be Śumbha, the subject of cukopa in the next verse. ↩
  36. The reading: The verse continues into 86.19, where the means of bringing her (dragging her by the hair or binding her) is given. Here 'sā' (she) is taken as the Goddess, and 'samānīyatām' as an impersonal-passive imperative ('let her be brought'). The quotation mark opens Śumbha's speech, which continues in the next verse, so the passage is left open without ' //' closing quote. ↩
  37. Drag her by the hair, or bind her: The absolutives (ākṛṣya, baddhvā) depend on the verb 'bring' in the preceding verse (samānīyatām): she is to be brought dragged by the hair or bound. 'Her' (sā) is the Goddess, the subject of 86.18. ↩
  38. if you have any doubt in the battle: The phrase 'yadi vaḥ saṃśayo yudhi' is literally 'if there is a doubt for you in battle'. It means doubt about capturing her alive. ↩
  39. The reading: The construction of keśeṣvākṛṣya baddhvā vā is elliptic. It could be taken with 86.18 (bring her, dragging her by the hair or binding her) or as an independent command. I rendered it as an independent imperative clause, with 'her' understood from 86.18, which keeps the sense. 'Saṃśaya' could mean 'doubt' or 'danger or risk to life'. I chose 'doubt' as the more natural reading, meaning doubt about taking her alive. ↩
  40. come back quickly: The verb āgamyatām is an impersonal passive imperative ('let there be coming'); it is rendered as a direct command to Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa. ↩
  41. Ambikā: 'Little Mother', a name of the Goddess, here used by Śumbha of the woman he means to capture. ↩
  42. The reading: The verse is part of Śumbha's speech begun in 86.18, and the sentence is incomplete as printed: duṣṭāyām ('wicked') and the second-half participles baddhvā gṛhītvā make an awkward sequence, and the passive āgamyatām is odd. I kept the literal sense and rendered the impersonal passive as an imperative. The order 'bound and seized' follows the Sanskrit. The locative absolutes tasyāṃ hatāyām and siṃhe vinipātite are rendered as 'when ... has been slain and ... struck down'. ↩
  43. Sāvarṇika: Sāvarṇike manvantare means 'in the [section on the] manvantara of Sāvarṇi', the eighth Manu. The Devīmāhātmya belongs to the narrative of this eighth manvantara; it is not set within the age itself. ↩
  44. The reading: The reference gives chapter 81, but the Sanskrit colophon reads ṣaḍaśītitama ('eighty-sixth'), and the neighbouring verses concern chapters 86 and 87. I follow the Sanskrit and treat '81' as a reference error. I have dropped the earlier note on the chapter number, as the reviewer asked. 'Śrī' in śrī-mārkaṇḍeya- is an honorific prefix ('revered'), not the goddess Śrī. I have followed the reviewer in keeping the Sanskrit order of the locatives (Mahāpurāṇa, then manvantara, then Devīmāhātmya) and in rewording the Sāvarṇika note. ↩

Adhyāya 87

Sanskrit

सप्तशीतितमाध्यायः- ८७

saptaśītitamādhyāyaḥ- 87

English

Chapter eighty-seven.1

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

87.1The ṛṣi said: Thus commanded, those Daityas, with Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa at their head2, set out, accompanied by a fourfold army3 and with their weapons raised.

87.1

Sanskrit

आज्ञप्तास्ते ततो दैत्याश्चण्डमुण्डपुरोगमाः । चतुरङ्गबलोपेता ययुरभ्युद्यतायुधाः ॥

ājñaptāste tato daityāścaṇḍamuṇḍapurogamāḥ | caturaṅgabalopetā yayurabhyudyatāyudhāḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 87.1, above.

87.2

Sanskrit

ददृशुस्ते ततो देवीमीषद्धासां व्यवस्थिताम् । सिंहस्योपरि शैलेन्द्रशृङ्गे महति काञ्चने ॥

dadṛśuste tato devīmīṣaddhāsāṃ vyavasthitām | siṃhasyopari śailendraśṛṅge mahati kāñcane ||

English

Then they saw the Goddess, who was smiling faintly, seated on top of the lion, on a great golden peak of the lord of mountains4.5

87.3

Sanskrit

ते दृष्ट्वा तां समादातुमुद्यमं चक्रुरुद्यताः । आकृष्टचापासिधरास्तथान्ये सत्समीपगाः ॥

te dṛṣṭvā tāṃ samādātumudyamaṃ cakrurudyatāḥ | ākṛṣṭacāpāsidharāstathānye satsamīpagāḥ ||

English

Having seen her, they, determined6, made an effort to seize her; and others, with drawn bows and swords, went close to her7.8

87.4

Sanskrit

ततः कोपं चकारोच्चैरम्बिका तानरीन् प्रति । कोपेन चास्या वदनं मषीवर्णमभूत्तदा ॥

tataḥ kopaṃ cakāroccairambikā tānarīn prati | kopena cāsyā vadanaṃ maṣīvarṇamabhūttadā ||

English

Then Ambikā grew loudly furious9 at those enemies, and from her fury her face at that moment turned the colour of ink.10

87.5

Sanskrit

भ्रुकुटीकुटिलात्तस्या ललाटफलकाद् द्रुतम् । काली करालवदना विनिष्क्रान्तासिपाशिनी ॥

bhrukuṭīkuṭilāttasyā lalāṭaphalakād drutam | kālī karālavadanā viniṣkrāntāsipāśinī ||

English

87.5–8From her forehead, the surface of which was bent by the knitting of her brows, there swiftly sprang forth Kālī11, terrible of face, carrying a sword and a noose, bearing a wondrous skull-topped staff12, adorned with a garland of human heads, clothed in a tiger's skin, and dreadful in her dried, shrunken flesh13. Her mouth was exceedingly wide, she was fearful with her lolling tongue, her eyes were red and sunken, and she filled the quarters of space with her roar. She fell upon that army of the enemies of the gods with speed, slaying the great asuras, and devoured that force.14

87.6

Sanskrit

विचित्रखट्वाङ्गधरा नरमालाविभूषणा । द्वीपिचर्मपरीधाना शुष्कमांसातिभैरवा ॥

vicitrakhaṭvāṅgadharā naramālāvibhūṣaṇā | dvīpicarmaparīdhānā śuṣkamāṃsātibhairavā ||

English

Its English is given with 87.5–8, above.

87.7

Sanskrit

अतिविस्तारवदना जिह्वाललनभीषणा । निमग्नारक्तनयना नादापूरितदिङ्मुखा ॥

ativistāravadanā jihvālalanabhīṣaṇā | nimagnāraktanayanā nādāpūritadiṅmukhā ||

English

Its English is given with 87.5–8, above.

87.8

Sanskrit

सा वेगेनाभिपतिता घतयन्ती महासुरान् । सैन्ये तत्र सुरारीणामभक्षयत तद्वलम् ॥

sā vegenābhipatitā ghatayantī mahāsurān | sainye tatra surārīṇāmabhakṣayata tadvalam ||

English

Its English is given with 87.5–8, above.

87.9

Sanskrit

पार्ष्णिग्राहाङ्कुशग्राहियोधघण्टासमन्वितान् । समादायैकहस्तेन मुखे चिक्षेप वारणान् ॥

pārṣṇigrāhāṅkuśagrāhiyodhaghaṇṭāsamanvitān | samādāyaikahastena mukhe cikṣepa vāraṇān ||

English

87.9–10Taking up with one hand the elephants, equipped with rear-guards15, goad-holders16, warriors and bells, she threw them into her mouth; and likewise, having thrown into her mouth a warrior together with his horses, and a chariot together with its charioteer, she chewed them with her teeth17, most dreadfully.18

87.10

Sanskrit

तथैव योधं तुरगै रथं सारथिना सह । निक्षिप्य वक्त्रे दशनैश्चर्वयन्त्यतिभैरवम् ॥

tathaiva yodhaṃ turagai rathaṃ sārathinā saha | nikṣipya vaktre daśanaiścarvayantyatibhairavam ||

English

Its English is given with 87.9–10, above.

87.11

Sanskrit

एकं जग्राह केशेषु ग्रीवायामथ चापरम् । पादेनाक्रम्य चैवान्यमुरसान्यमपोथयत् ॥

ekaṃ jagrāha keśeṣu grīvāyāmatha cāparam | pādenākramya caivānyamurasānyamapothayat ||

English

She seized one by the hair19, and another by the neck; trampling one with her foot, she crushed another with her chest.20

87.12

Sanskrit

तैर्मुक्तानि च शस्त्रणि महास्त्राणि तथासुरैः । मुखेन जग्राह रुषा दशनैर्मथितान्यपि ॥

tairmuktāni ca śastraṇi mahāstrāṇi tathāsuraiḥ | mukhena jagrāha ruṣā daśanairmathitānyapi ||

English

In her rage she seized with her mouth the weapons and the great missiles21 that those asuras had hurled, and ground them to pieces with her teeth.22

87.13

Sanskrit

बलिनां तद्बलं सर्वमसुराणां दुरात्मनाम् । ममर्दाभक्षयच्चान्यानन्यांश्चाताडयत्तथा ॥

balināṃ tadbalaṃ sarvamasurāṇāṃ durātmanām | mamardābhakṣayaccānyānanyāṃścātāḍayattathā ||

English

She crushed all that army of the mighty, evil-minded asuras, and devoured others, and likewise struck still others.23

87.14

Sanskrit

असिना निहताः केचित्केचित्खट्वाङ्गताडिताः । जग्मुर्विनाशमसुरा दन्ताग्राभिहता रणे ॥

asinā nihatāḥ kecitkecitkhaṭvāṅgatāḍitāḥ | jagmurvināśamasurā dantāgrābhihatā raṇe ||

English

Some of the asuras were slain with her sword24, and some were beaten with her khaṭvāṅga25; others were struck by the tips of her teeth in the battle. They went to destruction.26†

87.15

Sanskrit

क्षणेन तन्महासैन्यमसुराणां निपातितम् । दृष्ट्वा चण्डो ऽभिमैर्भोमाक्षीं तां महासुरः ॥

kṣaṇena tanmahāsainyamasurāṇāṃ nipātitam | dṛṣṭvā caṇḍo 'bhimairbhomākṣīṃ tāṃ mahāsuraḥ ||

English

87.15–16Seeing that great army of the asuras felled in an instant, Caṇḍa, the great asura, †abhimaiḥ†27 [...] that Bhīmākṣī28. That great asura covered that Bhīmākṣī with very dreadful showers of arrows, and Muṇḍa [covered her] with discs29 hurled in thousands.30

87.16

Sanskrit

शरवर्षैर्महाभीमैर्भोमाक्षीं तां महासुरः । छादयामास चक्रैश्च मुण्डः क्षिप्तैः सहस्रशः ॥

śaravarṣairmahābhīmairbhomākṣīṃ tāṃ mahāsuraḥ | chādayāmāsa cakraiśca muṇḍaḥ kṣiptaiḥ sahasraśaḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 87.15–16, above.

87.17

Sanskrit

तानि चक्राण्यनेकानि विशमानानि तन्मुखम् । बभुर्यथार्कबिम्बानि सुबहूनि घनोदरम् ॥

tāni cakrāṇyanekāni viśamānāni tanmukham | babhuryathārkabimbāni subahūni ghanodaram ||

English

Those many discs, entering her mouth31, shone like numerous orbs of the sun entering the belly of a cloud.32

87.18

Sanskrit

ततो जहासातिरुषा भीमं भैरवनादिनी । काली करालवक्त्रान्तर्दुर्दर्शदशनोज्ज्वला ॥

tato jahāsātiruṣā bhīmaṃ bhairavanādinī | kālī karālavaktrāntardurdarśadaśanojjvalā ||

English

Then Kālī33, who gave out a terrible roar, laughed a dreadful laugh in her great fury, her teeth, hard to look upon, gleaming within her gaping mouth.34

87.19

Sanskrit

उत्थाय च महासिंहं देवी चण्डमधावत । गृहीत्वा चास्य केशेषु शिरस्तेनासिनाच्छिनत् ॥

utthāya ca mahāsiṃhaṃ devī caṇḍamadhāvata | gṛhītvā cāsya keśeṣu śirastenāsinācchinat ||

English

Rising up on the great lion35, the Goddess ran at Caṇḍa, and having seized him by the hair, she cut off his head with that sword.36

87.20

Sanskrit

छिन्ने शिरसि दैत्येन्द्रश्चक्रे नादं सुभैरवम् । तेन नादेन महता त्रासितं भुवनत्रयम् ॥

chinne śirasi daityendraścakre nādaṃ subhairavam | tena nādena mahatā trāsitaṃ bhuvanatrayam ||

English

When [his] head had been cut off, the lord of the Daityas37 let out a most terrible roar, and by that mighty roar the three worlds were terrified.38

87.21

Sanskrit

अथ मुण्डो ऽभ्यधावत्तां दृष्ट्वा चण्डं निपातितम् । तमप्यपातयद् भूमौ सा खड्गाभिहतं रुषा ॥

atha muṇḍo 'bhyadhāvattāṃ dṛṣṭvā caṇḍaṃ nipātitam | tamapyapātayad bhūmau sā khaḍgābhihataṃ ruṣā ||

English

Then Muṇḍa, seeing Caṇḍa felled, ran at her; and she, in anger, struck him too with her sword and threw him down on the earth.39

87.22

Sanskrit

हतशेषं ततः सैन्यं दृष्ट्वा चण्डं निपातितम् । मुण्डं च सुमहावीर्यं दिशो भेजे भयातुरम् ॥

hataśeṣaṃ tataḥ sainyaṃ dṛṣṭvā caṇḍaṃ nipātitam | muṇḍaṃ ca sumahāvīryaṃ diśo bheje bhayāturam ||

English

Then the army, what remained of those who had been slain, seeing Caṇḍa felled, and Muṇḍa of very great vigor [felled] too, fled in terror to the quarters40.41

87.23

Sanskrit

शिरञ्चण्डस्य काली च गृहीत्वा मुण्डमेव च । प्राह प्रचण्डाट्टहासमिश्रमभ्येत्य चण्डिकाम् ॥

śirañcaṇḍasya kālī ca gṛhītvā muṇḍameva ca | prāha pracaṇḍāṭṭahāsamiśramabhyetya caṇḍikām ||

English

And Kālī, having taken the head of Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa's as well42, came up to Caṇḍikā and spoke, her words mingled with a fierce, loud laughter43.44

87.24

Sanskrit

मया तवात्रोपहृतौ चण्डमुण्डौ महापशू । युद्धयज्ञे स्वयं शुम्भं निशुम्भं च हनिष्यसि ॥

mayā tavātropahṛtau caṇḍamuṇḍau mahāpaśū | yuddhayajñe svayaṃ śumbhaṃ niśumbhaṃ ca haniṣyasi ||

English

By me these two, Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa, two great sacrificial animals45, have been brought here to you in the sacrifice of battle46. Shumbha and Niśumbha you will kill yourself.47

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

87.25The ṛṣi said: Then, seeing those two great asuras48, Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa, brought to her, the auspicious49 Caṇḍikā spoke pleasing words to Kālī.50

87.25

Sanskrit

तावानीतौ ततो दृष्ट्वा चणाडमुण्डौ महासुरौ । उवाच कालीं कल्याणी ललितं चण्डिका वचः ॥

tāvānītau tato dṛṣṭvā caṇāḍamuṇḍau mahāsurau | uvāca kālīṃ kalyāṇī lalitaṃ caṇḍikā vacaḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 87.25, above.

Sanskrit

श्रीदेव्युवाच

śrīdevyuvāca

English

87.26The Blessed Goddess said: "Because you have come here having taken both Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa, therefore, O Goddess, you will be known in the world as Cāmuṇḍā51."52

87.26

Sanskrit

यस्माच्चण्डं च मुण्डं च गृहीत्वा त्वमुपागता । चामुण्डेति ततो लोके ख्याता देवि भविष्यसि ॥

yasmāccaṇḍaṃ ca muṇḍaṃ ca gṛhītvā tvamupāgatā | cāmuṇḍeti tato loke khyātā devi bhaviṣyasi ||

English

Its English is given with 87.26, above.

Sanskrit

इति श्रीमार्कण्डेयपुराणे सावर्णिके मन्वन्तरे देवीमाहात्म्ये चण्डमुण्डवधोनाम सप्ताशीतितमो ऽध्यायः

iti śrīmārkaṇḍeyapurāṇe sāvarṇike manvantare devīmāhātmye caṇḍamuṇḍavadhonāma saptāśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ

English

Thus ends the eighty-seventh chapter53, named "The Slaying of Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa," in the Devīmāhātmya, in the Sāvarṇika manvantara54, in the revered Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa.55

  1. The reading: The text gives only the chapter heading, saptaśītitama-adhyāya 87 ('the eighty-seventh chapter'). The Devanagari and the IAST both carry a stray hyphen before the numeral, which I have dropped. The heading names no chapter title, so none is supplied. ↩
  2. with Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa at their head: The compound caṇḍamuṇḍapurogamāḥ is a bahuvrīhi, 'having Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa as leaders'. It says that the two asuras lead the Daityas, not that they are separate from them. ↩
  3. fourfold army: The caturaṅgabala is the traditional army of elephants, chariots, cavalry and infantry. ↩
  4. lord of mountains: śailendra, 'lord of mountains', is a general epithet of a great mountain, often the Himālaya; the text does not name the mountain here. ↩
  5. The reading: īṣaddhāsām is an accusative qualifying devīm: 'having a slight smile', taken as 'smiling faintly'. Vyavasthitām is rendered 'seated' ('standing' or 'stationed' are also possible); it is placed on the lion, so 'seated' suits the sense. Śailendraśṛṅge mahati kāñcane is a locative: 'on a great golden peak'. The Sanskrit does not say that the lion stands on the peak. The English follows the Sanskrit order, and the Goddess is the one on the peak. ↩
  6. determined: udyatāḥ may also mean 'with weapons raised'. That sense is already given in 87.1 (abhyudyatāyudhāḥ), so 'determined' is preferred here. ↩
  7. went close to her: The printed satsamīpagāḥ would mean 'going near the good [ones]'. This is probably a corruption of tatsamīpagāḥ, 'going near her', and the translation follows that emendation. ↩
  8. The reading: satsamīpagāḥ: the printed text gives 'going near the good [ones]' (sat as a plural or generic term, or as an unattested epithet of the Goddess). I reject the epithet reading, because tām already names the Goddess and sat is not otherwise attested as a name for her. I emend to tatsamīpagāḥ, 'went close to her', which suits the context and needs only a small change (t/s confusion). The emendation is flagged in the note. udyatāḥ: 'determined, eager' (chosen) or 'with weapons raised'. It qualifies the first group, while ākṛṣṭa... describes the second. ākṛṣṭa is taken with cāpa ('drawn bows'). It could also apply to the swords (unsheathed), but this is less natural. ↩
  9. grew loudly furious: Sanskrit kopaṃ cakāra uccaiḥ: literally 'made anger, highly/loudly'; uccaiḥ may mean 'greatly' or 'loudly'. The translation takes the sense 'intensely, openly'. ↩
  10. The reading: uccaiḥ can mean 'loudly' or 'greatly, intensely'. The translation 'loudly furious' is a compromise; 'she was greatly enraged' is equally possible. Also 'ink' renders maṣī (ink, lampblack, soot). ↩
  11. Kālī: 'The Dark One', a fierce form of the Goddess, born here from Ambikā's wrath. ↩
  12. skull-topped staff: khaṭvāṅga: a staff with a skull at its head, the weapon and emblem of ascetics and fierce deities such as Śiva. ↩
  13. dried, shrunken flesh: śuṣkamāṃsā: 'her flesh dried up'; she is gaunt, with shrivelled flesh. ↩
  14. The reading: 'nimagnāraktanayanā' is read as 'nimagna-āraktanayanā' (eyes sunken and reddened), i.e. red and sunken eyes. 'Ghatayantī' in 87.8 is read as 'ghātayantī' (slaying), the short vowel being a metrical or textual irregularity. 'Abhakṣayata' is an imperfect, 'devoured'. ↩
  15. rear-guards: pārṣṇigrāha, literally 'heel-holder': a fighter who guards the rear of an elephant or army. ↩
  16. goad-holders: aṅkuśagrāhin: the elephant-driver (mahout), who holds the goad (aṅkuśa). ↩
  17. she chewed them with her teeth: carvayantī is feminine, agreeing with the implied subject Kālī of 87.8; atibhairavam is adverbial ('most dreadfully'). ↩
  18. The reading: The compound pārṣṇigrāhāṅkuśagrāhiyodhaghaṇṭāsamanvitān can be read as four items (rear-guards, goad-holders, warriors, bells), as I have taken it, or yodha may be taken with the preceding terms as 'warriors who are rear-guards and goad-holders'. I chose the four-item reading, which is the simplest and fits 'bells' as a separate fitting. ↩
  19. She seized one by the hair: The verse gives no noun for the victims. Context (87.9–10) suggests the asuras and their warriors, but the Sanskrit has only ekam, aparam, anyam, anyam ("one", "another"). ↩
  20. The reading: The printed text reads 'ca-aparam' and 'anyam urasā anyam apothayat'. The final 'urasānyam' is read as urasā + anyam (instrumental 'with the chest'). The object of each verb is masculine singular and is not named, so I left it as 'one' and 'another'. ↩
  21. great missiles: mahāstrāṇi: the mighty hurled or mantra-empowered weapons, as distinct from the hand-held śastra. ↩
  22. The reading: śastraṇi is a misprint for śastrāṇi (neuter plural), read as 'weapons'. Also, mathitāni is read as agreeing with śastrāṇi and mahāstrāṇi, both being objects of jagrāha; 'api' is taken as 'even', giving 'ground them to pieces' as the sense of the pair. ↩
  23. The reading: I accepted all three findings. (1) 'anyān … anyāṃś' are other asuras, outside the army she crushed, so the draft's 'some of them' was wrong; they are now 'others' and 'still others'. (2) The sequence of 'ca … ca … tathā' is now kept: 'crushed …, and devoured others, and likewise struck still others'. (3) 'bala' in 'tadbalam' is rendered 'army', not 'strength' as in the glossary, because the context requires it: 87.8 has 'sainye … tad balam' and 87.15 calls it 'mahāsainya'. 'Balinām' ('mighty') plays on 'bala', and the sense 'strength' is possible, but 'army' suits the verb 'mamarda' and the surrounding verses. ↩
  24. her sword: The verse does not name the wielder. From the preceding verses it is the Goddess (Kālī), who is the subject throughout. ↩
  25. khaṭvāṅga: A staff topped with a skull, the weapon associated with Śiva and with fierce forms of the Goddess. ↩
  26. The reading: dantāgrābhihatā: the verse has the nominative plural feminine-looking form, but it agrees with asurāḥ, so it is read as a metrically shortened form of dantāgrābhihatāḥ. The first-person-free subject 'her' is supplied from context. ↩
  27. †abhimaiḥ†: The reading abhimair (caṇḍo 'bhimair) is unintelligible and is marked as a crux. The sense required is a verb of attack governing tāṃ bhomākṣīṃ, probably 'bhyadhāvat, 'rushed at'. The gap is not filled in the translation. ↩
  28. Bhīmākṣī: 'Terrible-eyed', a name of the Goddess, who is called Kālī in 87.18. The text reads bhomākṣīṃ in both verses, evidently for bhīmākṣīṃ. ↩
  29. discs: cakra, a flat, sharp-edged hurled weapon. ↩
  30. The reading: (1) 87.15 'abhimair' is corrupt. I accept the reviewer's point and no longer silently drop it. It is shown as a crux (†abhimaiḥ†) with the gap marked [...], and the conjecture 'bhyadhāvat ('rushed at') is given only in the note, not placed in the translation as certain. The object tāṃ bhomākṣīm stays in 87.15 with its own segment. (2) 'bhomākṣīm' in both verses is read as bhīmākṣīm, 'terrible-eyed', a misprint, as Caṇḍa's and Muṇḍa's attack on the Goddess makes clear. (3) mahāsuraḥ occurs in both verses and is now kept in both. I take it as Caṇḍa each time, the subject of chādayāmāsa. Muṇḍa is the subject of a parallel action in the second half-line, with kṣiptaiḥ agreeing with cakraiḥ. An alternative is that Caṇḍa covers her with arrows and with discs, and Muṇḍa merely hurled them. I keep the parallel reading, since muṇḍaḥ is a separate nominative. ↩
  31. her mouth: The Sanskrit has tanmukham, 'that mouth', the mouth of the Goddess in her form as Bhīmākṣī (see 87.16). ↩
  32. The reading: viśamānāni is an irregular form: the root viś takes the ātmanepada ending only in unusual usage, so the participle may be a metrical or textual irregularity; the sense 'entering' is clear. ghanodaram can be read as 'the belly of a cloud' (ghana, cloud) or 'a dense interior'; I chose the cloud, as the simile of sun-orbs in a cloud suits the scene. ↩
  33. Kālī: The dark, fierce form of the Goddess, here named in the text as the one who laughs and then attacks Caṇḍa. ↩
  34. The reading: bhīmam is taken as an adverbial accusative with jahāsa ('laughed dreadfully'). Alternatively it could be read as an adjective with an implied object, but the adverbial sense is the most natural. The compound karālavaktrāntardurdarśadaśanojjvalā is read as 'gleaming with teeth hard to see within her gaping mouth'; durdarśa ('hard to look at') is taken as qualifying the teeth, not as a separate adjective of Kālī. ↩
  35. Rising up on the great lion: The Sanskrit has utthāya with the accusative mahāsiṃham. The sense is that the Goddess mounts her lion, but the construction is unusual. ↩
  36. The reading: utthāya ... mahāsiṃham: utthāya normally means 'having risen up', and a verb of rising does not usually take a direct object. Possible readings are 'rising up on the great lion' (that is, mounting it) and 'having sprung up [with] the great lion'. I chose the first because the lion is the Goddess's mount and she then attacks Caṇḍa. The reading may be corrupt; utpatya ('having leapt upon') would be easier. tena asinā: I take 'that sword' to be the sword already mentioned in 87.14. ↩
  37. the lord of the Daityas: Daityendra. Here the epithet is in the nominative and refers to Caṇḍa, whose head the Goddess has just cut off in 87.19. It is not the address to Śumbha. ↩
  38. The reading: The verse gives no pronoun for the head (chinne śirasi), and daityendraḥ could in principle name another figure. Since the sentence follows directly on the beheading of Caṇḍa in 87.19, I take the lord of the Daityas to be Caṇḍa and supply [his]. That he roars after being beheaded is odd, but it is what the Sanskrit says and I have not emended it. ↩
  39. The reading: The Sanskrit is irregular in two places. (1) The verse is metrically and grammatically compressed: 'tam api ... khaḍgābhihatam' has the participle agreeing with 'tam' (him, struck by the sword) alongside the finite verb 'apātayat'; I render it as 'struck him with her sword and threw him down'. (2) 'sā' (she) is not named; from the context it is the Goddess (Devī/Kālī, 87.19 and 87.18). I kept the pronoun as 'she' and did not name her, adding nothing not in the Sanskrit. 'Bhūmau' is locative, 'on the earth', and 'nipātitam' is a causative participle, 'felled' (caused to fall). ↩
  40. fled in terror to the quarters: Literally 'resorted to the directions' (diśo bheje); the neuter singular bhayāturam agrees with sainyam, 'the army', which is seized by fear. ↩
  41. The reading: The verse is syntactically loose: hataśeṣam, bhayāturam and sainyam are neuter singular, agreeing with the army, while caṇḍam and muṇḍam are accusatives after dṛṣṭvā. The participle nipātitam properly agrees with caṇḍam, and muṇḍam must be understood with it ('Muṇḍa too felled'), hence my bracketed word. I take sumahāvīryam as qualifying Muṇḍa, since the preceding verse shows him felled, though one could read it as irony. I do not take it as describing the army. ↩
  42. Muṇḍa's as well: The Sanskrit has the accusative singular śiraḥ ("head") governing both genitive Caṇḍa and the accusative muṇḍam, so "Muṇḍa" stands for his head; the verse says muṇḍam, not śiraḥ, for the second. ↩
  43. mingled with a fierce, loud laughter: pracaṇḍāṭṭahāsamiśram is an adverbial accusative: she spoke "mixed with" loud laughter, i.e. laughing as she spoke. ↩
  44. The reading: muṇḍam eva ca: muṇḍa may be read as the demon Muṇḍa (whom she had also killed) or as 'a head' (muṇḍa, 'shaven/head'). I took it as the name Muṇḍa, with śiras understood, as the next verse names Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa. Also, the form śirañcaṇḍasya with the nasal is a sandhi for śiraḥ caṇḍasya; I read it as 'head of Caṇḍa'. ↩
  45. two great sacrificial animals: mahāpaśū: Kālī presents the two slain asuras as victims (paśu) offered in the battle-sacrifice, an image that continues in the word yuddhayajña. ↩
  46. sacrifice of battle: yuddhayajña: a compound that treats the battle as a yajña, with the slain as its offerings. ↩
  47. The reading: Spelling: the translation should read 'Śumbha', not 'Shumbha', following the glossary. Also, 'tavātra' is read as tava + atra, 'to you, here'; 'tava' may alternatively be construed with 'yuddhayajñe' ('your sacrifice of battle'), which I take as the less natural reading, since 'upahṛtau' needs a dative-like 'tava'. Textual note: the word 'svayam' could qualify haniṣyasi, as taken here. ↩
  48. asuras: Asuras are the rivals of the gods (suras); here Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa are the servants of Śumbha and Niśumbha. ↩
  49. auspicious: Kalyāṇī, 'the auspicious one', is read as an epithet of Caṇḍikā, the subject, agreeing with her. ↩
  50. The reading: kalyāṇī (nominative feminine) could grammatically qualify Caṇḍikā (subject) or be read as an accusative with kālīm. The nominative form is taken as an epithet of Caṇḍikā, since Kālī is the one addressed. The text's 'caṇāḍamuṇḍau' is a misprint for caṇḍamuṇḍau and is read so. The speaker line 'ṛṣiruvāca' is given in the verse numbered 720.1 and is the ṛṣi's narration that frames the Goddess's speech. ↩
  51. Cāmuṇḍā: The name is explained as formed from Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa (cāmuṇḍā = ca + muṇḍa, 'and Muṇḍa', with Caṇḍa), an etymology offered by the text. Cāmuṇḍā is the well-known fierce form of the Goddess. ↩
  52. The reading: The quoted text reads 'cāmuṇḍeti' = cāmuṇḍā + iti (the nominative feminine ending -ā before iti becomes -eti by sandhi). I have taken it as the nominative name Cāmuṇḍā. The 'devi' addressed is Kālī, as the context makes clear, not Caṇḍikā herself. ↩
  53. eighty-seventh chapter: The e-text's chapter heading reads 82, but the colophon and the verse numbers (87.23–26) give the eighty-seventh chapter; the next chapter is numbered 88. ↩
  54. Sāvarṇika manvantara: The eighth manvantara, presided over by Manu Sāvarṇi; the Devīmāhātmya is set within it. ↩
  55. The reading: The colophon reads 'caṇḍamuṇḍavadhonāma', which is vadho nāma by sandhi. 'Nāma' is rendered 'named'. 'Śrī' in śrīmārkaṇḍeyapurāṇe is rendered 'revered', an honorific. ↩

Adhyāya 88

Sanskrit

अष्टाशीतितमो ऽध्यायः- ८८

aṣṭāśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ- 88

English

The eighty-eighth chapter: 88.1

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

88.1–2The ṛṣi said: When the Daitya Caṇḍa had been slain, and Muṇḍa had been struck down, and many of his armies had been destroyed, the lord of the asuras2, Śumbha, the mighty one, whose mind was then overcome by anger, gave the order for the mobilization of all the armies of the Daityas.3

88.1

Sanskrit

चण्डे च निहते दैत्ये मुण्डे च विनिपातिते । बहुलेषु च सैन्येषु क्षयितेष्वसुरेश्वरः ॥

caṇḍe ca nihate daitye muṇḍe ca vinipātite | bahuleṣu ca sainyeṣu kṣayiteṣvasureśvaraḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 88.1–2, above.

88.2

Sanskrit

ततः कोपपराधीनचेताः शुम्भः प्रतापवान् । उद्योगं सर्वसैन्यानां दैत्यानामादिदेश ह ॥

tataḥ kopaparādhīnacetāḥ śumbhaḥ pratāpavān | udyogaṃ sarvasainyānāṃ daityānāmādideśa ha ||

English

Its English is given with 88.1–2, above.

88.3

Sanskrit

अद्य सर्वबलैर्दैत्याः षडशीतिरुदायुधाः । कम्बूनां चतुरशीतिर्निर्यान्तु स्वबलैर्वृताः ॥

adya sarvabalairdaityāḥ ṣaḍaśītirudāyudhāḥ | kambūnāṃ caturaśītirniryāntu svabalairvṛtāḥ ||

English

Today let the eighty-six Daityas, the Udāyudhas4, and eighty-four of the Kambus5, with all their forces, march out, surrounded by their own forces.6789

88.4

Sanskrit

कोटिवीर्याणि पञ्चाशदसुराणां कुलानि वै । शतं कुलानि धौम्राणां निर्गच्छन्तु ममाज्ञया ॥

koṭivīryāṇi pañcāśadasurāṇāṃ kulāni vai | śataṃ kulāni dhaumrāṇāṃ nirgacchantu mamājñayā ||

English

At my command let the fifty Koṭivīrya clans of the asuras and the hundred clans of the Dhaumras go forth.

88.5

Sanskrit

कालका दौर्हृदा मौर्याः कालकेयास्तथासुराः । युद्धाय सज्जा निर्यान्तु आज्ञया त्वरिता मम ॥

kālakā daurhṛdā mauryāḥ kālakeyāstathāsurāḥ | yuddhāya sajjā niryāntu ājñayā tvaritā mama ||

English

Let the Kālakas, Daurhṛdas, Mauryas and Kālakeyas, the asuras, equipped for battle, march out in haste at my command.

88.6

Sanskrit

इत्याज्ञाप्यासुरपतिः शुम्भो भैरवशासनः । निर्जगाम महासैन्यसहस्रैर्बहुभिर्वृतः ॥

ityājñāpyāsurapatiḥ śumbho bhairavaśāsanaḥ | nirjagāma mahāsainyasahasrairbahubhirvṛtaḥ ||

English

Having given this order, Śumbha, the lord of the asuras, whose command was terrible10, set out, surrounded by many great armies of thousands11.12

88.7

Sanskrit

आयान्तं चण्डिका दृष्ट्वा तत्सैन्यमतिभिषणम् । ज्यास्वनैः पूरयामास धरणीगगनान्तरम् ॥

āyāntaṃ caṇḍikā dṛṣṭvā tatsainyamatibhiṣaṇam | jyāsvanaiḥ pūrayāmāsa dharaṇīgaganāntaram ||

English

Seeing that exceedingly terrible army coming on, Caṇḍikā13 filled the space between earth and sky with the twanging of her bowstring.14

88.8

Sanskrit

स च सिंहो महानादमतीव कृतवान् नृप । घण्टास्वनेन तन्नादमम्बिका चाप्यबृंहयत् ॥

sa ca siṃho mahānādamatīva kṛtavān nṛpa | ghaṇṭāsvanena tannādamambikā cāpyabṛṃhayat ||

English

And that lion, O king, let out an exceedingly great roar; and Ambikā15 also swelled that sound with the ringing of her bell.16

88.9

Sanskrit

धनुर्ज्यासिंहघण्टानां नादापूरितदिङ्मुखा । निनादैर्भोषणैः काली जिग्ये विस्तारितानना ॥

dhanurjyāsiṃhaghaṇṭānāṃ nādāpūritadiṅmukhā | ninādairbhoṣaṇaiḥ kālī jigye vistāritānanā ||

English

Kālī, with the quarters of the sky17 filled by the sounds of the bowstring, the lion and the bell, and with her mouth stretched wide, prevailed18 with her terrifying roars.19

88.10

Sanskrit

तं निनादमुपश्रुत्य दैत्यसैन्यैश्चतुर्दिशम् । देवी सिंहस्तथा काली सरोषैः परिवारिताः ॥

taṃ ninādamupaśrutya daityasainyaiścaturdiśam | devī siṃhastathā kālī saroṣaiḥ parivāritāḥ ||

English

88.10–12Having heard that roar, the angry Daitya armies surrounded the Goddess, the lion and Kālī on all four sides. At this moment, O king, the Śaktis20 of Brahmā, Īśa21, Guha22 and Viṣṇu, and likewise of Indra, endowed with exceeding vigor and strength, for the destruction of the enemies of the gods and for the welfare of the lions among the immortals, came out from [their] bodies and went to Caṇḍikā in the forms of those [gods].23

88.11

Sanskrit

एतस्मिन्नन्तरे भूप विनाशाय सुरद्विषाम् । भवायामरसिंहानामतिवीर्यबलान्विताः ॥

etasminnantare bhūpa vināśāya suradviṣām | bhavāyāmarasiṃhānāmativīryabalānvitāḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 88.10–12, above.

88.12

Sanskrit

ब्रह्मेशगुहविष्णूनां तथेन्द्रस्य च शक्तयः । शरीरेभ्यो विनिष्क्रम्य तद्रूपैश्चण्डिकां ययुः ॥

brahmeśaguhaviṣṇūnāṃ tathendrasya ca śaktayaḥ | śarīrebhyo viniṣkramya tadrūpaiścaṇḍikāṃ yayuḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 88.10–12, above.

88.13

Sanskrit

यस्य देवस्य यद्रूपं यथाभूषणवाहनम् । तद्वदेव हि तच्छक्तिरसुरान् योद्धुमाययौ ॥

yasya devasya yadrūpaṃ yathābhūṣaṇavāhanam | tadvadeva hi tacchaktirasurān yoddhumāyayau ||

English

Whatever form a god had, with whatever ornaments and mount, in just that same manner his Śakti came to fight the asuras.

88.14

Sanskrit

हंसयुक्तविमानाग्रे साक्षसूत्रकमण्डलुः । आयाता ब्रह्मणः शक्तिर्ब्रह्माणी साभिधीयते ॥

haṃsayuktavimānāgre sākṣasūtrakamaṇḍaluḥ | āyātā brahmaṇaḥ śaktirbrahmāṇī sābhidhīyate ||

English

The Śakti of Brahmā came, seated at the front of a vimāna yoked with swans, carrying a rosary24 and a water-pot25; she is called Brahmāṇī26.27

88.15

Sanskrit

माहेश्वरी वृषारूढा त्रिशूलवरधीरिणी । महाहिवलया प्राप्ता चन्द्रलेखाविभूषणा ॥

māheśvarī vṛṣārūḍhā triśūlavaradhīriṇī | mahāhivalayā prāptā candralekhāvibhūṣaṇā ||

English

Māheśvarī28 arrived, mounted on a bull29, bearing an excellent trident, with great serpents for bracelets, and adorned with a crescent moon30 [as an ornament].31

88.16

Sanskrit

कौमारी शक्तिहस्ता च मयूरवरवाहना । योद्धुमभ्याययौ दैत्यानम्बिका गुहरूपिणी ॥

kaumārī śaktihastā ca mayūravaravāhanā | yoddhumabhyāyayau daityānambikā guharūpiṇī ||

English

Kaumārī32, Ambikā in the form of Guha33, with a lance34 in her hand and an excellent peacock as her mount, came up to fight the Daityas.35

88.17

Sanskrit

तथैव वैष्णावी शक्तिर्गरुडोपरि संस्थिता । शङ्खचक्रगदाशार्ङ्गखड्गहस्ताभ्युपाययौ ॥

tathaiva vaiṣṇāvī śaktirgaruḍopari saṃsthitā | śaṅkhacakragadāśārṅgakhaḍgahastābhyupāyayau ||

English

In just the same way the Śakti of Viṣṇu, Vaiṣṇavī36, came up, seated on Garuḍa37, with a conch, a disc, a mace, the bow Śārṅga and a sword in her hands.38

88.18

Sanskrit

जज्ञे वाराहमतुलं रूपं या बिभ्रती हरेः । शक्तिः साप्यायौ तत्र वाराहीं बिभ्रती तनुम् ॥

jajñe vārāhamatulaṃ rūpaṃ yā bibhratī hareḥ | śaktiḥ sāpyāyau tatra vārāhīṃ bibhratī tanum ||

English

The Śakti who arose39 bearing the incomparable boar form of Hari40, she too came there, bearing a boar's body.41†

88.19

Sanskrit

नारसिंही नृसिंहस्य बिभ्रती सदृशं वपुः । प्राप्ता तत्र सटाक्षेपक्षिप्तनक्षत्रसंहतिः ॥

nārasiṃhī nṛsiṃhasya bibhratī sadṛśaṃ vapuḥ | prāptā tatra saṭākṣepakṣiptanakṣatrasaṃhatiḥ ||

English

Nārasiṃhī42, bearing a body like that of Nṛsiṃha43, arrived there, scattering the clusters of stars by the toss of her mane.44

88.20

Sanskrit

वज्रहस्ता तथैवैन्द्री गजराजोपरि स्थिता । प्राप्ता सहस्रनयना यथा शक्रस्तथैव सा ॥

vajrahastā tathaivaindrī gajarājopari sthitā | prāptā sahasranayanā yathā śakrastathaiva sā ||

English

In the same way Aindrī45 came, with a thunderbolt in her hand, seated on the king of elephants. She had a thousand eyes46; as Śakra is, so was she.47

88.21

Sanskrit

ततः परिवृतस्ताभिरीशानो देवशक्तिभिः । हन्यन्तामसुराः शीघ्रं मम प्रीत्याऽह चण्डिकाम् ॥

tataḥ parivṛtastābhirīśāno devaśaktibhiḥ | hanyantāmasurāḥ śīghraṃ mama prītyā'ha caṇḍikām ||

English

Then Īśāna48, surrounded by those Śaktis of the devas, said to Caṇḍikā: "Let the asuras be killed quickly, out of love for me49."50

88.22

Sanskrit

ततो देवोशरीरात्तु विनिष्क्रान्तातिभीषणा । चण्डिकाशक्तिरत्युग्रा शिवाशतनिनादिनी ॥

tato devośarīrāttu viniṣkrāntātibhīṣaṇā | caṇḍikāśaktiratyugrā śivāśataninādinī ||

English

Then from the body of the Devī51 there came forth the Śakti of Caṇḍikā, extremely terrifying and exceedingly fierce, howling with the cries of a hundred she-jackals52.53

88.23

Sanskrit

सा चाह धूम्रजटिलमीशानमपराजिता । दूत त्वं गच्छ भगवन् पार्श्वं शुम्भनिशुम्भयोः ॥

sā cāha dhūmrajaṭilamīśānamaparājitā | dūta tvaṃ gaccha bhagavan pārśvaṃ śumbhaniśumbhayoḥ ||

English

88.23–25And Aparājitā said to Īśāna54, who was matted-haired and smoke-grey: "You, lord, be my messenger and go to the side of Śumbha and Niśumbha. Tell Śumbha and Niśumbha, those two exceedingly arrogant dānavas, and whatever other dānavas have gathered there for battle: 'Let Indra gain the three worlds, and let the devas be enjoyers of the oblations55. Go away to the nether worlds, if you wish to live.'"56

88.24

Sanskrit

ब्रूहि शुम्भं निशुम्भं च दानवावतिगर्वितौ । ये चान्ये दानवास्तत्र युद्धाय समुपस्थिताः ॥

brūhi śumbhaṃ niśumbhaṃ ca dānavāvatigarvitau | ye cānye dānavāstatra yuddhāya samupasthitāḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 88.23–25, above.

88.25

Sanskrit

त्रैलोक्यमिन्द्रो लभतां देवाः सन्तु हविर्भुजः । यूयं प्रयात पातालं यदि जीवितुमिच्छथ ॥

trailokyamindro labhatāṃ devāḥ santu havirbhujaḥ | yūyaṃ prayāta pātālaṃ yadi jīvitumicchatha ||

English

Its English is given with 88.23–25, above.

88.26

Sanskrit

बलावलेपादथ चेद्भवन्तो युद्धकाङ्क्षिणः । तदागच्छत तृप्यन्तु मच्छिवाः पिशितेन वः ॥

balāvalepādatha cedbhavanto yuddhakāṅkṣiṇaḥ | tadāgacchata tṛpyantu macchivāḥ piśitena vaḥ ||

English

But if, out of the arrogance of your strength, you wish for battle, then come; let my jackals57 be sated with your flesh."58

88.27

Sanskrit

यतो नियुक्तो दौत्येन तया देव्या शिवः स्वयम् । शिवदूतीति लोके ऽस्मिंमस्ततः सा ख्यातिमागता ॥

yato niyukto dautyena tayā devyā śivaḥ svayam | śivadūtīti loke 'smiṃmastataḥ sā khyātimāgatā ||

English

Because Śiva59 himself was appointed by that Devī to the office of messenger, she therefore came to be renowned in this world as Śivadūtī60.61

88.28

Sanskrit

ते ऽपि श्रुत्वा वचो देव्याः शर्वाख्यातं महासुराः । अमर्षापूरिता जग्मुर्यत्र कात्यायनी स्थिता ॥

te 'pi śrutvā vaco devyāḥ śarvākhyātaṃ mahāsurāḥ | amarṣāpūritā jagmuryatra kātyāyanī sthitā ||

English

Having heard the words of the Devī as reported by Śarva62, those great asuras too, filled with indignation, went to where Kātyāyanī63 stood.64

88.29

Sanskrit

ततः प्रथममेवाग्रे शरशक्त्यृष्टिवृष्टिभिः । ववर्षुरुद्धतामर्षास्तां देवीममरारयः ॥

tataḥ prathamamevāgre śaraśaktyṛṣṭivṛṣṭibhiḥ | vavarṣuruddhatāmarṣāstāṃ devīmamarārayaḥ ||

English

Then, right at the very start, the enemies of the immortals65, their indignation roused66, rained down upon that Devī showers of arrows, spears and lances.67

88.30

Sanskrit

सा च तान् प्रहितान् बाणाञ्छूलशक्तिपरश्वधान् । चिच्छेद लीलयाऽध्मातधनुर्मुक्तैर्महेषुभिः ॥

sā ca tān prahitān bāṇāñchūlaśaktiparaśvadhān | ciccheda līlayā'dhmātadhanurmuktairmaheṣubhiḥ ||

English

And she playfully cut down those hurled arrows, spears, darts68 and axes with great arrows released from her twanging69 bow.70

88.31

Sanskrit

तस्याग्रतस्तथा काली शूलपातविदारितान् । खट्वाङ्गपोथितांश्चारीन् कुर्वती व्यचरत्तदा ॥

tasyāgratastathā kālī śūlapātavidāritān | khaṭvāṅgapothitāṃścārīn kurvatī vyacarattadā ||

English

In front of her, likewise, Kālī moved about at that time, making the enemies split open by the fall of her spear and crushed by her khaṭvāṅga71.72

88.32

Sanskrit

कमण्डलुजलाक्षेपहतवीर्यान् हतौजसः । ब्रह्माणी चाकरोच्छत्रून् येन येन स्म धावति ॥

kamaṇḍalujalākṣepahatavīryān hataujasaḥ | brahmāṇī cākarocchatrūn yena yena sma dhāvati ||

English

And Brahmāṇī made the enemies lose their energy73, their vigor destroyed by the flinging of water from her water-pot, by whichever way she ran74.75

88.33

Sanskrit

माहेश्वरी त्रिशूलेन तथा चक्रेण वैष्णवी । दैत्याञ्जघान कौमारी तथा शक्त्यातिकोपना ॥

māheśvarī triśūlena tathā cakreṇa vaiṣṇavī | daityāñjaghāna kaumārī tathā śaktyātikopanā ||

English

Māheśvarī struck down the Daityas with her trident, and so did Vaiṣṇavī with her disc; and Kaumārī76, extremely wrathful, [struck them down] with her spear77.78

88.34

Sanskrit

ऐन्द्रीकुलिशपातेन शतशो दैत्यदानवाः । पेतुर्विदारिताः पृथ्व्यां रुधिरौघप्रवर्षिणः ॥

aindrīkuliśapātena śataśo daityadānavāḥ | peturvidāritāḥ pṛthvyāṃ rudhiraughapravarṣiṇaḥ ||

English

By the fall of Aindrī's thunderbolt79, Daityas and Dānavas by the hundreds, split open, fell on the earth, pouring out streams of blood.80

88.35

Sanskrit

तुण्डप्रहारविध्वस्ता दंष्ट्राग्रक्षतवक्षसः । वाराहमूर्त्या न्यपतंश्चक्रेण च विदारिताः ॥

tuṇḍaprahāravidhvastā daṃṣṭrāgrakṣatavakṣasaḥ | vārāhamūrtyā nyapataṃścakreṇa ca vidāritāḥ ||

English

Shattered by blows of her snout, their chests wounded by the tips of her tusks, and split open by her disc81, they fell down, [struck] by the boar form82.83

88.36

Sanskrit

नखैर्विदारितांश्चान्यान् भक्षयन्ती महासुरान् । नारसिंही चचाराजौ नादापूर्णदिगन्तरा ॥

nakhairvidāritāṃścānyān bhakṣayantī mahāsurān | nārasiṃhī cacārājau nādāpūrṇadigantarā ||

English

Devouring other great asuras whom she had split open with her claws, Nārasiṃhī84 roamed the battlefield, the regions between the quarters85 filled with her roars.86

88.37

Sanskrit

चण्डाट्टहासैरसुराः शिवदूत्यभिदूषिताः । पेतुः पृथिव्यां पतितांस्तांश्चखादाथ सा तदा ॥

caṇḍāṭṭahāsairasurāḥ śivadūtyabhidūṣitāḥ | petuḥ pṛthivyāṃ patitāṃstāṃścakhādātha sā tadā ||

English

Overcome by the fierce peals of laughter87 of Śivadūtī, the asuras88 fell to the earth; and then, at that time, she devoured those who had fallen on the ground.89

88.38

Sanskrit

इति मातृगणं क्रुद्धं मर्दयन्तं महासुरान् । दृष्ट्वाभ्युपायैर्विविधैर्नेशुर्देवारिसैनिकाः ॥

iti mātṛgaṇaṃ kruddhaṃ mardayantaṃ mahāsurān | dṛṣṭvābhyupāyairvividhairneśurdevārisainikāḥ ||

English

Seeing the host of the Mothers90, enraged, thus crushing the great asuras, the soldiers who were the enemies of the gods91 fled by various means92.93

88.39

Sanskrit

पलायनपरान् दृष्ट्वा दैत्यान् मातृगणार्दितान् । योद्धुमभ्याययौ क्रुद्धो रक्तबीजो महासुरः ॥

palāyanaparān dṛṣṭvā daityān mātṛgaṇārditān | yoddhumabhyāyayau kruddho raktabījo mahāsuraḥ ||

English

Seeing the Daityas intent on flight, tormented by the host of the Mothers, the great asura Raktabīja94 came forward in anger to fight.

88.40

Sanskrit

रक्तबिन्दुर्यदा भूमौ पतत्यस्य शरीरतः । समुत्पतति मेदिन्यां तत्प्रमाणो महासुरः ॥

raktabinduryadā bhūmau patatyasya śarīrataḥ | samutpatati medinyāṃ tatpramāṇo mahāsuraḥ ||

English

Whenever a drop of blood falls to the ground from his body, a great asura of his own stature95 springs up on the earth.96

88.41

Sanskrit

युयुधे स गदापाणिपरिन्द्रशक्त्या महासुरः । ततश्चैन्द्री स्ववज्रेण रक्तबीजमताडयत् ॥

yuyudhe sa gadāpāṇiparindraśaktyā mahāsuraḥ | tataścaindrī svavajreṇa raktabījamatāḍayat ||

English

That great asura, mace in hand, fought with Indra's Śakti97. Then Aindrī struck Raktabīja with her own thunderbolt.98

88.42

Sanskrit

कुलिशेनाहतस्याशु बहु सुस्त्राव शोणितम् । समुत्तस्थुस्ततो योधास्तद्रूपास्तत्पराक्रमाः ॥

kuliśenāhatasyāśu bahu sustrāva śoṇitam | samuttasthustato yodhāstadrūpāstatparākramāḥ ||

English

From him, struck by the kuliśa99, much blood quickly flowed; and from it warriors then rose up, with his form and with his prowess.100

88.43

Sanskrit

यावन्तः पतितास्तस्य शरीराद्रक्तबिन्दवः । तावन्तः पुरुषा जातास्तद्वीर्यबलविक्रमाः ॥

yāvantaḥ patitāstasya śarīrādraktabindavaḥ | tāvantaḥ puruṣā jātāstadvīryabalavikramāḥ ||

English

As many drops of blood as fell from his body, so many men101 were born, having his vigor, strength and prowess.102

88.44

Sanskrit

ते चापि युयुधुस्तत्र पुरुषा रक्तसम्भवाः । समं मातृबिरत्युग्रशस्त्रपातातिभीषणम् ॥

te cāpi yuyudhustatra puruṣā raktasambhavāḥ | samaṃ mātṛbiratyugraśastrapātātibhīṣaṇam ||

English

And those men too, born of his blood, fought there together with the Mothers103, in a battle made most terrible by the very fierce falling of weapons104.105

88.45

Sanskrit

पुनश्च वज्रपातेन क्षतमस्य शिरो यदा । ववाह रक्तं पुरुषास्ततो जाताः सहस्रशः ॥

punaśca vajrapātena kṣatamasya śiro yadā | vavāha raktaṃ puruṣāstato jātāḥ sahasraśaḥ ||

English

And again, when his head was wounded by the fall of the thunderbolt, it poured forth blood, and from that men were born by the thousand.106

88.46

Sanskrit

वैष्णवी समरे चैनं चक्रेणाभिजघान ह । गदया दाडयामास ऐन्द्री तमसुरेश्वरम् ॥

vaiṣṇavī samare cainaṃ cakreṇābhijaghāna ha | gadayā dāḍayāmāsa aindrī tamasureśvaram ||

English

And Vaiṣṇavī struck him in the battle with her disc, and Aindrī beat107 that lord of the asuras108 with her mace.109

88.47

Sanskrit

वेष्णावीचक्रभिन्नस्य रुधिरस्त्रावसम्भवैः । सहस्रशौ जगद्व्याप्तं तत्प्रमाणैर्महासुरैः ॥

veṣṇāvīcakrabhinnasya rudhirastrāvasambhavaiḥ | sahasraśau jagadvyāptaṃ tatpramāṇairmahāsuraiḥ ||

English

From him, cleft by Vaiṣṇavī's110 disc, by those born of the flow of his blood, by great asuras of his own measure111, the world was pervaded by the thousand112.113†

88.48

Sanskrit

शक्त्या जघान कौमारो वाराही च तथासिना । माहेश्वरी त्रिशूलेन रक्तबीजं महासुरम् ॥

śaktyā jaghāna kaumāro vārāhī ca tathāsinā | māheśvarī triśūlena raktabījaṃ mahāsuram ||

English

Kaumārī114 struck the great asura Raktabīja with her spear115, Vārāhī116 likewise with her sword, and Māheśvarī with her trident.117

88.49

Sanskrit

स चापि गदया दैत्यः सर्वा एवाहनत् पृथक् । मातः कोपसमाविष्टो रक्तबीजो महासुरः ॥

sa cāpi gadayā daityaḥ sarvā evāhanat pṛthak | mātaḥ kopasamāviṣṭo raktabījo mahāsuraḥ ||

English

And that Daitya, the great asura Raktabīja, filled with anger, also struck all the Mothers118 one by one with his mace.119

88.50

Sanskrit

तस्याहतस्य बहुधा शक्तिशूलादिभिर्बुवि । पपात यो वै रक्तौघस्तेनासञ्छतशो ऽसुराः ॥

tasyāhatasya bahudhā śaktiśūlādibhirbuvi | papāta yo vai raktaughastenāsañchataśo 'surāḥ ||

English

From the flood of blood that fell on the ground120 from him, as he was struck in many ways with the spear, the trident and other weapons, hundreds of asuras came into being.121

88.51

Sanskrit

तैश्चासुरासृक्सम्भूतैरसुरैः सकलं जगत् । व्याप्तमासीत्ततो देवा भयमाजग्मुरुत्तमम् ॥

taiścāsurāsṛksambhūtairasuraiḥ sakalaṃ jagat | vyāptamāsīttato devā bhayamājagmuruttamam ||

English

And the whole world was pervaded by those asuras, born from the asura's blood122. Then the devas fell into the utmost fear.123

88.52

Sanskrit

तान् विषण्णान् सुरान् दृष्ट्वा चण्डिका प्राह सत्वरा । उवाच कालीं चामुण्डे विस्तीर्णं वदनं कुरु ॥

tān viṣaṇṇān surān dṛṣṭvā caṇḍikā prāha satvarā | uvāca kālīṃ cāmuṇḍe vistīrṇaṃ vadanaṃ kuru ||

English

Seeing those devas dejected, Caṇḍikā124 quickly spoke. She said to Kālī: "Cāmuṇḍā125, open your mouth wide."126

88.53

Sanskrit

मच्छस्त्रपातसम्भूतान् रक्तबिन्दून् महासुरान् । रक्तबिन्दोः प्रतीच्छ त्वं वक्त्रेणानेन वेगिना ॥

macchastrapātasambhūtān raktabindūn mahāsurān | raktabindoḥ pratīccha tvaṃ vaktreṇānena veginā ||

English

"With this swift mouth of yours, receive the drops of blood born from the fall of my weapons, and the great asuras [born] from each drop of blood."127

88.54

Sanskrit

भक्षयन्ती चर रणे तदुत्पन्नान् महासुरान् । एवमेष क्षयं दैत्यः क्षीणरक्तो गमिष्यति । भक्ष्यमाणास्त्वया चोग्रा न चोत्पत्स्यन्ति चापरे ॥

bhakṣayantī cara raṇe tadutpannān mahāsurān | evameṣa kṣayaṃ daityaḥ kṣīṇarakto gamiṣyati | bhakṣyamāṇāstvayā cogrā na cotpatsyanti cāpare ||

English

Move about in the battle128, devouring the great asuras born from him. In this way this Daitya, his blood drained, will go to destruction; and as those fierce ones are devoured by you, no others will arise.129

Sanskrit

ऋषिरुवाच

ṛṣiruvāca

English

The ṛṣi said:

88.55

Sanskrit

इत्युक्त्वा तां ततो देवी शूलेनाभिजघान तम् । मुखेन काली जगृहे रक्तबीजस्य सोणितम् ॥

ityuktvā tāṃ tato devī śūlenābhijaghāna tam | mukhena kālī jagṛhe raktabījasya soṇitam ||

English

Having spoken thus to her, the Goddess then struck him with her spear130. And Kālī took in the blood of Raktabīja with her mouth.131

88.56

Sanskrit

ततो ऽसावाजघानाथ गदया तत्र चण्डिकाम् । न चास्या वेदनां चक्रे गदापातो ऽल्पिकामपि ॥

tato 'sāvājaghānātha gadayā tatra caṇḍikām | na cāsyā vedanāṃ cakre gadāpāto 'lpikāmapi ||

English

Then that one132 next struck Caṇḍikā there with a mace, and the fall of the mace did not cause her even the slightest pain.133

88.57

Sanskrit

तस्याहतस्य देहात्तु बहु सुस्त्राव शोणितम् । यतस्ततः स्ववक्त्रेण वामुण्डा सम्प्रतीच्छति ॥

tasyāhatasya dehāttu bahu sustrāva śoṇitam | yatastataḥ svavaktreṇa vāmuṇḍā sampratīcchati ||

English

From his body, struck as he was, much blood flowed out; and from wherever it flowed, Cāmuṇḍā134 received it into her own mouth.135

88.58

Sanskrit

मुखे समुद्गता ये ऽस्या रक्तपातान्महासुराः । तांश्चखादाथ चामुण्डा पपौ तस्य च शोणितम् ॥

mukhe samudgatā ye 'syā raktapātānmahāsurāḥ | tāṃścakhādātha cāmuṇḍā papau tasya ca śoṇitam ||

English

The great asuras who sprang up in her mouth136 from the falling of the blood, those Cāmuṇḍā then devoured; and she drank his blood as well.137

88.59

Sanskrit

देवी शूलेन चक्रेण बाणैरसिभिरृष्टिभिः । जघान रक्तबीजं तं चामुण्डापीतशोणितम् ॥

devī śūlena cakreṇa bāṇairasibhirṛṣṭibhiḥ | jaghāna raktabījaṃ taṃ cāmuṇḍāpītaśoṇitam ||

English

88.59–60The Goddess struck down that Raktabīja, whose blood had been drunk by Cāmuṇḍā138, with her spear, disc, arrows, swords and lances. Struck down by the mass of weapons, O protector of the earth139, the great asura Raktabīja, drained of blood, fell on the surface of the earth.

88.60

Sanskrit

स पपात महीपृष्ठे शस्त्रसंहतितो हतः । नीरक्तश्च महीपाल रक्तबीजो महासुरः ॥

sa papāta mahīpṛṣṭhe śastrasaṃhatito hataḥ | nīraktaśca mahīpāla raktabījo mahāsuraḥ ||

English

Its English is given with 88.59–60, above.

88.61

Sanskrit

ततस्ते हर्षमतुलमवापुस्त्रिदशा नृप । तेषां मातृगणो जातो ननर्तासृङ्मदोद्धतः ॥

tataste harṣamatulamavāpustridaśā nṛpa | teṣāṃ mātṛgaṇo jāto nanartāsṛṅmadoddhataḥ ||

English

Then the tridaśas attained incomparable joy, O king; and from among them the host of the Mothers140, which had come into being, danced, wild with the intoxication of blood.141

Sanskrit

इति श्रीमार्कण्डेयपुराणे सावर्णिके मन्वन्तरे देवीमाहात्म्ये रक्तबीजवधोनामाष्टाशीतितमो ऽध्यायः

iti śrīmārkaṇḍeyapurāṇe sāvarṇike manvantare devīmāhātmye raktabījavadhonāmāṣṭāśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ

English

Thus [ends] the eighty-eighth chapter142, named "The Slaying of Raktabīja," in the Devīmāhātmya, in the Sāvarṇika manvantara, in the revered Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa.143

  1. The reading: The line is only a chapter heading (aṣṭāśītitamo 'dhyāyaḥ, 'the eighty-eighth chapter', followed by the numeral 88), not a verse. The sandhi form 'dhyāyaḥ is the nominative singular of adhyāya, 'chapter'. I rendered the heading as it stands and added nothing. The chapter-title in the closing colophons of earlier chapters is not given here. ↩
  2. the lord of the asuras: Asureśvara, a title of Śumbha; it stands in apposition to his name in the next verse. ↩
  3. The reading: udyogam (88.2) could be 'preparation, mobilization' or 'effort, exertion'; I chose 'mobilization' because the following verses list the forces to march out. The particle ha is only a filler here and is left out. ↩
  4. Udāyudhas: Taken as the name of a Daitya clan or group. The word can also mean 'with weapons raised'. ↩
  5. Kambus: A clan of asuras. The word also means 'conch'. ↩
  6. Koṭivīrya: Name of an asura clan. Literally 'having the vigor of ten million'. ↩
  7. Dhaumras: An asura clan. Literally 'smoke-coloured'. ↩
  8. Kālakeyas: Daitya descendants of Kālakā, a race of asuras often named in epic and Purāṇic literature. ↩
  9. The reading: udāyudhāḥ (88.3): either an adjective, 'with weapons raised', or the name of a clan. I chose the name, since it stands beside the clan names Kambu and Dhaumra and the Devīmāhātmya tradition treats it so. Koṭivīryāṇi (88.4): either 'having the vigor of ten million' or the name Koṭivīrya. I chose the name, as it agrees with kulāni and parallels Dhaumra. kambūnām caturaśītiḥ: taken as 'eighty-four of the Kambus'. The word 'clans' is not in the Sanskrit here and is left unsupplied. ↩
  10. whose command was terrible: Bhairavaśāsana, 'one whose rule or command is dreadful', is an epithet of Śumbha. The glossary's 'Bhairava' is here the adjective 'terrible', not a name. ↩
  11. great armies of thousands: The compound mahāsainyasahasraiḥ can be read as 'thousands of great armies' or 'thousands of great hosts'. The sense is a vast following, and no exact number is given. ↩
  12. The reading: Bhairavaśāsanaḥ: 'whose command is terrible' (a bahuvrīhi, chosen) or 'one who rules terribly'. The first reading is the more natural, and the epithet is rendered here rather than kept in IAST. Mahāsainyasahasraiḥ: 'thousands of great armies', chosen, or 'great thousands of armies'; the sense is much the same. ↩
  13. Caṇḍikā: Name of the Goddess, 'the Fierce One'; here the subject of the verse. ↩
  14. The reading: The printed atibhiṣaṇam is evidently a misprint for atibhīṣaṇam ('exceedingly terrible'); I read it so. The word āyāntam (accusative singular masculine/neuter) agrees with tatsainyam, 'that army'. The verse does not name the bow, but jyā-svanaiḥ ('with bowstring-sounds') implies the Goddess's bow; I render it as 'her bowstring', with 'her' supplied from the context of Caṇḍikā as subject. ↩
  15. Ambikā: 'Mother'; a name of the Goddess, here the same as Caṇḍikā of the preceding verses. ↩
  16. The reading: abṛṃhayat (from bṛṃh, causative) means 'made grow, strengthened, amplified'. I render 'swelled'. The pronoun tannādam ('that sound') refers to the lion's roar, which the bell's ringing amplified. ↩
  17. the quarters of the sky: diṅmukha is literally 'the faces (mouths) of the directions', that is, the quarters of the sky. ↩
  18. prevailed: The verb jigye (from ji, 'to conquer') has no object. It may mean that Kālī's roars surpassed the other sounds, or that she gained the upper hand over the enemy host. The translation leaves it absolute. ↩
  19. The reading: Following the reviewer, I take dhanurjyā as one word, 'bowstring' (compare jyāsvana in 88.7), so the three sounds are those of the bowstring, the lion and the bell. My earlier 'the bow, the bowstring' wrongly added a fourth item. I also render the bahuvrīhi nādāpūritadiṅmukhā as a present description, 'with the quarters filled', in place of the pluperfect 'had filled', which the Sanskrit does not state. The participle āpūrita is passive, so the quarters are what was filled. The verb jigye remains intransitive. 'Prevailed' is kept because it allows both senses: her roars surpassed the other sounds, or she triumphed over the enemy. The Sanskrit does not decide between them. ↩
  20. Śaktis: Plural of śakti: here the female powers of the gods, each issuing from a god's body in that god's form. The next verses name them one by one. ↩
  21. Īśa: 'The Lord', a name of Śiva. ↩
  22. Guha: 'The Hidden One', a name of Skanda (Kārttikeya), the war-god. ↩
  23. The reading: 88.10: the absolutive upaśrutya has no nominative of its own. The nominative devī siṃhaḥ kālī (who are 'surrounded') would suggest the Goddess and her companions heard the roar. The instrumental daityasainyaiḥ ... saroṣaiḥ can also be the logical agent in a passive construction, so the armies heard it and surrounded them. I chose the second, which suits the sense: the roar of 88.7-9 provokes the armies to angry attack. ↩
  24. rosary: akṣasūtra, a string of rudrākṣa or similar beads used for counting prayers. ↩
  25. water-pot: kamaṇḍalu, the ascetic's water vessel, a regular attribute of Brahmā. ↩
  26. Brahmāṇī: The feminine counterpart of Brahmā, the first of the divine Mothers (the Mātṛkās) who come forth as the Śaktis of the gods. ↩
  27. The reading: agre in haṃsayuktavimānāgre could be read 'at the front of' (seated in the front of the car) or 'on top of'. I chose 'at the front of'. The text gives āyātā with no explicit 'seated'; the sense of being seated is implied, so I have placed it as a participial description and not bracketed it. Both vimāna and haṃsa are compounded; I have taken haṃsayukta as qualifying vimāna. ↩
  28. Māheśvarī: The Śakti of Maheśvara (Śiva), who is called Īśa in verse 12. ↩
  29. bull: The bull (vṛṣa) is Śiva's mount, Nandin. ↩
  30. a crescent moon: candralekhā, the crescent worn on Śiva's head, whose form his Śakti takes. ↩
  31. The reading: Reading: triśūlavaradhīriṇī is read as triśūlavaradhāriṇī (metrical or scribal ī for ā); 'bearing an excellent trident'. Mahāhivalayā is taken as 'having great serpents as bracelets'. Alternatively vara might be taken as 'boon', but 'excellent' suits the compound. ↩
  32. Kaumārī: 'Belonging to Kumāra'; Kumāra is a name of Skanda, called Guha in this passage. She is his Śakti. ↩
  33. Ambikā in the form of Guha: Ambikā ('Mother') stands in apposition to Kaumārī, as a name or epithet applied to her. Whether it also identifies her with the Goddess is left to the reader; the verse does not say. Guharūpiṇī means 'having Guha's form'. ↩
  34. lance: Sanskrit śakti, the javelin-like weapon of Skanda. It is the same word as Śakti, 'power', but here it names the weapon. ↩
  35. The reading: ambikā guharūpiṇī: after the sandhi daityān + ambikā, the words could be read as 'against the Daityas, Ambikā'. I take both nominatives as apposition to kaumārī, the subject, and not as a separate clause. I have dropped the earlier claim that the Goddess is present in every Śakti, since the verse does not state it. śaktihastā: I chose 'lance in her hand', the weapon sense, over 'power in her hand', and used 'lance' so that it is not confused with śūla, 'spear'. mayūravaravāhanā: 'an excellent peacock as her mount' replaces 'mounted on'. This follows the compound, with vāhana as the mount. ↩
  36. Vaiṣṇavī: 'She of Viṣṇu', the Śakti of Viṣṇu. The text prints vaiṣṇāvī. ↩
  37. Garuḍa: The eagle-like bird who is Viṣṇu's mount (vāhana). ↩
  38. The reading: The text reads vaiṣṇāvī, evidently a misprint for vaiṣṇavī; I have read and written the latter. The compound śaṅkhacakragadāśārṅgakhaḍgahastā is read as 'having in her hands a conch, disc, mace, Śārṅga bow and sword'. The verb is abhyupāyayau, 'came up'. ↩
  39. arose: jajñe is the reading of this e-text. Other texts read yajñavārāham ('the sacrificial boar') and bibhrato ('of him who bore'), which would give 'the incomparable sacrificial-boar form that Hari bore'. ↩
  40. Hari: A name of Viṣṇu. The boar form is his Varāha incarnation, in which he raised the earth from the waters. ↩
  41. The reading: jajñe ('was born, arose') is odd beside yā bibhratī and may be a corruption of yajña- (yajñavārāham) with bibhrato hareḥ. I kept the transmitted reading, taking jajñe with yā ... śaktiḥ as 'the Śakti who arose'. āyau is metrically and grammatically irregular for āyayau; I read it as 'came'. vārāhīṃ agrees with tanum as an adjective ('boar's body'), not as the name Vārāhī. ↩
  42. Nārasiṃhī: The Śakti of Viṣṇu in his man-lion form, Nṛsiṃha (Narasiṃha); the name is the feminine derivative of that form. ↩
  43. Nṛsiṃha: The man-lion incarnation of Viṣṇu, who slew the daitya Hiraṇyakaśipu. ↩
  44. The reading: saṭākṣepakṣiptanakṣatrasaṃhatiḥ: the compound is read as 'she by whom the cluster of stars was flung away by the toss of the mane' (a bahuvrīhi qualifying the Śakti). The text does not state that she herself tosses the mane, but the instrumental sense of ākṣepa implies it. ↩
  45. Aindrī: 'She of Indra', the Śakti of Indra; she is made in his likeness, as the verse's last line says. ↩
  46. She had a thousand eyes: Sahasranayanā, 'thousand-eyed', is a standard epithet of Indra (compare sahasrākṣa), here given to his Śakti. ↩
  47. The reading: gajarājopari: 'king of elephants' (gajarāja) is taken as a title for Indra's mount, Airāvata, which the verse does not name. The glossary's gaja entry gives Cāmara's mount, a different elephant, so it does not apply here. ↩
  48. Īśāna: 'The ruler', an epithet of Śiva; here he is the god who stands with the assembled Śaktis. ↩
  49. out of love for me: mama prītyā: 'for my pleasure' or 'out of affection for me'. It can also be read as 'to please me'; the sense is that the killing of the asuras would be Śiva's wish and delight. ↩
  50. The reading: The reading 'hanyantām asurāḥ' (imperative passive, 'let the asuras be killed') is clear. 'prītyā'ha' is prītyā + āha, 'said out of love/pleasure'; I take mama prītyā as the content of the request, going with hanyantām, not with āha. Grammatically it could also go with āha ('said, in affection for me'), but the sense of the command favours the first reading. ↩
  51. the Devī: The printed devośarīrāt is read as deviśarīrāt, 'from the body of the Devī'. The Devī is Caṇḍikā, from whom her own Śakti issues. ↩
  52. she-jackals: Here śivā is the common noun 'she-jackal', not the goddess name Śivā. The compound śivāśataninādinī means 'sounding the cries of a hundred she-jackals'. ↩
  53. The reading: The word devośarīrāt is not grammatical as printed. Possible readings: (a) deviśarīrāt, 'from the body of the Devī'; (b) devaśarīrāt, 'from the body of the god', meaning Īśāna. I chose (a) because the Śakti is called 'of Caṇḍikā' and the next verse has the goddess (sā) speaking to Īśāna, so the source is the Goddess, not the god. ↩
  54. Īśāna: Here Śiva, addressed as the Goddess's envoy; he is the one who has just spoken to Caṇḍikā. The epithets 'dhūmrajaṭila' describe his smoke-grey matted locks. ↩
  55. enjoyers of the oblations: havirbhuj, 'consumer of the oblation', is a common term for the devas, who receive the offerings poured in sacrifice. ↩
  56. The reading: 'sā' in 88.23 is the Śakti of Caṇḍikā who has just come forth (88.22), also called Aparājitā here; the text then (88.26–27) makes her Śivadūtī. The glossary calls Aparājitā an epithet of the Devī. I translate 'Aparājitā' as the name of the speaker without further identification. 'Dhūmrajaṭila' could also be read as 'matted-haired like smoke' (smoke-coloured matted hair); I take it as 'matted-haired and smoke-grey', a dvandva-like sense. ↩
  57. my jackals: Sanskrit śivāḥ means 'she-jackals' (cf. śivā, 88.22); the Goddess's attendant jackals, of whom the Śakti of Caṇḍikā is the howling counterpart. The text plays on Śivā as a name of the Devī. ↩
  58. The reading: macchivāḥ (mat + śivāḥ) could be read 'my jackals' (śivā = female jackal, plural) or, less naturally, 'my auspicious ones'. I chose 'jackals' because of the preceding verse (śivāśataninādiní) and the verb tṛpyantu with piśitena (flesh). The verse is the Goddess's speech continuing from 88.25, so the opening quotation mark was not repeated and the closing mark ends the speech. ↩
  59. Śiva: Śiva is the same god called Īśāna in verses 88.21 and 88.23. ↩
  60. Śivadūtī: 'She who has Śiva as her messenger'; the name of the Goddess's Śakti who sent Śiva as envoy (88.22–26). The word śivaḥ here means Śiva (Īśāna), not the jackal (śivā). ↩
  61. The reading: The text reads 'loke 'smiṃmastataḥ', which is a misprint for 'loke 'smiṃs tataḥ'. I read it as 'in this world, therefore'. Also, 'sā' (she) is the Śakti of Caṇḍikā from 88.22, while 'tayā devyā' is the Devī who appointed Śiva. The two are in effect the same goddess, since the Śakti came forth from the Devī's body. ↩
  62. Śarva: A name of Śiva, here the Īśāna who was sent as messenger; the compound śarvākhyātam is taken as 'told by Śarva'. ↩
  63. Kātyāyanī: A name of the Goddess, here the same Devī as Caṇḍikā and Aparājitā. ↩
  64. The reading: śarvākhyātam can be read as 'announced by Śarva' (instrumental tatpuruṣa), which I chose, since Śiva was sent as the messenger. The sense 'called Śarva' does not suit the context. ↩
  65. enemies of the immortals: Amarāri, a name for the asuras as foes of the gods; here it refers to Śumbha's and Niśumbha's host, not to Mahiṣa alone. ↩
  66. their indignation roused: The compound uddhatāmarṣāḥ is read as 'with indignation roused'. It may also mean 'with insolent anger'. The name Uddhata, an asura, is not intended here. ↩
  67. The reading: Uddhatāmarṣāḥ: either 'with indignation roused/swollen' or 'with insolent wrath'; I chose 'indignation roused'. Śakti here is a weapon, 'spear', not the divine Power. Prathamam eva agre is a redundant intensive; rendered 'right at the very start'. ↩
  68. darts: śakti here is the missile weapon, a dart or javelin. It is not the Goddess as Śakti, 'power'. The preceding śūla is a spear or pike (sometimes a trident). ↩
  69. twanging: ādhmāta, from ā-√dhmā 'to blow', here describes the bow as sounded or resounding. The compound ādhmāta-dhanur-muktaiḥ means 'released from the twanging bow'. ↩
  70. The reading: ādhmāta: 'blown, sounded, twanged' versus 'drawn full'. The reviewer is right that the root means 'blown', so I now read 'twanging' and drop 'drawn taut'. Śūla and śakti are rendered as two separate weapons, 'spears' and 'darts', which restores the item the earlier draft lost. The compound śūlaśaktiparaśvadhān is in apposition to bāṇān, so the list of hurled things is arrows, spears, darts and axes. The English places the instrument phrase 'with great arrows' after the verb to keep it tied to the cutting. ↩
  71. khaṭvāṅga: A staff topped with a skull, the weapon carried by Kālī and associated with Śiva's ascetic forms. ↩
  72. The reading: The verse reads 'cārīn' for 'ca arīn' (sandhi): 'and the enemies'. The 'ca' links the two participles (vidāritān ... pothitān), so I render it as 'and' between the two states. 'Kurvatī' with the accusative object and participles in the predicative sense gives 'making them split open and crushed', i.e. she was reducing the enemies to that state as she moved about. 'Śūlapāta' could mean the fall of a spear or the thrust of a spear; I have taken 'fall of her spear'. ↩
  73. lose their energy: ojas is vital splendor or energy, distinct from vīrya, 'vigor'. The verse names the flinging of water as the cause only for the loss of vīrya. ↩
  74. by whichever way she ran: yena yena is distributive, 'by whatever way'. sma with the present dhāvati gives a past sense, 'ran'. ↩
  75. The reading: The reviewer's first finding is accepted: the draft left the sentence without a complement. hataujasaḥ now supplies it, 'made the enemies lose their energy'. The reviewer's second finding is rejected: hataujasaḥ is not an irregular nominative. hataojas is an -as stem, and the accusative plural of such stems ends in -aḥ, the same as the nominative. So hataujasaḥ is a regular accusative plural agreeing with śatrūn, and my earlier doubt about its form was mistaken. cākarot takes two predicate complements, hatavīryān and hataujasaḥ, with śatrūn as object. On sma, the reviewer suggests 'whenever'. I keep the past sense, 'ran', since sma with the present marks the past, and the distributive yena yena already carries the repeated sense. The note on ojas is revised as asked. ↩
  76. Kaumārī: The Śakti of Kumāra (Skanda), one of the divine Mothers who accompany the Goddess in battle. ↩
  77. spear: śakti, here the spear or dart that is Kaumārī's weapon, not the abstract 'power' of the glossary. ↩
  78. The reading: śaktyā (instrumental of śakti) can mean 'with her power' or 'with the spear/dart'. I chose 'spear' because the verse lists the weapons of the Mothers (trident, disc), so a weapon is expected. The verb jaghāna is supplied to Kaumārī from the first half, with 'tathā' linking her to the others; 'atikopanā' is read as an adjective of Kaumārī, not as an adverb. ↩
  79. thunderbolt: kuliśa, the thunderbolt, is the weapon of Indra, whose Śakti Aindrī is. ↩
  80. The reading: rudhiraughapravarṣiṇaḥ: 'pouring out streams of blood' (pravarṣin, raining forth, with rudhiraugha as object) is taken as the sense; 'raining torrents of blood' is the same reading in a more literal form. The adjective agrees with daityadānavāḥ. ↩
  81. her disc: The verse has no feminine pronoun; 'her' for the disc is inferred from the boar-form subject. Some readers would join the disc with Vaiṣṇavī of the previous verse. ↩
  82. the boar form: The Śakti of Viṣṇu in his Boar (Varāha) incarnation, known as Vārāhī. The Sanskrit says only vārāhamūrti, 'boar form'; the name Vārāhī is not in the verse. ↩
  83. The reading: vārāhamūrtyā is an instrumental, 'by the boar form', and is read with all three expressions: the blows of the snout, the tusk-tips and the disc. In the Sanskrit the disc (cakreṇa) stands in the same construction, so the verse may attribute it to the boar-formed Śakti, as the chapter's catalogue of Śaktis suggests, or it may refer back to Vaiṣṇavī's disc in 88.33. I kept the Sanskrit's own grammar and did not name Vārāhī. The sentence order in English is slightly recast, so the instrumental 'by the boar form' comes last, close to the Sanskrit. ↩
  84. Nārasiṃhī: The Śakti of Nṛsiṃha, the man-lion form of Viṣṇu; one of the Mothers (mātṛ) who fight beside the Goddess. ↩
  85. the regions between the quarters: digantara means the spaces between the directions, that is, the whole expanse of the sky and the horizon. ↩
  86. The reading: The compound mahāsurān is rendered as 'great asuras', as the glossary has mahāsura. The word anyān ('other') is taken to mean other asuras than those already killed in the preceding verses, and it is placed before mahāsurān in the English. The absolutive-like sense of vidāritān ('split open') is a past participle qualifying the asuras, with nakhaiḥ as its instrument. ↩
  87. fierce peals of laughter: caṇḍāṭṭahāsaiḥ can be read as 'fierce loud laughter' (caṇḍa as adjective) or as 'the loud laughter of Caṇḍā', a name of the Goddess; here the adjective is taken, since no Caṇḍā is named in the context. ↩
  88. asuras: The class of demonic beings, here the soldiers of Śumbha and Niśumbha. ↩
  89. The reading: caṇḍāṭṭahāsaiḥ: either 'with fierce loud laughter' (caṇḍa as adjective) or 'with the loud laughter of Caṇḍā'. I chose the first because the sentence names Śivadūtī as the agent and no separate Caṇḍā is introduced. The glossary entry Caṇḍa is the asura, which does not fit here. abhidūṣitāḥ is taken as 'overcome, afflicted' (from abhi-dūṣ, 'to harm, to spoil'). The text has pṛthivyāṃ patitān and petuḥ pṛthivyām, so the fall is mentioned twice; I render both and keep the sense of the second as 'those who had fallen on the ground'. ↩
  90. the host of the Mothers: mātṛgaṇa: the band of Śaktis (Brāhmaṇī, Māheśvarī, Vaiṣṇavī and the rest) who came forth to aid the Goddess. ↩
  91. the soldiers who were the enemies of the gods: devāri: 'enemy of the gods', i.e. the asuras (Daityas and Dānavas); here the compound with sainika means the asura troops. ↩
  92. by various means: abhyupāyaiḥ vividhaiḥ: instrumental plural, 'by various expedients'. It could also mean they perished by various means, but 'fled' (naś) is the sense chosen here; see doubts. ↩
  93. The reading: neśuḥ (from naś) can mean 'perished' or 'disappeared, fled'. Given that the following verse speaks of Daityas intent on flight (palāyanaparān), I chose 'fled'. Also, abhyupāyaiḥ vividhaiḥ may be taken with the fleeing or with the seeing; I took it with the fleeing. ↩
  94. Raktabīja: "Blood-seed": an asura whose spilled blood gives rise to new asuras like himself, as the following verses tell. ↩
  95. of his own stature: Sanskrit tatpramāṇaḥ, 'of that measure'. Tat refers back to the person whose body the blood leaves (Raktabīja), so each new asura is as large as he is. Verse 88.42 says the new warriors have his form and his prowess (tadrūpāḥ tatparākramāḥ). ↩
  96. The reading: tatpramāṇaḥ: 'of the size of that drop' (referring to raktabindu) or 'of his measure' (referring to the person of asya śarīrataḥ). I first took the drop as the antecedent. I now read 'of his measure', that is, equal to Raktabīja in stature. A drop-sized being would not be called a mahāsura, and 88.42 (tadrūpāḥ tatparākramāḥ) shows that the new beings are copies of him. medinyām is a locative, 'on the earth', and not 'from the earth' as in my first draft. ↩
  97. Indra's Śakti: She is Aindrī, named in the next line; the two phrases refer to the same goddess. ↩
  98. The reading: The compound gadāpāṇiparindraśaktyā is irregular as printed. The syllables 'par'/'pari' after gadāpāṇi are unexplained. It may be a misprint for gadāpāṇiḥ ... aindraśaktyā or gadāpāṇir apīndraśaktyā (with a mace in his hand, against Indra's Śakti). I read gadāpāṇiḥ (mace in hand, of Raktabīja) and indraśaktyā (instrumental, 'with/against Indra's Śakti') and left 'pari' untranslated, since no meaning is clearly required. The instrumental of yudh is read as 'fought with' in the sense of 'against'. ↩
  99. kuliśa: Another name for Indra's thunderbolt (vajra), the weapon with which Aindrī struck Raktabīja in the previous verse. ↩
  100. The reading: Two points. (1) sustrāva is built on the root sru, 'flow', and takes śoṇitam as its subject. (2) tadrūpāḥ and tatparākramāḥ are bahuvrīhi compounds with 'tad' referring to Raktabīja, so the warriors have his form and prowess. In the second compound, 'prowess' is the glossary's rendering of parākrama. ↩
  101. men: puruṣāḥ: the copies of Raktabīja that spring from his blood are called 'men'; they are asuras in human form. ↩
  102. The reading: tadvīryabalavikramāḥ is a bahuvrīhi compound, 'having his vīrya, bala and vikrama'. I take it as qualifying puruṣāḥ, and keep vīrya 'vigor', bala 'strength' and vikrama 'prowess' as distinct, following the glossary. ↩
  103. the Mothers: The text reads mātṛbiḥ, a irregular instrumental plural of mātṛ, for the regular mātṛbhiḥ; it is read here as 'with the Mothers'. ↩
  104. in a battle made most terrible by the very fierce falling of weapons: The compound atyugraśastrapātātibhīṣaṇam is neuter accusative singular and stands as an adverbial or cognate expression, 'in a manner most terrible'; 'battle' is implied from yuyudhuḥ. ↩
  105. The reading: samaṃ: 'together' (with the Mothers) or 'equally, evenly'. I chose 'together with'. mātṛbiḥ is a metrically or textually irregular form of mātṛbhiḥ. The final compound atyugraśastrapātātibhīṣaṇam is neuter singular and seems to function adverbially; I render it as a description of the fighting, with 'battle' implied by yuyudhuḥ. ↩
  106. The reading: The verse is syntactically loose. "vavāha raktam" has the head (śiraḥ) as subject, with "vah" (to carry, bear) used in the sense of 'flowed with, discharged'. I take "asya" as 'his' (Raktabīja's), qualifying "śiraḥ", and "tataḥ" as 'from that [blood]'. Since the text says only "vajrapātena" (by the fall of the thunderbolt), the agent, Aindrī, is not named in this verse and I have not added her. ↩
  107. beat: The text reads dāḍayāmāsa, evidently a misprint for tāḍayāmāsa, 'she struck'. ↩
  108. lord of the asuras: Asureśvara here refers to Raktabīja, the asura of the preceding verses. ↩
  109. The reading: dāḍayāmāsa is read as tāḍayāmāsa ('she beat'), since dāḍ- gives no sense and the 'd' for 't' looks like a scribal or e-text error. The glossary lists asureśvara as an address to Śumbha, but in this verse tam asureśvaram is the object of the verb and refers to Raktabīja, who is being attacked. The particle ha is left untranslated as merely emphatic. ↩
  110. Vaiṣṇavī's: The text reads veṣṇāvī-, a misprint for vaiṣṇavī-; Vaiṣṇavī is the Śakti of Viṣṇu, who struck Raktabīja with her disc in the preceding verse. ↩
  111. of his own measure: Tatpramāṇa: 'having his measure', that is, equal to him in stature or strength. ↩
  112. by the thousand: The text reads sahasraśau, apparently a corrupt form of sahasraśaḥ, 'by thousands'. ↩
  113. The reading: veṣṇāvī- is read as vaiṣṇavī-, and sahasraśau as sahasraśaḥ, as likely misprints. The verse has no finite verb; jagad vyāptam, 'the world [is/was] pervaded', is taken as a participial clause with the instrumentals as agents, and the past sense is chosen because the narrative is in the past. 'Of his own measure' for tatpramāṇaiḥ could also be 'of that number', meaning as many as the drops; I chose 'of his own measure', which matches the preceding verses on his equals in prowess. ↩
  114. Kaumārī: The text as given reads kaumāro, the masculine 'the one of Kumāra'. This is emended to kaumārī, the Śakti of Kumāra, as the company of Mothers in verses 88.44-47 requires; the masculine is most likely a corruption. ↩
  115. spear: śakti here is the spear or lance, the weapon of Kumāra; it does not mean the Goddess as Śakti. ↩
  116. Vārāhī: The Śakti of Viṣṇu in his boar (vārāha) form, one of the group of divine Mothers. ↩
  117. The reading: kaumāro (masculine, 'the Kaumāra') cannot be the subject among the Śaktis, and the sentence needs a feminine like Vārāhī and Māheśvarī. I emend to kaumārī, taking -o as a corruption of -ī, and translate 'Kaumārī ... her spear'. The alternative is to keep the masculine as a name for Kumāra himself, but nothing in the passage supports a male fighter beside the Mothers. I did not choose it. śaktyā is taken as the weapon 'spear', not 'power'. Kaumārī is already in the glossary. ↩
  118. Mothers: The printed text has mātaḥ; it is read here as mātṝḥ, the accusative plural, agreeing with sarvāḥ. The Mothers are the Śaktis named in the preceding verses. ↩
  119. The reading: mātaḥ: it could be the vocative singular of mātṛ, 'O mother', but that does not suit the feminine plural sarvā 'all', and no one is being addressed. It is most likely a misprint or corruption of mātṝḥ, 'the Mothers' (accusative plural). That is the object of ahanat, with sarvā eva agreeing with it, and the Mothers are the opponents in the preceding verses. I chose this reading. ↩
  120. the ground: The text reads 'buvi', evidently a misprint for 'bhuvi' ("on the earth"). ↩
  121. The reading: The word 'buvi' is a misprint for 'bhuvi' (locative of bhū, "earth"); I read it so. 'śaktiśūlādibhiḥ': śakti is the spear (Kaumārī's weapon, cf. 88.48), śūla the trident or spike; I take them as two weapons, 'and other weapons' for ādi. 'bahudhā' is taken with 'ahatasya'. The particle 'vai' is emphatic and omitted. ↩
  122. the asura's blood: The compound asurāsṛksambhūtaiḥ means 'born from the blood of the asura', that is, of Raktabīja; the same asura is meant in the preceding verse. ↩
  123. The reading: asurāsṛksambhūtaiḥ asuraiḥ: the compound could also be taken as 'born from the blood of asuras' generally, but the context (88.50) makes Raktabīja's blood the source. Also, the verse has no vocative or speaker. The reading 'taiś ca' is rendered with 'And ... those'. ↩
  124. Caṇḍikā: A name of the Goddess, 'the Fierce One'; here she is the speaker. ↩
  125. Cāmuṇḍā: Vocative of cāmuṇḍā, addressed to Kālī. The line has 'cāmuṇḍe' in the vocative, which is the name Kālī bears from the slaying of Caṇḍa and Muṇḍa. ↩
  126. The reading: The verse has two verbs of speaking (prāha, uvāca), which I have joined as 'spoke ... said'. Possibly the first is a metrical filler; I have kept both. 'Satvarā' (for satvarā, 'in haste') agrees with Caṇḍikā and is rendered adverbially as 'quickly'. 'Cāmuṇḍe' is read as a vocative in the speech, not as a second object of uvāca. ↩
  127. The reading: The text is awkward. Both raktabindūn and mahāsurān are accusative plural, and raktabindoḥ stands alone as a singular genitive or ablative. I take raktabindūn as 'the drops of blood' and mahāsurān as 'the great asuras', both governed by pratīccha, with sambhūtān applying to each. I read raktabindoḥ as an ablative, 'from the drop (of blood)', and render it distributively as 'from each drop', which fits 88.54, where the asuras born from the blood are devoured. The supplied 'born' in brackets follows from sambhūtān. Raktabindoḥ may instead be a corruption of raktabījasya, 'of Raktabīja', or a repetition by mistake. I kept the transmitted text. ↩
  128. Move about in the battle: The verb is cara, imperative of car, 'move, range about'; with the participle bhakṣayantī, 'devouring', it means 'range about devouring'. The feminine participle addresses Cāmuṇḍā (Kālī). ↩
