  1. The reading: The line is a chapter heading, not a verse of narrative. 'trinavatitama' is the ordinal 'ninety-third'; the figure 93 is repeated in the text, so the English repeats it. The preceding colophon (767.1) calls the previous chapter the ninety-second, which agrees with this numbering. ↩
  2. Devīmāhātmya: 'Greatness of the Goddess': the name of this section of the Purāṇa, here named as the work just recited. ↩
  3. The reading: Devīmāhātmya is the glossary's name for the text; I keep it in IAST between asterisks as a title, with a brief note. The verb dhāryate can mean 'is supported' or 'is held up'; I chose 'sustained'. The glossary's 'ṛṣi' is rendered in IAST as the glossary spelling. ↩
  4. Knowledge too is brought about: vidyā is the subject of kriyate, 'is made'. The sense is that even saving knowledge is produced by the Goddess's power, just as delusion is. ↩
  5. The reading: tathaiva in pāda a can mean 'likewise' (knowledge is made by her just as delusion is) or can link back to the sustaining of the world in 93.1. I took it as 'too'. The verbs mohyante, mohitāḥ and moham eṣyanti give present, past and future delusion. I read mohyante with 'you, this Vaiśya, and other discerning men', mohitāḥ as 'those already deluded', and apare as 'others yet to come'. The subject of mohitāḥ is not stated, so I rendered it as 'they'. ↩
  6. Mahārāja: Vocative 'great king'; here the ṛṣi addresses King Suratha, not Śumbha as in the glossary's other usage. ↩
  7. final liberation: apavarga, release from rebirth, the 'completion' of the soul's course; the verse names the three goals as bhoga (enjoyment), svarga (heaven) and apavarga. ↩
  8. The reading: The glossary says Mahārāja is addressed to Śumbha, but in this verse the ṛṣi (Medhas) is speaking to King Suratha, so the vocative refers to Suratha. 'Śaraṇam' is taken adverbially/in apposition with 'upaihi' ('go to for refuge'); 'saiva' = 'sā eva', 'she alone'. ↩
  9. of praised vows: śaṃsitavrata: the ṛṣi is described as one whose vows are praised, that is, strict or well proven. This epithet describes the ṛṣi who has just spoken; the verses give no name for him in this passage. ↩
  10. O great sage: mahāmune is vocative. Mārkaṇḍeya is speaking here to his own listener, the disciple to whom he narrates the Devīmāhātmya. It is not addressed to the ṛṣi in the story. ↩
  11. The reading: jagama is singular, and nirviṇṇaḥ is singular too, though the Vaiśya also goes (sa ca vaiśyo). A dual jagmatuḥ would be expected. I take Suratha as the grammatical subject, with the Vaiśya added as 'and so did that Vaiśya'. The first word, śaṃsitavrata, may mean 'of praised vows' or 'of strict or sharp vows'; I chose 'praised', following the literal sense of śaṃsita. ↩
  12. Devīsūkta: The 'hymn to the Goddess', usually identified with the Vāk hymn of the Ṛgveda (10.125), in which the goddess Vāk speaks of herself as the supreme power. Here it is used as a japa, a softly or silently repeated recitation. ↩
  13. The reading: I accept the reviewer's main point. nadīpulinasaṃsthitaḥ is nominative singular masculine and agrees with sa vaiśyaḥ, the subject of tepe, so one man is both settled on the bank and practising tapas. I have removed the bracketed 'Suratha' and its note. The 'ca' ('too') ties this Vaiśya to Suratha of 93.5. Paraṃ could be adverbial ('supremely') or an adjective of devīsūktam. I keep 'the supreme Devīsūkta' because the word stands next to the noun. Saṃdarśanārtham ambāyāḥ is rendered 'in order to behold the Mother', following the glossary's rendering of Ambā. I did not add the name 'Ambā' beside it, because the glossary already fixes 'the Mother'. ↩
  14. sandbank: The text reads piline; the standard form is pulina, 'sandbank' (as in pulina in 93.6), so piline is probably a corruption or a variant spelling. ↩
  15. libations: tarpaṇa: satisfying a deity with offerings, usually of water; here it is listed together with flowers, incense and fire. ↩
  16. The reading: piline (93.7) is evidently a variant of pulina, 'sandbank', and I read it so. mūrti is printed for mūrtiṃ, which is metrically and grammatically required as the object of kṛtvā; I read the accusative. tarpaṇaiḥ may be read 'libations' or more generally 'offerings that satisfy'; I chose 'libations'. The compound puṣpadhūpāgnitarpaṇaiḥ could also be 'flowers, incense, and fire-libations', but I took the four items separately. ↩
  17. sprinkled with blood from their own bodies: The compound nijagātrāsṛgukṣitam qualifies bali: the offering was sprinkled with the blood of their own limbs. ↩
  18. the sustainer of the world: jagaddhātrī, 'nurse' or 'bearer' of the world; an epithet of the Goddess, related to the glossary's Dhātrī. ↩
  19. The reading: pratyakṣam is taken adverbially ('face to face', in visible presence) and not as an adjective of Caṇḍikā. The genitive dual samārādhayatoḥ yatātmanoḥ ('of the two worshipping') is taken as a genitive absolute, with the two as the implied audience of prāha; 'to them' is supplied in brackets-free English because the sense requires it. ↩
  20. O delight of your family: kulanandana, 'one who gladdens his family'. The Goddess addresses the Vaiśya with it, since bhūpa in the first half is addressed to Suratha. The two vocatives follow the order of the two instrumentals, 'by you ... and by you'. ↩
  21. The reading: The vocative kulanandana comes after the second tvayā and is taken to address the Vaiśya, while bhūpa addresses Suratha. This is inferred from the parallel structure; the text does not say so outright. The e-text's reference label 772.1 for the speaker's line is kept as given. ↩
  22. The reading: (1) The printed avibhraṃśyanyajanmani is read as avibhraṃśi anyajanmani, with avibhraṃśi an adjective agreeing with rājyam ('not liable to fall away, unfailing'). The alternative, taking it as a locative or an absolutive form, makes no sense. (2) hataśatrubalam is taken as a bahuvrīhi qualifying nijaṃ rājyam: 'in which the enemies' strength is destroyed'. It could also mean 'with enemies and their forces destroyed'. The first reading is kept as the closer one. (3) balāt is rendered 'by force'. 'Through [his own] strength' is possible, but the sense is nearly the same. ↩
  23. knowledge: jñāna here is the insight that dissolves the sense of ownership and selfhood, not ordinary perception, unlike the sense the glossary gives for this text's earlier use. ↩
  24. "mine" and "I.": The words mama and aham are quoted with iti: the notions of possession and of ego, from which attachment (saṅga) arises. ↩
  25. The reading: nirviṇṇamānasaḥ: 'with a mind turned away from the world' (disenchanted, weary of worldly life); alternative 'despondent', rejected because the context is renunciation. The compound saṅgavicyutikārakam is taken as qualifying jñānam, 'that which causes separation from attachment', with mametyahamiti read as the content of that attachment. ↩
  26. unfailing: askhalitam is neuter, 'unstumbling, unbroken'. It is the predicate of an implied subject, here taken as the king's rule (rājyam) in that kingdom; tava is 'for you / yours'. ↩
  27. The reading: askhalitaṃ tava tatra bhaviṣyati has no expressed subject. I took the neuter askhalitam as the predicate of an implied 'rule' or 'kingdom' (rājya), and supplied it in brackets. The finding on tava is accepted: it is now rendered 'for you' (dative or possessive sense), not as the agent of 'hold'. The vocative nṛpate is now 'O king', as the glossary has for nṛpa. bhavān is kept as 'you yourself' because the pronoun is expressed beside the verb and carries some emphasis. The form prāpsyate is middle voice, unusual for prāp but acceptable, and it is translated as an ordinary future. The absolutive hatvā has the same agent as the second clause, 'you'; English 'when you have killed' avoids a dangling participle. ↩
  28. Vivasvat: A name of Sūrya, the Sun-god, father of the eighth Manu. ↩
  29. Sāvarṇika: The Sanskrit reads sāvarṇiko nāma manuḥ, 'the Manu named Sāvarṇika', that is, Sāvarṇi, the eighth Manu. The glossary's Sāvarṇika is also the name of the eighth manvantara. ↩
  30. The reading: The Sanskrit has devād vivasvataḥ, which I take as 'from the god Vivasvat' (ablative, with devāt and Vivasvataḥ in apposition), meaning that Vivasvat is the source of the birth. The vocative-free address is to Suratha, since bhavān is the king. The glossary's Sāvarṇika (the manvantara) is here used as the Manu's own name, so I keep the IAST form Sāvarṇika as it stands in the text. ↩
  31. O best of Vaiśyas: vaiśyavarya, addressed to the merchant Samādhi, the companion of King Suratha. ↩
  32. full accomplishment: saṃsiddhyai is a dative of purpose; it may also be read as 'for [your] final perfection', i.e. liberation. ↩
  33. The reading: The verse is syntactically awkward: 'yaś ca varo ... abhivāñchataḥ' has a genitive participle (abhivāñchataḥ, 'of you who wish') with tvayā, and the relative is continued by taṃ. I read tvayā ... abhivāñchataḥ as 'wished by you', treating the genitive as a loose substitute for the instrumental. Saṃsiddhyai is taken as 'for full accomplishment'; the alternative is 'for the final perfection (liberation)'. The relative 'yaḥ ca' is left implicit in the English by joining the clauses. ↩
  34. The reading: The verse numbering (778.1, then 93.16) is as given in the e-text. The word 'yathābhilaṣitam' is rendered 'as each had desired' because the two petitioners, the king and the Vaiśya, had asked for different boons. Literally it is 'as desired'. The phrase 'bhaktyā tābhyām abhiṣṭutā' is taken as 'praised by them with devotion', with bhaktyā as instrumental of manner. ↩
  35. the bull among Kṣatriyas: Kṣatriyarṣabha: 'bull (best) among Kṣatriyas', a standard epithet of Suratha, a member of the warrior class. ↩
  36. Sāvarṇi: Here the eighth Manu is called Sāvarṇi, the same figure named Sāvarṇika in verse 14; the name derives from his mother's class (savarṇā). ↩
  37. The reading: Sāvarṇi's name is given as Sāvarṇika in 93.14 and Sāvarṇi here; I take them as one Manu. The future bhavitā is rendered 'will ... become'. Verse 16 says the Goddess vanished, and this verse continues as Mārkaṇḍeya's narration, so 'Thus' picks up the boon. ↩
  38. ninety-third chapter: The e-text labels this chapter 88 in its header, but the colophon itself says trinavatitama, 'ninety-third'; the verse numbering (93.13–17) agrees with the colophon. ↩
  39. Devīmāhātmya: The 'Greatness of the Goddess', the section of the Purāṇa (chapters 81–93) that celebrates the Devī; it is told within the account of the Sāvarṇika age. ↩
  40. Sāvarṇika manvantara: Sāvarṇika means 'of Sāvarṇi', the eighth Manu; the section is set within the narrative of his age. ↩
  41. The reading: The chapter header gives 88, but the colophon's trinavatitama means 93, and I followed the Sanskrit of the verse. 'Śrī' in śrīmārkaṇḍeya is taken as an honorific ('revered'), not as the goddess Śrī. The compound could also be read as 'in the Mārkaṇḍeya Mahāpurāṇa, in the Devīmāhātmya, in the Sāvarṇika manvantara' as a nested sequence, which I followed in the English order. ↩
